            •	During peace, Hindus practiced their religion openly and publicly, even in Muslim-ruled areas.
            •	Example:
                •	Jalaluddin Khalji observed that:
                    •	In Delhi and provincial centers, Hindus publicly worshipped idols and preached Hindu texts.
                    •	Hindus celebrated processions with singing, dancing, and drumbeats outside the royal palace.
                    •	He admitted feeling “helpless” to stop it.
        - Limitations and Occasional Religious Persecution
            •	Some Sultans and orthodox theologians pressured rulers to enforce stricter religious policies.
            •	Occasional instances of forced conversions:
                •	Prisoners of war were sometimes converted.
                •	Criminals could escape punishment by accepting Islam.
                •	Firuz Tughlaq executed a Brahmin for allegedly abusing the Prophet of Islam.
            •	Despite such instances, the overall policy remained one of “toleration within limits”, with occasional lapses.
        - Conversion to Islam: Causes and Patterns
            •	Conversions were not primarily due to force.
                •	If Islam had spread by the sword, the Delhi region’s Hindu population would have been the first to convert, which was not the case.
                •	The Hindu faith proved too strong to be destroyed by force.
                •	Shaikh Nizamuddin Auliya noted that some Hindus believed Islam to be true but did not convert.
                •	Barani described Hindus as discarding Islam from their hearts “like a hair from kneaded flour.”
            Reasons for Conversion
                - Political and economic benefits:
                    •	Conversion often brought state patronage and career opportunities.
                - Social mobility:
                    •	Some converted to improve social status.
                    •	Rulers or tribal chiefs converted first, followed by their followers.
                - Agricultural expansion:
                    •	In Punjab, Kashmir, and East Bengal, tribal communities converted when they became cultivators.
                - Occupational influence:
                    •	Many artisans in towns converted, including:
                        •	Weavers
                        •	Ironworkers
                        •	Paper-makers
                        •	These groups depended on Turkish patronage.
                - Role of Sufi Saints:
                    •	Sufis generally did not force conversions.
                    •	They welcomed both Hindus and Muslims to their discourses.
                    •	Their spiritual reputation created a receptive environment for Islam.
                    •	However, there is no evidence that lower-caste Hindus converted in large numbers due to caste discrimination.
        - Influx of Foreign Muslims and Demographic Impact
            •	The Mongol invasion of West Asia led to the migration of many prominent Muslim families to India.
            •	A steady influx of Afghans into India took place:
                •	Many joined Turkish armies.
                •	Others engaged in trade.
            •	Under the Lodi rule (15th century), another large wave of Afghans arrived.
            •	Despite this, Muslims remained a minority in India, influencing Hindu-Muslim relations and cultural attitudes.
        - Conclusion
            •	The Delhi Sultanate followed a policy of limited religious tolerance, allowing Hindus to practice their faith, but with restrictions.
            •	Conversions to Islam were driven by practical and social factors rather than force.
            •	Despite foreign Muslim migrations, Hindus remained the majority, shaping the nature of Hindu-Muslim relations.


- The Age of Vijayanagara and the Bahmanids, and the Coming of the Portuguese (Circa 1350-1565)
    - The Vijayanagara and Bahmani Kingdoms: Stability and Transformation
        - Dominance in Southern India
            •	The Vijayanagara and Bahmani kingdoms ruled south of the Vindhyas for over 200 years.
            •	They contributed significantly to:
                •	Urban development: Built magnificent capitals and cities.
                •	Architecture and culture: Constructed splendid buildings and promoted arts and literature.
                •	Administration and economy: Ensured law and order, encouraged commerce and handicrafts.
            •	During this period, while northern India faced political disintegration, the Deccan and southern India remained stable under their rule.
        - Decline of the Southern Kingdoms
            •	Stability in the south ended with:
                •	The disintegration of the Bahmani empire (late 15th century).
                •	The fall of Vijayanagara after its defeat in the Battle of Bannihatti (1565).
        - Major Transformations in Indian History
            Two significant events reshaped Indian history during this period:
                (a) Arrival of the Portuguese
                    •	The Portuguese arrived in southern India and sought to dominate the Indian seas.
                    •	This marked the beginning of European involvement in India.
                (b) Rise of the Mughals in Northern India
                    •	The advent of the Mughals initiated a new period of political integration in northern India.
        - Impact of These Developments
            •	The arrival of the Portuguese led to a long era of conflict between:
                •	Land-based Asian powers.
                •	European naval powers dominating the seas.

    The Vijayanagara Empire: Its Foundation and Conflict with the Bahmani Kingdom
        Foundation of the Vijayanagara Kingdom
            •	Founded by Harihara and Bukka, members of a family of five brothers.
            •	Initially feudatories of the Kakatiyas of Warangal, later became ministers in Kampili (modern Karnataka).
            •	When Kampili was overrun by Muhammad Tughlaq for sheltering a Muslim rebel, Harihara and Bukka were:
                •	Imprisoned and converted to Islam.
                •	Appointed to deal with rebellions in the region.
            •	The Muslim governor of Madurai had already declared independence, while the rulers of Mysore and Warangal were asserting their autonomy.
            •	Return to Hinduism:
                •	On the advice of their guru Vidyaranya, they abandoned their new faith and founded Vijayanagara.
                •	Some modern scholars dispute their conversion to Islam, considering them Karnataka nayaks rebelling against Turkish rule.
            •	Harihara’s Coronation:
                •	Took place in 1336.
                •	Faced initial conflicts with the Hoysala ruler of Mysore and the Sultan of Madurai.
        Expansion Under Harihara and Bukka
            •	The Hoysala kingdom dissolved, allowing Harihara and Bukka to expand.
            •	By 1346, the entire Hoysala kingdom came under Vijayanagara rule.
            •	The administration was initially a cooperative commonwealth, with brothers and relatives managing newly conquered areas.
            •	Bukka succeeded Harihara in 1356 and ruled until 1377.
        Conflicts with the Sultanate of Madurai and the Bahmani Kingdom
            Struggle Against the Sultanate of Madurai (1336-1377)
                •	The Vijayanagara Empire clashed with the Sultan of Madurai, who:
                    •	Defeated and executed the Hoysala ruler.
                    •	Was eventually overthrown by Vijayanagara in 1377.
                •	By this time, Vijayanagara ruled all of South India up to Rameshwaram, including Tamil Nadu and Kerala.
            Conflict with the Bahmani Kingdom
                •	The Bahmani Kingdom was founded in 1347 by Alauddin Hasan Bahman Shah, also known as Hasan Gangu.
                •	The Bahmani rulers and Vijayanagara clashed over three key regions:
                    1.	Tungabhadra Doab (between the Krishna and Tungabhadra rivers).
                    2.	Krishna-Godavari Delta (fertile region controlling foreign trade).
                    3.	Marathwada and the Konkan (included Goa, an important port for horse imports from Iran and Iraq).
        Military Conflicts Between Vijayanagara and Bahmani
            •	The wars between the two kingdoms resulted in widespread devastation:
                •	Towns and villages were burned and plundered.
                •	Men, women, and children were captured and sold into slavery.
                •	Brutal massacres were common.
            •	1367:
                •	Bukka of Vijayanagara attacked the Mudkal fortress in the Tungabhadra doab, slaughtering its entire garrison except one man.
                •	The Bahmani Sultan, enraged, vowed to slaughter 100,000 Hindus in revenge.
                •	The Bahmani forces crossed the Tungabhadra River and defeated Vijayanagara.
                •	Use of artillery was recorded for the first time in this battle.
                •	The war ended in a treaty, restoring the doab to joint control and agreeing to humane treatment in future wars.
        Expansion Under Harihara II (1377-1404)
            •	After securing South India, Vijayanagara expanded towards the eastern seacoast.
            •	Major rivals in the east:
                •	Reddis in the Krishna-Godavari delta.
                •	Rulers of Warangal.
                •	Bahmani Sultans and Orissa.
            •	Warangal and the Bahmanis formed an alliance, preventing Vijayanagara from gaining control of the Tungabhadra doab.
            •	Harihara II’s Achievements:
                •	Captured Belgaum and Goa from the Bahmanis.
                •	Sent an expedition to Sri Lanka.
        Reign of Deva Raya I (1404-1422)
            •	Renewed war over the Tungabhadra doab with Firuz Shah Bahmani.
            •	Deva Raya I’s Defeat:
                •	Forced to pay 10 lakh gold coins, pearls, and elephants.
                •	Married his daughter to the Bahmani Sultan, giving Bankapur as dowry.
            •	Infrastructure Development:
                •	Built a dam across the Tungabhadra for irrigation.
                •	Constructed another dam on the Haridra River.
        Rise of Deva Raya II (1425-1446)
            •	Considered the greatest ruler of the Vijayanagara dynasty.
            •	Military Reforms:
                •	Realized the Bahmani superiority in cavalry and archery.
                •	Enlisted 2,000 Muslim cavalrymen and ordered his Hindu soldiers to learn archery from them.
                •	Increased the army to 80,000 cavalry, 200,000 infantry, and 60,000 archers.
            •	Campaigns Against the Bahmanis (1443):
                •	Attempted to reclaim Mudkal and Bankapur.
                •	Three major battles were fought, but borders remained unchanged.
            •	Foreign Relations:
                •	Nuniz (Portuguese writer) claims Quilon, Sri Lanka, Pulicat, Pegu, and Tenasserim paid tribute to Vijayanagara.
                •	Sri Lanka was invaded multiple times.
        Vijayanagara as a Prosperous Empire
            Nicolo Conti (Italian Traveler, 1420)
                •	Described Vijayanagara as a massive and powerful city:
                •	90,000 men fit for war.
                •	City walls stretched 60 miles.
            Abdur Razzaq (Persian Traveler, 1440s)
                •	Praised the empire’s wealth:
                •	Had 300 ports comparable to Calicut.
                •	The army numbered 1.1 million.
                •	The city had seven citadels and magnificent markets.
                •	Muslim quarters were provided in the city.
            Ferishta (Historian)
                •	Compared Vijayanagara’s power and wealth to the Bahmani kingdom:
                •	Vijayanagara had greater economic strength but relied on superior military tactics.
            Wealth and Trade
                •	Gold and bullion were hoarded by the Vijayanagara rulers.
                •	This stockpiling of wealth sometimes invited foreign invasions.
        Conclusion
            •	The Vijayanagara Empire emerged as South India’s most dominant power in the 15th century.
            •	Its rulers balanced warfare and economic prosperity, while maintaining diplomatic ties with regional and foreign powers.
            •	The constant struggle with the Bahmani kingdom shaped the political and military landscape of the Deccan region.

    The Bahmani Kingdom—Its Expansion and Disintegration
        Reign of Firuz Shah Bahmani (1397-1422)
            •	Scholarship & Cultural Patronage:
                •	Well-versed in religious sciences (Quranic commentaries, jurisprudence) and natural sciences (botany, geometry, logic).
                •	Skilled calligrapher and poet, composed extempore verses.
                •	Multilingual: Fluent in Persian, Arabic, Turkish, Telugu, Kannada, and Marathi.
                •	Encouraged migration of scholars from Delhi, Iran, and Iraq to the Deccan.
                •	Respected all religions, read the Old and New Testaments.
                •	Ferishta describes him as an orthodox Muslim, though fond of wine and music.
            •	Administrative Reforms:
                •	Inducted Hindus into administration on a large scale.
                •	Deccani Brahmins became dominant, especially in revenue administration.
                •	Balanced the influence of foreign nobles.
            •	Economic & Scientific Advancements:
                •	Encouraged astronomy, built an observatory near Daulatabad.
                •	Developed major ports—Chaul and Dabhol—to boost maritime trade.
                •	Ports attracted ships from the Persian Gulf and the Red Sea, bringing luxury goods.
            •	Military Campaigns:
                •	Expansion towards Berar:
                    •	Defeated Gond Raja Narsing Rai of Kherla.
                    •	Received 40 elephants, 5 maunds of gold, and 50 maunds of silver as tribute.
                    •	Married the Rai’s daughter, later reinstated Narsing Rai as an Amir.
                •	Conflict with Vijayanagara:
                    •	Married Deva Raya I’s daughter.
                    •	Continued struggle for Krishna-Godavari basin.
                    •	1419: Defeated by Deva Raya II, weakening his position.
                    •	Forced to abdicate in favor of his brother, Ahmad Shah I.
        Reign of Ahmad Shah I (1422-1436)
            •	Religious Influence:
                •	Known as a saint (wali) due to association with Sufi saint Gesu Daraz.
            •	Military Conquests:
                •	Warangal Campaign:
                    •	Aimed to punish Warangal ruler for aiding Vijayanagara.
                    •	Invaded Warangal, defeated and killed the ruler.
                    •	Annexed most of its territories.
                •	Capital Shift:
                    •	Moved capital from Gulbarga to Bidar to consolidate newly acquired regions.
                •	Further Expansion:
                    •	Turned attention towards Malwa, Gondwana, and the Konkan.
        Mahmud Gawan and the Expansion of the Bahmani Kingdom
            - Early Life and Rise to Power
                •	Iranian by birth; originally a trader.
                •	Introduced to the Sultan, became a favorite, and received the title Malik-ud-Tujar.
                •	Rose to the position of Prime Minister (Peshwa) and dominated state affairs for nearly 20 years.
            - Military Campaigns and Expansion
                •	Eastern Expansion:
                    •	Led a deep raid into Vijayanagara territories, reaching Kanchi.
                    •	Overran the western coastal regions, including Dabhol and Goa.
                    •	Loss of Goa and Dabhol was a severe blow to Vijayanagara, boosting Bahmani overseas trade with Iran and Iraq.
                •	Northern Frontiers:
                    •	Malwa (ruled by the Khalji dynasty) and Bahmani sultans vied for control over Gondwana, Berar, and the Konkan.
                    •	Bahmani sultans sought help from Gujarat to counter Malwa’s ambitions.
                    •	After a prolonged struggle, it was agreed that Kherla would go to Malwa and Berar to Bahmani.
                    •	Despite this, Malwa continued attempting to seize Berar, leading to further battles.
                •	Interconnected Conflicts:
                    •	Struggles in North and South India were interconnected.
                    •	Western India: Malwa and Gujarat were drawn into Deccan affairs.
                    •	Eastern India: Orissa engaged in conflicts with Bengal and sought control over the Coromandel coast.
                    •	Orissa’s expansion southward weakened Vijayanagara further, especially after Deva Raya I’s death.
            - Administrative and Economic Reforms
                •	Division of the Kingdom:
                    •	The Bahmani kingdom was reorganized into eight provinces (tarafs).
                    •	Each taraf was governed by a Tarafdar.
                •	Salary and Military Administration:
                    •	Salaries and obligations of nobles were fixed.
                    •	Example: A noble maintaining 500 horses received 1,00,000 Awns per year (paid in cash or through jagir).
                    •	Those receiving a jagir were allowed expenses for revenue collection.
                •	Revenue System:
                    •	Khalsa land was set aside in each province for the Sultan’s expenses.
                    •	Land measurement system was introduced to determine the cultivator’s dues to the state.
            - Patronage of Art and Education
                •	Built a grand madrasa (college) in Bidar.
                •	The building was three stories high, decorated with colored tiles, and accommodated 1,000 teachers and students.
                •	Provided free clothing and food to students.
                •	Attracted renowned scholars from Iran and Iraq.
            - Internal Struggles and Execution
                •	Noble Rivalries:
                    •	The Bahmani nobility was divided into:
                        •	Deccanis (long-established local nobles).
                        •	Afranjis/Gharibs (newcomers, including Persians like Mahmud Gawan).
                    •	Mahmud Gawan faced opposition from Deccani nobles despite his conciliatory approach.
                •	Downfall:
                    •	His enemies poisoned the Sultan’s mind against him.
                    •	Wrongfully accused, executed in 1482 at the age of 70.
                •	Consequences:
                    •	Internal noble rivalries worsened.
                    •	Provincial governors became independent, leading to the fragmentation of the Bahmani kingdom into:
                        •	Golconda
                        •	Bijapur
                        •	Ahmadnagar
                        •	Berar
                        •	Bidar
                    •	Among them, Ahmadnagar, Bijapur, and Golconda played significant roles in Deccan politics until their eventual absorption into the Mughal Empire in the 17th century.
            - Cultural Significance of the Bahmani Kingdom
                •	Acted as a cultural bridge between North and South India.
                •	The unique Deccani culture that developed had distinct features, different from North India.
                •	The successor states of the Bahmani kingdom continued these cultural traditions, influencing even the Mughal culture.

    Climax of the Vijayanagara Empire and Its Disintegration
        - Internal Struggles After Deva Raya II (1446)
            •	The death of Deva Raya II led to confusion and civil wars due to the absence of primogeniture.
            •	Feudatories declared independence, and ministers became powerful, imposing heavy taxes.
            •	The authority of the Raya shrank to Karnataka and parts of western Andhra.
            •	The throne was usurped by the minister Saluva, ending the previous dynasty.
            •	Saluva restored internal order and founded the Saluva dynasty, but it soon collapsed.
            •	Eventually, the Tuluva dynasty was established by Krishna Deva Raya.
        - The Reign of Krishna Deva Raya (1509-1530)
            - Political and Military Challenges
                •	Faced threats from:
                    •	Successor states of the Bahmani kingdom
                    •	The state of Orissa, which had seized Vijayanagara territories
                    •	The Portuguese, who were expanding their influence
                •	The Portuguese tried to win Krishna Deva’s support by offering:
                    •	Assistance in recovering Goa from Bijapur
                    •	A monopoly in the supply of horses
            - Military Campaigns and Expansion
                •	War with Orissa (Lasted 7 years) → Vijayanagara regained territories up to the Krishna River.
                •	Conflict over the Tungabhadra Doab → Led to an alliance between Bijapur and Orissa against Vijayanagara.
                •	1520: Defeated Bijapur in the Battle of Raichur
                    •	Overran Raichur and Mudkal.
                    •	The Bijapur ruler fled across the Krishna River.
                    •	Vijayanagara armies reached Belgaum, occupied Bijapur, and sacked Gulbarga.
                •	Vijayanagara emerged as the strongest military power in South India.
                •	However, Krishna Deva paid little attention to naval power, ignoring the growing Portuguese influence over commerce.
            - Administration and Reforms
                •	Attempted centralized administration by ousting the Nayaks from the Tungabhadra Doab and bringing it under direct rule.
                •	Strengthened governance and law enforcement.
            - Cultural and Literary Contributions
                •	A great builder → Constructed a new town near Vijayanagara and an enormous irrigation tank.
                •	Scholar in Telugu and Sanskrit → Wrote:
                    •	A Telugu work on polity
                    •	A Sanskrit drama
                •	Patron of Literature → Supported Telugu, Kannada, and Tamil poets.
                •	Foreign travelers like Barbosa, Paes, and Nuniz praised his administration and the prosperity of Vijayanagara.
            - Religious Tolerance and Social Policies
                •	Vijayanagara rulers were great patrons of Hinduism → Built temples, schools, and maths.
                •	Temples were expanded with pillared halls, pavilions, and new structures.
                •	Despite being a Hindu ruler, Krishna Deva allowed religious freedom.
                    •	Barbosa wrote:
                        •	“The king allows such freedom that every man may come and go and live according to his own creed, without suffering any annoyance, and without enquiry whether he is a Christian, Jew, Moor, or heathen.”
                •	Justice and equity prevailed in his empire.
        - Decline After Krishna Deva Raya (1530-1565)
            •	After Krishna Deva’s death, a succession struggle broke out since his sons were minors.
            •	1543: Sadashiva Raya ascended the throne (ruled until 1567) but was a puppet ruler.
            •	Real power was held by a triumvirate, led by Rama Raja.
            - Wars and Conflicts Under Rama Raja
                •	Diplomatic Manipulation → Played off the Muslim kingdoms (Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar) against each other.
                •	Treaty with the Portuguese → Stopped supplying horses to Bijapur.
                •	Series of Wars:
                    •	Defeated Bijapur and humiliated Golconda and Ahmadnagar.
                •	However, Rama Raja lacked long-term strategy and merely maintained a balance of power.
            - The Battle of Talikota (1565)
                •	The Muslim kingdoms (Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar) united against Vijayanagara.
                •	Battle of Bannihatti (Talikota) → 1565
                    •	Rama Raja was captured and executed.
                    •	100,000 Hindus were slain.
                    •	Vijayanagara was thoroughly looted and destroyed.
        - The Final Decline of Vijayanagara
            •	The Battle of Talikota marked the end of Vijayanagara’s golden age.
            •	The kingdom survived for another 100 years but kept shrinking.
            •	The Raya no longer played a major role in South Indian politics.

    State and Economy under Vijayanagara
        Kingship and Administration
            •	The Vijayanagara rulers upheld an elevated concept of kingship. Krishna Deva Raya, in his book on polity, advised the king:
                •	“With great care and according to your power, you should attend to the work of protecting (the good) and punishing (the wicked) without neglecting anything that you see or hear.”
                •	He also emphasized levying taxes moderately.
            •	The king was assisted by a council of ministers, consisting of great nobles.
            •	The empire was administratively divided into:
                •	Rafyes/Mandalam (Provinces)
                •	Nadu (Districts)
                •	Shale (Sub-districts)
                •	Granza (Villages)
        Provincial Governance and Nayak System
            •	The tradition of village self-government, strong under the Cholas, weakened due to the rise of hereditary Nayakships.
            •	Initially, provincial governors were royal princes, but later, nobles and vassal rulers were also appointed.
            •	The governors:
                •	Held independent courts
                •	Appointed their own officers
                •	Maintained their own armies
                •	Issued small-denomination coins
                •	Had no fixed tenure—ruled as long as they were capable
                •	Could impose new taxes or remit old ones
                •	Paid a fixed contribution of men and money to the central government
            •	Some territories were controlled by subordinate rulers, who had been defeated but reinstated.
            •	In centrally governed regions, Ameram (fixed revenue land grants) were given to military chiefs known as Palegars/Nayaks.
            •	These Nayaks:
                •	Maintained a fixed number of soldiers, horses, and elephants
                •	Paid a sum to the central treasury
                •	Grew powerful, sometimes resisting central control
            •	This decentralization contributed to Vijayanagara’s defeat in the Battle of Bannihatti (1565) and eventual disintegration.
                •	Some Nayaks, like those of Tanjore and Madurai, later declared independence.
        Economic Conditions
            •	Historians disagree on the condition of peasants, as foreign travellers had little knowledge of village life.
            •	General observations:
                •	Peasants: Lived in thatched houses with small doors, generally barefooted, and wore minimal clothing above the waist.
                •	Upper Classes: Wore silk turbans and sometimes shoes but remained uncovered above the waist.
                •	Ornaments: Worn by all classes, including earrings, necklaces, and armlets.
            Taxation and Land Revenue
                •	Land revenue rates varied based on crop type, soil, and irrigation method.
                •	As per an inscription:
                    •	Winter crops (Aruvai rice): 1/3rd of the produce
                    •	Sesame, ragi, horse gram, etc.: 1/4th of the produce
                    •	Millet & dry land crops: 1/6th of the produce
                •	Additional taxes included:
                    •	Property tax
                    •	Sales tax on agricultural produce
                    •	Professional tax
                    •	Military contributions (in times of distress)
                    •	Marriage tax
                •	Nikitin (a 16th-century traveler) observed:
                    •	“The land is overstocked with people, but those in the country are very miserable, while the nobles are extremely affluent and delight in luxury.”
            Urban Growth and Trade
                •	Urbanization expanded significantly under Vijayanagara.
                •	Many towns developed around temples, which played a crucial economic role.
                    •	Temples:
                        •	Needed foodstuffs and commodities for prasadam, priests, and devotees
                        •	Accumulated wealth and actively participated in both internal and overseas trade
                •	Historians view the Vijayanagara period as a transitional phase from the old to a more organized economic structure.

    The Advent of the Portuguese
        Arrival of Vasco da Gama (1498)
            •	Vasco da Gama landed at Calicut in 1498.
            •	He was guided by Gujarati pilots from the African coast.
            •	His arrival marked the beginning of European control over the oceans.
            •	Impact:
                •	Indian trade and traders suffered setbacks.
                •	Eventually, European colonial rule expanded over India and neighboring countries.
            •	However, modern historians (both Western and Indian) question the extent of Portuguese dominance.
        Reasons for Portuguese Arrival
            •	The 15th-century European economy was growing rapidly due to:
                •	Expansion of agriculture (draining marshes, deforestation, better ploughs, scientific crop rotation).
                •	Increased cattle production and meat supply.
                •	Rise of towns and trade (internal and external).
                •	High demand for Oriental goods (silk, spices, drugs) since Roman times.
                •	Spices were especially important for preserving meat during winter.
            •	Ottoman Expansion and Trade Monopoly
                •	Ottoman Turks captured Constantinople (1453), Syria, and Egypt, controlling major trade routes.
                •	Turkish monopoly on pepper hurt European traders.
                •	Expansion of Turkish navy alarmed European nations.
                •	Venice and Genoa, major traders, were too weak to resist Turkish control.
                •	Spain and Portugal, supported by North Europeans and Genoese merchants, took the lead in finding a direct sea route to India.
        Portuguese Maritime Expansion
            •	Prince Henry the Navigator (1418 onwards):
                •	Sent annual expeditions to explore Africa’s western coast.
                •	Objectives:
                    •	Oust Arabs and Venetian rivals from Eastern trade.
                    •	Convert “heathens” in Africa and Asia to Christianity.
            •	The Pope granted Portugal perpetual rights over any newly discovered lands in Africa and India.
            •	Key Discoveries:
                •	Bartholomew Diaz (1488) rounded the Cape of Good Hope, opening the sea route to India.
                •	Technological Advancements:
                    •	Mariner’s compass & astrolabe enabled long voyages.
                    •	Gunpowder, printing, and metallurgy helped European naval supremacy.
        Portuguese Trade Monopoly in India
            •	Vasco da Gama’s return to Portugal:
            •	Goods he brought were valued at 60 times the cost of the expedition.
            •	However, direct trade between India and Europe remained slow.
            •	Portuguese government imposed a monopoly, excluding:
                •	Other European nations
                •	Asian traders
                •	Even private Portuguese merchants
        Portuguese Naval Supremacy (1509)
            •	The Sultan of Egypt, alarmed by Portuguese expansion, sent a fleet.
                •	This fleet, supported by Gujarat’s ruler, was initially successful.
                •	Portuguese, however, defeated them in 1509, making their navy supreme in the Indian Ocean.
                •	This victory allowed Portuguese expansion into the Persian Gulf and the Red Sea.
        Governorship of Albuquerque
            •	Alfonso de Albuquerque (1510), new Portuguese governor, followed a fort-based expansion policy:
                •	Established strategic forts in Asia and Africa.
                •	Believed forts were essential for trade and political dominance.
            •	Key Conquests:
                •	Goa (1510): Captured from Bijapur.
                    •	Became the Portuguese commercial and political center in the East.
                    •	Allowed the Portuguese to dominate Gujarat’s seaports.
                •	Colombo (Sri Lanka), Achin (Sumatra), Malacca (Malay Peninsula), and Socotra (Red Sea):
                    •	Strengthened Portuguese control over trade routes.
                •	Failed attempts:
                    •	Could not capture Aden but forced Ormuz’s ruler (Persian Gulf) to allow a Portuguese fort.
        Challenges to Portuguese Rule
            •	Opposition from Turks and Indian Powers:
                •	Ottoman Empire’s rise led to conflicts over Indian Ocean control.
                •	The Turks advanced into Eastern Europe (1529), threatening Vienna.
                •	Gujarat’s Sultan sought Ottoman support against the Portuguese.
            •	Portuguese Concessions to Gujarat (1531-1536):
                •	Built a fort at Chaul after failing to take Daman and Diu.
                •	Formed a defensive alliance with Bahadur Shah (Gujarat’s ruler) against the Mughals.
                •	Bahadur Shah granted Bassein (Vasai) Island to the Portuguese.
            •	Portuguese Betrayal and Bahadur Shah’s Death (1536):
                •	Bahadur Shah later regretted his concessions.
                •	While negotiating with the Portuguese, a scuffle broke out.
                •	Bahadur Shah drowned while trying to escape.
        Turkish Naval Campaigns (1536-1554)
            •	Ottoman Empire challenged the Portuguese in Indian waters:
                •	1536: Ottoman fleet (45 galleons, 20,000 men) besieged Diu, but failed.
                •	1551: Peri Rais (Turkish admiral) attacked Portuguese forts in Muscat and Ormuz.
                •	1554: Final Ottoman expedition under Ali Rais failed.
            •	Portuguese-Ottoman Agreement (1566):
                •	Both empires agreed to divide Indian trade, avoiding further conflict.
                •	Ottomans shifted focus to Europe, abandoning Mughal alliance against the Portuguese.
        Conclusion
            •	Despite initial dominance, Portuguese power declined due to:
                •	Rising Indian and European rivals.
                •	Limited resources to maintain vast trade networks.
                •	Growing strength of the Mughals, Marathas, and Dutch.
            •	The Portuguese legacy continued in Goa, Daman, and Diu, which they controlled until 1961.

    Portuguese Impact on Indian Trade, Society, and Politics
        - Limited Control Over Indian Ocean Trade
            •	The Portuguese lacked the resources to police the vast Indian Ocean or fully control trade.
            •	Their strategy focused on monopolizing certain goods and taxing others:
                •	Royal monopoly: Pepper, arms and ammunition, and warhorses.
                •	Permit system: Ships trading other commodities had to obtain permits from Portuguese officials.
                •	Customs duty: All ships traveling to the East or Africa were required to pass through Goa and pay duties.
            •	To enforce these rules:
                •	The Portuguese searched ships suspected of contraband trade.
                •	Ships refusing inspection were seized or sunk, with passengers often enslaved.
                •	This created continuous friction with traders accustomed to open trade practices.
            •	However, their aggressive tactics were counterproductive:
                •	Arab and Indian traders armed their ships, but primarily against pirates, not the Portuguese.
                •	The Portuguese navy struggled to dominate long-established Asian trade networks.
        - Resistance and Adaptation of Indian and Arab Traders
            •	Gujarat and Arab traders continued to dominate major trade routes:
                •	Indian textiles, rice, and sugar were exchanged for Southeast Asian spices, West Asian gold and horses, and Chinese silk and porcelain.
                •	Despite early Portuguese successes, they could not sustain a monopoly on the pepper and spice trade.
            •	By the late 16th century, alternative trade routes emerged:
                •	Mughal and Safavid empires promoted and safeguarded overland trade.
                •	Gujarati merchants established a new supply route via Achin (Sumatra) → Lakshadweep → Egypt → Red Sea, beyond the reach of the Portuguese navy.
        - Limited Success in Establishing Goa as a Trade Hub
            •	The Portuguese attempted to make Goa the dominant Asian trade center, surpassing Cambay and Surat (Gujarat).
            •	However, they failed to achieve this goal.
            •	Their influence was mostly negative, as they:
                •	Weakened the Malabar trade by aggressive interference.
                •	Disrupted sea trade from Bengal by raiding ships from Chittagong.
        - Expansion of New Trade Routes
            •	The Portuguese expanded India’s trade with Japan (exporting Indian textiles and importing copper and silver).
            •	They also opened Indian trade with the Philippines:
                •	Indian textiles were exported to South America via the Philippines.
                •	Silver was imported from South America in exchange.
            •	Their naval power both disrupted traditional trade and created new opportunities.
        - Limited Contribution to Science and Technology
            •	Unlike other European nations, the Portuguese did not transfer Renaissance science and technology to India.
            •	Reasons:
                •	Portugal was less influenced by the Renaissance than Italy or Northern Europe.
                •	Later, the Jesuit-led Catholic reaction opposed scientific advancements.
            •	However, they introduced new crops from Central America, including:
                •	Potatoes, tobacco, maize, and peanuts, which gradually became widespread.
        - Military Conflicts and Survival in the Deccan
            •	The defeat of Vijayanagara (1565) at the Battle of Banihatti changed power dynamics in South India.
            •	Previously, Vijayanagara relied on the Portuguese for warhorses, preventing direct conflict.
            •	In 1570, an anti-Portuguese alliance formed:
                •	Ali Adil Shah (Bijapur)
                •	Nizam Shah (Ahmadnagar)
                •	Zamorin (Calicut)
            •	Their goal: Expel the Portuguese from the Deccan coast.
            •	Attack strategy:
                •	Adil Shah led an assault on Goa.
                •	Nizam Shah besieged Chaul.
            •	However, Portuguese naval and military strength proved decisive.
            •	Result: The Portuguese retained their dominance over the Indian seas and Deccan coast.


- Struggle for Empire in North India—I (Circa 1400-1525)
    The Fragmentation of the Delhi Sultanate and the Struggle for Power in North India
        - The Collapse of the Delhi Sultanate
            •	The weakening of the Delhi Sultanate and Timur’s invasion of Delhi (1398) led to a major political crisis.
            •	After Timur’s attack, the Tughlaq king fled, creating a power vacuum.
            •	This emboldened several provincial governors and autonomous rulers to declare their independence.
        - Emergence of Independent States
            •	First breakaway regions:
                •	Bengal (East)
                •	Sindh and Multan (West)
                •	Deccan states (South)
            •	Further disintegration:
                •	Gujarat, Malwa, and Jaunpur (Eastern Uttar Pradesh) also declared independence.
                •	In Rajputana, after the Muslim governor was expelled from Ajmer, various Rajput states asserted independence.
        - Balance of Power Among Regional States
            •	Over time, a power balance emerged among these independent states:
                •	West: Gujarat, Malwa, and Mewar checked each other’s expansion.
                •	East: Bengal faced resistance from the Gajapati rulers of Orissa and Jaunpur.
                •	North: The Lodi dynasty, which rose to power in Delhi (mid-15th century), engaged in a prolonged struggle with Jaunpur for control of the Ganga-Yamuna valley.
            •	Key turning point:
                •	The Lodis absorbed Jaunpur (late 15th century), shifting the balance of power.
                •	This allowed them to expand into eastern Rajasthan and Malwa.
        - The Struggle for the Domination of North India
            •	As Malwa began to disintegrate, tensions increased between:
                •	Gujarat
                •	Mewar
                •	The Lodis (Delhi)
            •	The victor in this contest would dominate all of North India.
            •	Malwa became the focal point of this power struggle.
            •	This intense rivalry motivated Rana Sanga to invite Babur, believing that the Lodi defeat would leave Mewar as the dominant power in North India.
            
    Eastern India: Bengal, Assam, and Orissa
        - Bengal’s Independence from Delhi
            •	Reasons for frequent independence from Delhi:
                •	Distance from Delhi and unfamiliar waterways hindered Turkish rulers.
                •	Muhammad Tughlaq’s preoccupation with rebellions led Bengal to break away in 1338.
            •	Ilyas Khan’s Rise to Power (1342):
                •	Captured Lakhnauti and Sonargaon, declaring himself Sultan Shamsuddin Ilyas Khan.
                •	Expanded westward to Tirhut, Champaran, Gorakhpur, and Banaras.
            •	Firuz Tughlaq’s Bengal Campaign:
                •	Marched through Champaran and Gorakhpur, capturing Pandua.
                •	Ilyas sought shelter in Ekdala Fort.
                •	After two months, Firuz attempted a trick retreat, but Ilyas re-entered Ekdala.
                •	A treaty established the river Kosi (Bihar) as the boundary.
                •	Ilyas maintained independence while exchanging gifts with Delhi.
        - Ilyas Shah’s Legacy and Successors
            •	Ilyas Shah’s popularity helped resist Firuz’s attempts to sway the people.
            •	Firuz Tughlaq invaded again after Ilyas’ death, but his son Sikandar used the same tactics and defended Bengal.
            •	Bengal remained independent for 200 years until Sher Shah’s conquest in 1538.
        - Notable Rulers and Developments
            •	Ghiyasuddin Azam Shah (1389–1409):
                •	Known for justice; humbly accepted a court ruling when he accidentally killed a widow’s son.
                •	Maintained close relations with Persian poet Hafiz of Shiraz.
                •	Revived diplomatic and trade ties with China.
                •	Buddhist monks were sent to China upon the emperor’s request, proving Buddhism’s survival in Bengal.
                •	Chittagong port flourished as a trade hub.
            •	Hindu Rule Under Raja Ganesh:
                •	Brief period, but his sons ruled as Muslims.
            •	Cultural Achievements:
                •	Capitals Pandua and Gaur adorned with unique Bengali-Islamic architecture.
                •	Patronage of Bengali literature:
                    •	Maladhar Basu, author of Sri-Krishna-Vijaya, was titled Gunaraja Khan.
                    •	His son received the title Satyaraja Khan.
                    •	Alauddin Hussain Shah (1493–1519) oversaw significant literary growth.
            •	Alauddin Hussain Shah’s Policies:
                •	Restored law and order.
                •	Liberal administration, offering high offices to Hindus (wazir, chief physician, mint master).
                •	Vaishnavite saint Chaitanya was highly respected.
        - Bengal’s Expansion and Conflict with Assam
            •	Attempts to control Brahmaputra valley:
                •	Muslim rulers since Muhammad bin Bakhtiyar Khalji faced disastrous defeats.
                •	Ilyas Shah invaded Kamta (Western Assam) but withdrew after reaching Gauliati.
                •	Successors attempted plundering raids, but the river Karatoya remained Bengal’s boundary.
            •	Ahoms and Kamta’s Conflict:
                •	Kamta (West) and Ahoms (East) were rivals.
                •	Ahoms, a Mongoloid tribe from Burma, became Hinduized, giving rise to the name Assam.
                •	Alauddin Hussain Shah allied with Ahoms and destroyed Kamta’s capital, Kamtapur.
                •	His son Nusrat Shah’s attack on the Ahoms failed.
                •	Suhungmung (Svarga Narayana), the greatest Ahom ruler, expanded his kingdom and resisted Muslim invasions.
                •	Vaishnavite reformer Shankaradeva flourished under Suhungmung’s rule, spreading Vaishnavism.
        - Bengal’s Expansion Towards Arakan and Chittagong
            •	Sultan Hussain Shah conquered Chittagong from Arakan.
            •	Also annexed Tipperah (modern Tripura).
        - Bengal’s Conflict with Orissa
            •	Early skirmishes:
                •	The Ganga dynasty of Orissa assisted Radha (South Bengal) in resisting Delhi’s rule.
                •	Tried to conquer Lakhnauti, but failed.
            •	Orissa’s Flourishing Under the Ganga Dynasty:
                •	Built the Jagannatha temple (Puri) and Sun temple (Konark).
                •	Ilyas Shah raided Orissa (1338), reaching Chilka Lake and looting elephants.
                •	Firuz Tughlaq (1360) raided Orissa, massacring people and desecrating Jagannatha temple.
                •	These raids weakened the Ganga dynasty, leading to the rise of the Gajapati dynasty.
            •	The Gajapati Dynasty’s Rise (15th Century):
                •	Expanded southward into Karnataka, clashing with Vijayanagara, Reddis, and Bahmani sultans.
                •	Bengal’s strength prevented Orissa from expanding northward.
            •	Bengal-Orissa Border Conflicts:
                •	Frontier at river Saraswati (Midnapore and Hugli districts).
                •	Orissa attempted to advance up to Bhagirathi but failed.
                •	Alauddin Hussain Shah led raids into Orissa, reaching Puri and Cuttack.
                •	Despite frequent battles, Orissa held its territory beyond Saraswati.
        - Conclusion: A Region of Dynamic Power Struggles
            •	Bengal, Assam, and Orissa were highly contested regions during this period.
            •	Bengal’s political stability, economic prosperity, and cultural vibrancy made it a dominant power.
            •	Assam’s Ahom rulers resisted external invasions, strengthening their rule.
            •	Orissa’s powerful Gajapati rulers expanded into the south but faced resistance from Bengal and Vijayanagara.
            •	The constant rivalries shaped the political landscape of Eastern India.

    Western India
        Gujarat: A Wealthy Province
            •	Gujarat was one of the wealthiest provinces of the Delhi Sultanate due to:
                •	Flourishing seaports
                •	Excellence in handicrafts
                •	Fertile soil
            •	Under Firuz Tughlaq:
                •	Gujarat had a benevolent governor who encouraged Hindu worship (Ferishta’s account).
            •	Zafar Khan, son of a Rajput convert, succeeded as governor.
            •	After Timur’s invasion of Delhi:
                •	Gujarat and Malwa became independent in all but name.
                •	In 1407, Zafar Khan formally declared himself ruler as Muzaffar Shah.
            Ahmad Shah I (1411–1443): Founder of the Gujarat Kingdom
                •	Grandson of Muzaffar Shah.
                •	Brought the nobility under control and strengthened administration.
                •	Moved the capital from Patan to Ahmedabad (1413).
                •	Expanded and consolidated the kingdom.
                Ahmad Shah’s Architectural Contributions
                    •	Patronized a distinctive architectural style influenced by Jain traditions:
                        •	Slender turrets
                        •	Exquisite stone carvings
                        •	Highly ornate brackets
                    •	Major constructions:
                        •	Jama Masjid, Ahmedabad
                        •	Tin Darwaza
                Ahmad Shah’s Military Campaigns and Policies
                    •	Conquest of Saurashtra:
                        •	Captured Girnar Fort, later restored it to the raja in exchange for tribute.
                    •	Religious Policies:
                        •	Imposed jizyah tax on Hindus (first time in Gujarat).
                        •	Attacked Sidhpur, destroying Hindu temples.
                    •	Administrative Inclusivity:
                        •	Despite religious policies, he appointed Hindus to key positions.
                        •	Ministers Manik Chand and Motichand (Bania community) served under him.
                    •	Strict Justice:
                        •	Executed his own son-in-law in the marketplace for murder.
                    •	Expansion in Rajasthan:
                        •	Subjugated Idar Fort, Jhalawar, Bundi, and Dungarpur.
                Gujarat-Malwa Rivalry
                    •	Constant conflict between Gujarat and Malwa:
                        •	Muzaffar Shah defeated and imprisoned Hushang Shah of Malwa, but later reinstated him.
                        •	Malwa’s rulers tried to weaken Gujarat by supporting rebels and Hindu rajas.
                        •	Gujarat retaliated by attempting to install its own candidate as Malwa’s ruler.
                    •	Impact of the rivalry:
                        •	Weakened both kingdoms.
                        •	Prevented them from playing a larger role in North Indian politics.
            Mahmud Begarha (1459–1511)
                Expansion and Military Campaigns
                    •	Most famous Sultan of Gujarat, known for expansion and consolidation.
                    •	Called Begarha for capturing two powerful forts:
                        •	Girnar (Saurashtra, now Junagadh)
                        •	Champaner (Southern Gujarat)
                Conquest of Girnar (Saurashtra)
                    •	The ruler of Girnar had been regularly paying tribute, but Mahmud decided to annex the kingdom to control Saurashtra, a rich region with:
                        •	Fertile land
                        •	Flourishing ports
                        •	Robbers and pirates disrupting trade
                    •	Siege of Girnar:
                        •	The raja resisted despite having few guns.
                        •	Allegedly fell due to treason—his kamdar (minister), whose wife had been taken by the raja, conspired against him.
                        •	The raja converted to Islam and joined Mahmud’s service.
                    •	Mahmud founded Mustafabad at the base of Girnar as Gujarat’s second capital.
                Attack on Dwarka
                    •	Mahmud sacked Dwarka, claiming it harbored pirates attacking Mecca-bound pilgrims.
                    •	The campaign also resulted in the destruction of Hindu temples.
                Conquest of Champaner (1454)
                    •	Strategic importance:
                        •	Key location for controlling Khandesh and Malwa.
                        •	The ruler had ties with the Sultan of Malwa despite being a feudatory of Gujarat.
                    •	Siege of Champaner:
                        •	The raja and his warriors, left without aid, performed jausar (ritual sacrifice) and fought to death.
                    •	Mahmud built Muhammadabad near Champaner, making it his principal residence with gardens and architectural marvels.
                Architecture and Legacy
                    •	Jama Masjid in Champaner still stands, featuring:
                        •	Covered courtyard
                        •	Jain architectural influences
                        •	Finely crafted stonework, comparable to goldsmith artistry
                Conflict with the Portuguese
                    •	The Portuguese disrupted Gujarat’s trade with West Asia.
                    •	Mahmud allied with Egypt’s ruler to counter them but failed.
                Economic and Cultural Contributions
                    •	Long and peaceful reign fostered trade and commerce.
                    •	Built caravanserais and inns to ensure safe travel for merchants.
                    •	Encouraged translation of Arabic works into Persian.
                    •	Court poet Udayaraja composed in Sanskrit.
                Personal Traits and Unique Characteristics
                    •	Appearance:
                        •	Long waist-length beard and mustache tied over his head.
                    •	Strange legend:
                        •	According to traveler Barbosa, he was fed poison since childhood, making his touch deadly to flies.
                    •	Enormous appetite:
                        •	Breakfast: Cup of honey, cup of butter, 100–150 bananas.
                        •	Daily intake: 10–15 kg of food.
                        •	Kept meat samosas by his pillow in case of midnight hunger.
                Gujarat at its Peak
                    •	Under Mahmud Begarha, Gujarat reached its largest territorial extent.
                    •	Became one of the most powerful and well-administered states in India.
                    •	Later posed a serious challenge to Mughal Emperor Humayun.
        Malwa: A Strategic Kingdom
            Geographical and Political Importance
                •	Located on the high plateau between the Narmada and Tapti rivers.
                •	Controlled key trade routes between:
                    •	Gujarat and northern India.
                    •	North and south India.
                •	Served as a buffer state against the ambitions of Gujarat, Mewar, the Bahmanis, and the Lodi Sultans of Delhi.
                •	Any powerful state that controlled Malwa had a strategic advantage in dominating northern India.
            Rise of Malwa (15th Century)
                •	Reached its peak during the 15th century.
                •	Capital shifted from Dhar to Mandu, a highly defensible and scenic location.
                •	Mandu’s Architecture:
                    •	Unlike Gujarat’s architectural style, Mandu’s structures were massive and built on lofty plinths.
                    •	Extensive use of colored and glazed tiles added variety.
                    •	Famous monuments:
                        •	Jama Masjid
                        •	Hindola Mahal
                        •	Jahaz Mahal (Ship Palace)
                •	Ruins still remain impressive today.
            Internal Struggles and External Threats
                •	Malwa was plagued by internal conflicts:
                    •	Frequent succession struggles.
                    •	Factional fights among noble groups for power and wealth.
                •	Neighboring states Gujarat and Mewar often exploited Malwa’s instability for their own gains.
            Hushang Shah (Early Malwa Ruler, Tolerant Policies)
                •	Practiced religious toleration and encouraged Rajput settlement.
                •	Granted jagirs to two elder brothers of Rana Mokal of Mewar.
                •	Hindu temple construction was unrestricted (e.g., Lalitpur temple inscription).
                •	Patronized Jains, the dominant merchants and bankers of the region.
                    •	Nardeva Soni, a Jain merchant, became treasurer and advisor to the Sultan.
            Mahmud Khalji (1436–69): Malwa’s Most Powerful Ruler
                •	Less tolerant than Hushang Shah.
                    •	Destroyed many temples during wars with Rana Kumbha of Mewar and neighboring Hindu rajas.
                    •	However, not part of a general policy of temple destruction.
                •	A restless and ambitious ruler, constantly at war.
                    •	Fought against almost all neighbors, including:
                        •	Gujarat
                        •	Rajas of Gondwana and Orissa
                        •	Bahmani Sultans
                        •	Sultan of Delhi
                •	Focused on overrunning South Rajputana and subduing Mewar.
        Mewar: Rise and Dominance in Northern India
            Early History and Background
                •	Mewar’s rise in the 15th century significantly influenced northern India’s political landscape.
                •	The Chauhans, after being ousted from Ajmer, shifted to Ranthambhor and established a powerful state.
                    •	Their power ended when Alauddin Khalji conquered Ranthambhor.
                    •	From its ruins, new states emerged:
                        •	Marwar (capital: Jodhpur, founded in 1465).
                        •	Nagaur, a Muslim principality.
                •	Ajmer, once ruled by Muslim governors, changed hands frequently and became a point of contention among Rajput states.
                •	Eastern Rajputana remained disputed, with Delhi’s rulers maintaining interest in the region.
            Formation of Mewar
                •	The early history of Mewar is obscure.
                •	Bapa Rawal of the Guhilot clan is considered its virtual founder.
                    •	Migrated from Gujarat in the 7th century and dominated southwestern Rajasthan.
                •	Guhilots ruled from Chittor until Ratan Singh was ousted by Alauddin Khalji.
                •	Chittor then passed to the Sisodiyas, former feudatories of the Guhilots.
                •	Rao Lakha and Mokal strengthened Mewar, making it the most powerful state in Rajasthan by the late 14th and early 15th centuries.
            Rana Kumbha (1433–1468): The Greatest Mewar Ruler
                •	Consolidated his rule by defeating internal rivals.
                •	Expanded Mewar by conquering Bundi, Kotah, and Daungarpur (on the Gujarat border).
                    •	Kotah had been aligned with Malwa, and Daungarpur with Gujarat.
                •	This led to conflicts with Gujarat and Malwa:
                    •	Khan of Nagaur, attacked by Rana Kumbha, sought Gujarat’s help.
                    •	Kumbha supported a rival of Mahmud Khalji of Malwa in an attempt to install him as ruler.
                    •	In retaliation, Mahmud Khalji gave refuge to Kumbha’s rivals, including his brother Mokal.
            Mewar’s Struggles and Resistance
                •	Wars with Gujarat and Malwa lasted throughout Kumbha’s reign.
                •	Conflict with Rathors of Marwar:
                    •	Marwar had been under Mewar but gained independence under Rao Jodha.
                •	Despite external threats, Rana Kumbha maintained Mewar’s position:
                    •	Kumbhalgarh was besieged multiple times by Gujarat.
                    •	Mahmud Khalji raided as far as Ajmer and placed his own governor there.
                    •	Despite these attacks, Kumbha retained most of his conquests (except outlying areas like Ranthambhor).
            Rana Kumbha’s Contributions
                •	Scholar and Patron of Learning:
                    •	Composed several books, some of which still exist.
                •	Architectural Legacy:
                    •	Built the Victory Tower (Kirtistambha) at Chittor.
                    •	His palace ruins reflect his enthusiasm for architecture.
                    •	Developed irrigation projects by constructing lakes and reservoirs.
                    •	Mewar’s stone-cutting and sculpture remained at a high level during his reign.
            Rana Sanga and Mewar’s Expansion
                •	Kumbha was murdered by his son Uda in a bid for the throne.
                •	Rana Sanga (Kumbha’s grandson) took the throne in 1508 after a long fratricidal conflict.
                •	Key events before Sanga’s rise:
                    •	Malwa weakened internally.
                    •	Mahmud II of Malwa sought Gujarat’s help against Medini Rai, a powerful Rajput leader.
                    •	Medini Rai turned to Rana Sanga for support.
                    •	In 1517, Rana Sanga defeated Mahmud II and took him prisoner.
                    •	Eastern Malwa, including Chanderi, came under Mewar’s control.
            Mewar vs. Delhi Sultanate and the Mughals
                •	The Lodi rulers of Delhi saw Mewar’s expansion as a threat.
                •	Ibrahim Lodi invaded Mewar but was defeated by Rana Sanga at Khatoli.
                •	Lodi withdrew to focus on internal matters.
                •	Meanwhile, Babur was approaching India (1525), setting the stage for a decisive conflict for supremacy.

    Northwest and North India
        The Political Landscape After Timur’s Invasion
            Collapse of the Tughlaq Dynasty
                •	Following Timur’s invasion, Sultan Mahmud Tughlaq fled Delhi, seeking refuge first in Gujarat and then in Malwa.
                •	By the time he attempted to return, the throne of Delhi had lost its prestige.
                •	In the Delhi region, ambitious nobles and zamindars began asserting their independence.
        The Sharqi Sultanate (Jaunpur Sultanate)
            Rise of the Sharqis
                •	Malik Sarwar, a noble under Firuz Tughlaq, was one of the first to assert independence in the Ganga valley.
                •	Initially appointed as the governor of the eastern region with the title Malik-us-Sharq (Lord of the East), his successors were called the Sharqis.
                •	The Sharqi sultans established their capital at Jaunpur (eastern Uttar Pradesh) and adorned it with magnificent palaces, mosques, and mausoleums.
                •	The Sharqi rulers developed a unique architectural style distinct from the Delhi Sultanate, marked by lofty gates and huge arches.
            Cultural Patronage
                •	Jaunpur became a center of learning and culture, earning the title “Shiraz of the East.”
                •	Scholars, poets, and saints thrived under the patronage of the Sharqi rulers.
                •	Malik Muhammad Jaisi, the famous poet and author of Padmavat, lived in Jaunpur.
            Decline and Fall
                •	At its peak, the Sharqi Sultanate extended from Aligarh (western Uttar Pradesh) to Darbhanga (north Bihar), and from Nepal’s boundary to Bundelkhand.
                •	The Sharqis aspired to conquer Delhi but faced resistance from the Lodi dynasty.
                •	After a series of conflicts, Bahlul Lodi of Delhi annexed Jaunpur in 1484.
                •	The last Sharqi ruler died in exile, marking the end of the dynasty.
                •	Despite their fall, the Sharqis maintained law and order and prevented Bengal’s expansion into eastern Uttar Pradesh.
                •	Their cultural contributions continued to influence the region long after their demise.
        Rise of the Lodi Dynasty
            Bahlul Lodi (1451–1489)
                •	Following Timur’s invasion, the Saiyid dynasty emerged in Delhi, but its rule was weak.
                •	In Punjab, Afghan warlords gained power, with Bahlul Lodi emerging as the most significant.
                •	Initially, he was granted the iqta (land grant) of Sirhind and checked the growing power of the warlike Khokhars in the Salt Range.
                •	Called in to assist Delhi’s ruler against an attack from Malwa, Bahlul Lodi took control of Delhi.
                •	In 1451, he formally ascended the throne, ending the Saiyid dynasty.
            Expansion and Afghan Influence
                •	The Lodis, unlike earlier Delhi rulers who were Turks, were Afghans.
                •	Although Afghans had long served in the Delhi Sultanate’s army, few held high-ranking positions.
                •	Their rising influence was evident in both Malwa and the Bahmani kingdom in the south.
                •	Bahlul Lodi focused on consolidating his rule by inviting Afghan warriors from Roh (Afghanistan), promising them wealth and power.
                •	This influx of Afghans strengthened his army, allowing him to defeat the Sharqis and change the demographic composition of Muslim society in India.
            Sikandar Lodi (1489–1517)
                Governance and Administration
                    •	Sikandar Lodi, the most capable Lodi ruler, ruled alongside Mahmud Begarha (Gujarat) and Rana Sanga (Mewar).
                    •	Unlike the tribal Afghan sardars, who viewed the sultan as primus inter pares (first among equals), Sikandar emphasized royal authority.
                    •	He introduced strict court protocols and enforced regular financial audits for nobles.
                    •	Corrupt officials were punished severely.
                    •	Though he managed to centralize power, the Afghan tradition of dividing the kingdom among the ruler’s sons persisted.
                Economic and Agricultural Reforms
                    •	Sikandar Lodi ensured efficient governance, emphasizing law and order.
                    •	Roads and highways were made secure from bandits.
                    •	Prices of essential goods remained low.
                    •	To encourage agriculture, he abolished the octroi duty on grains.
                    •	He introduced the gaz-i-Sikandari (a new yard measurement) for standardized land assessment, later adopted by Sher Shah Suri.
                Religious Policies
                    •	Sikandar Lodi was an orthodox ruler who strictly followed Islamic law (Sharia).
                    •	He prohibited certain practices among Muslims, such as women visiting saintly graves.
                    •	He re-imposed the jizya tax on Hindus and executed a Brahman for equating Hindu and Muslim scriptures.
                    •	Hindu temples, such as the one at Nagarkot, were demolished during his campaigns.
                Patronage of Arts and Learning
                    •	Despite his orthodoxy, Sikandar Lodi was a patron of scholars, poets, and philosophers.
                    •	He invited intellectuals from Arabia and Iran to his court.
                    •	Many Sanskrit works were translated into Persian, including texts on music.
                    •	His reign saw an increase in Hindu participation in administration due to their Persian language skills.
                Expansion and Strategic Developments
                    •	Sikandar Lodi expanded the Lodi territory by conquering Dholpur and Gwalior.
                    •	In 1506, he carefully selected the site for Agra, intending it to serve as a strategic center for eastern Rajasthan and routes to Malwa and Gujarat.
                    •	Over time, Agra became the second capital of the Lodi dynasty.
            The Looming Conflict: Lodi vs. Mewar
                •	Sikandar Lodi sought to extend influence over Malwa and Rajasthan.
                •	He took the Khan of Nagaur under his protection and tried to shift Ranthambhor’s allegiance from Malwa to Delhi.
                •	His successor, Ibrahim Lodi, continued this expansionist policy but faced resistance from Mewar.
                •	The growing power of Rana Sanga in Malwa and his expansion toward Agra and Bayana signaled an inevitable confrontation.
                •	However, before this struggle could reach its climax, Babur arrived in India, altering the course of history.
        Kashmir in the Fifteenth Century
            Isolation and Early Turmoil
                For a long time, the beautiful valley of Kashmir remained a forbidden land to outsiders. According to al-Biruni, even Hindus who were not personally known to nobles were denied entry. 
                During this period, Kashmir was a center of Shaivism. However, the situation changed with the fall of Hindu rule in the mid-fourteenth century.
                A major turning point was the devastating Mongol invasion of 1320, led by Dalucha. He ordered:
                    •	Massacre of men, while women and children were enslaved and sold to Central Asian merchants.
                    •	Plundering and burning of towns and villages.
                The Kashmir government failed to resist this onslaught, leading to widespread discontent among the people.
            Rise of Islam and Persecution of Hindus
                Following the Mongol invasion, Kashmir underwent significant changes:
                    •	Muslim saints and refugees from Central Asia entered Kashmir, facilitated by the Baramulla route.
                    •	A unique group of Sufi saints, called Rishis, emerged, blending elements of Hinduism and Islam.
                    •	Conversion of lower-class Hindus to Islam took place, partly through preaching and partly by force.
                During the reign of Sikandar Shah (1389–1413), Hindu persecution peaked:
                    •	Brahmins and learned Hindus were forced to either convert or flee.
                    •	Temples were destroyed, and gold and silver idols were melted for currency.
                    •	The harsh policies were influenced by his minister Suha Bhatt, a former Hindu who had converted to Islam.
            Reign of Zainul Abidin (1420–1470) – The Great Sultan
                The accession of Zainul Abidin, regarded as Kashmir’s greatest Muslim ruler, marked a reversal of these policies.
                Reconciliation with Hindus:
                    •	Cancelled orders against Hindus and invited exiles to return.
                    •	Allowed those who had converted under pressure to revert to Hinduism.
                    •	Restored temples, land grants, and revived Hindu scholarship.
                    •	More than a century later, Abul Fazl recorded that Kashmir had 150 majestic temples, likely restored under Zainul Abidin.
                Policies of Toleration:
                    •	Abolished the jizya (tax on non-Muslims) and banned cow slaughter.
                    •	Lifted the ban on sati out of respect for Hindu customs.
                    •	Appointed Hindus to high offices, including Suha Bhatt, his former minister.
                    •	His first two queens were Hindu princesses from Jammu, and they bore all his sons.
                Contributions to Literature and Learning:
                    •	A scholar himself, fluent in Persian, Kashmiri, Sanskrit, and Tibetan.
                    •	Encouraged translations of Sanskrit texts (e.g., Mahabharata, Rajatarangini).
                    •	Patronized Persian and Sanskrit scholars.
                    •	Music enthusiast—received rare Sanskrit works on music from the Raja of Gwalior.
                Economic and Cultural Development:
                    •	Sent artisans to Samarkand to learn papermaking and bookbinding.
                    •	Encouraged crafts like stone-cutting, bottle-making, gold-beating, and shawl-weaving.
                    •	Advanced musket-making and fireworks manufacturing.
                    •	Developed agriculture through the construction of dams, canals, and bridges.
                    •	Greatest engineering feat: Zaina Lanka, an artificial island in Wular Lake, where he built his palace and a mosque.
                Military and Political Achievements
                    Though not a great warrior, Zainul Abidin successfully:
                            •	Defeated Mongol invasions in Ladakh.
                            •	Conquered Baltistan (Tibbat-i-Buzurg).
                            •	Maintained control over Jammu and Rajouri.
                            •	Unified the Kashmiri kingdom.
                    His fame spread across India and Asia, and he maintained diplomatic ties with major rulers of his time.
        Conclusion: Cultural Impact of Regional Kingdoms
            The fifteenth-century political landscape remained unstable, but the regional kingdoms made lasting cultural contributions:
                •	Unique architectural styles emerged, incorporating local traditions.
                •	Local languages and literature flourished.
                •	While some rulers engaged in religious persecution, the broader trend was one of mutual accommodation and cultural integration.
            Zainul Abidin’s rule anticipated Akbar’s policies in many ways, fostering a spirit of tolerance and cultural exchange that shaped Kashmir’s history.

- Cultural Development in India (1300–1500)
    Cultural Impact of the Delhi Sultanate
        The establishment of the Delhi Sultanate in the early thirteenth century marked a new phase in India’s cultural development. 
        The Turkish invaders who arrived in India were not mere barbarians.
        Turks and Their Cultural Background
            •	The Turks originated from Central Asia and migrated to West Asia in the ninth and tenth centuries.
            •	Unlike earlier Central Asian invaders who embraced Buddhism or Hinduism, the Turks adopted Islam and quickly assimilated into Arabo-Persian culture.
            •	By the time they reached India, they had been influenced by a flourishing Islamic civilization that spanned from Morocco and Spain to Iran.
            •	The region had made significant contributions in areas such as science, navigation, literature, and governance.
            •	The Turks brought with them:
                •	A well-defined faith in Islam to which they were deeply committed.
                •	Advanced ideas in government, art, architecture, and administration.
        Interaction Between Turks and Indians
            •	Indian society had its own strong religious traditions and developed cultural expressions in art, architecture, and literature.
            •	The interaction between these two civilizations led, over time, to the emergence of a new, enriched culture.
            •	However, this process was long and complex, often marked by:
                •	Destruction, followed or accompanied by periods of reconstruction.
                •	Mutual misunderstandings and conflicts, as both cultures held deeply entrenched beliefs.
        Assimilation and Cultural Exchange
            Despite the challenges, the two cultures gradually influenced each other in various fields:
                •	Art and architecture
                •	Music and literature
                •	Customs, ceremonies, and rituals
                •	Religious beliefs
                •	Science and technology
            However, elements of confrontation and conflict remained strong within both communities.
        A Process of Ups and Downs
            •	The process of assimilation and convergence was not uniform.
            •	It varied across different regions, fields, and historical periods.
            •	While cultural integration did take place, it was accompanied by periods of resistance and discord.
        Ultimately, the Delhi Sultanate played a crucial role in shaping India’s cultural landscape, fostering both conflict and synthesis between Turkish and Indian traditions.

    Architecture During the Delhi Sultanate
        The arrival of the Turks in India led to significant developments in architecture, blending indigenous and Islamic styles. 
        Their architectural contributions were shaped by their religious beliefs, technological advancements, and artistic preferences.
        Early Turkish Constructions
            •	The first requirement of the new rulers was houses to live in and places of worship.
            •	Initially, existing temples and buildings were converted into mosques, while many others were destroyed, and their materials reused.
            •	Examples:
                •	Quwwat-ul-Islam Mosque (near Qutub Minar, Delhi) – Originally a temple.
                •	Arhai Din ka Jhonpra (Ajmer) – Originally a monastery.
            •	The only new construction in Delhi was a façade of three elaborately carved arches in front of the garbhagriha, which was demolished.
            •	Decorative Features:
                •	Human and animal figures were avoided, as they were considered un-Islamic.
                •	Instead, floral scrolls and Quranic verses were used in intricate designs.
        Introduction of New Architectural Elements
            The Turks soon began constructing their own buildings, employing both indigenous Indian craftsmen and architects from West Asia.
                •	Key Features Introduced:
                    •	Arches and domes were widely used.
                    •	The arch and dome were not Turkish inventions; they were borrowed from Rome (via the Byzantine Empire) and refined by the Arabs.
                •	Advantages of Arches and Domes:
                    •	Domes created an impressive skyline and were gradually built higher.
                    •	Large halls could be constructed without the need for numerous supporting pillars, making them useful for mosques and palaces.
                    •	Fine quality mortar was used for strong construction.
                •	Comparison with Indigenous Architecture:
                    •	Indians had known arches and domes earlier but had not used them extensively.
                    •	Indian methods relied on placing stones over one another or using beams, whereas the Turks introduced the scientific method of constructing arches.
                    •	Both methods—arch and dome as well as slab and beam—were used in their buildings.
        Decorative Styles and Artistic Influences
            •	The Turks avoided human and animal figures in their architecture.
            •	Instead, they used:
                •	Geometrical and floral designs (Arabesque).
                •	Calligraphy – Quranic verses carved into walls.
            •	Indigenous motifs such as the bell, leaf, swastika, and lotus were also incorporated.
            •	Indian stone-cutters’ expertise was fully utilized, as seen in:
                •	Tomb of Iltutmish (Delhi) – Highly intricate carvings, with hardly any blank space.
            •	The Turks introduced colored stone architecture, using:
                •	Red sandstone as the primary material.
                •	Yellow sandstone and marble for decoration and contrast.
        Significant Architectural Achievements
            Qutub Minar (13th Century)
                •	The most magnificent Turkish construction of the 13th century.
                •	Height: Originally 71.4 meters.
                •	Builders: Begun by Aibak, completed by Iltutmish.
                •	Common Misconception: It was not dedicated to Sufi saint Qutb-ud-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki; it was part of the Quwwat-ul-Islam Mosque.
                •	Unique Features:
                    •	Skillfully projected balconies linked to the main tower.
                    •	Use of red and white sandstone, and marble.
                    •	Ribbed design, enhancing visual appeal.
        Khalji Period (Late 13th – Early 14th Century)
            •	Alauddin Khalji’s Architectural Contributions:
                •	Built his capital at Siri (remains largely destroyed).
                •	Planned a tower twice the height of Qutub Minar but did not complete it.
                •	Constructed the Alai Darwaza, an entrance to the Qutub complex, featuring:
                    •	Well-proportioned arches.
                    •	First dome built using correct scientific principles.
                •	By this time, Indian craftsmen had mastered the art of arches and domes.
        Tughlaq Period (14th Century)
            •	Marked both the climax of the Delhi Sultanate and the beginning of its decline.
            •	Key Constructions:
                •	Tughlaqabad Fort (Ghiyasuddin and Muhammad Tughlaq):
                    •	A huge palace-fortress complex.
