                    •	A massive artificial lake was created by blocking the Yamuna.
                •	Ghiyasuddin Tughlaq’s Tomb:
                    •	Built on a high platform, enhancing its skyline.
                    •	Marble dome, adding elegance.
            •	Architectural Innovations:
                •	Sloping Walls (Batter Technique):
                    •	Created an impression of strength and solidity.
                    •	Common in early Tughlaq buildings but absent in Firuz Tughlaq’s constructions.
                •	Combination of Arch and Lintel-Beam Systems:
                    •	Visible in Firuz Tughlaq’s buildings.
                •	Hauz Khas Complex:
                    •	A pleasure resort with a large lake and madrasa.
                •	Kotla (Firuz Shah’s Fort):
                    •	Continued using indigenous Rajasthani and Gujarati architectural elements.
                •	Material Choices:
                    •	Avoided expensive red sandstone.
                    •	Used cheaper grey stone, leading to minimal decoration.
                    •	Lotus motif became a standard decorative feature.
        Lodi Period (15th – Early 16th Century)
            •	Further developed the architectural traditions of the Delhi Sultanate.
            •	Key Features:
                •	Both arch-based and lintel-beam structures were used.
                •	Balconies, kiosks, and eaves inspired by Rajasthani and Gujarati styles.
                •	Buildings were placed on high platforms to enhance grandeur.
                •	Many tombs were built in lush gardens (e.g., Lodi Garden, Delhi).
                •	Octagonal tomb structures became common.
            •	Legacy:
                •	Many Lodi-era features were later adopted by the Mughals, culminating in masterpieces like the Taj Mahal.
        Regional Variations and Later Developments
            •	As the Delhi Sultanate fragmented, distinct architectural styles emerged in different regions:
                •	Bengal
                •	Gujarat
                •	Malwa
                •	Deccan
            •	These styles retained local influences while incorporating Turkish innovations.
            •	The Tughlaq-era style evolved and was modified in regional kingdoms, leading to an architectural renaissance in India.
        Conclusion
            The architectural legacy of the Delhi Sultanate was a blend of Indian and Islamic influences, introducing arches, domes, calligraphy, and intricate ornamentation while utilizing local craftsmanship. 
            Over time, this synthesis led to the emergence of an independent Indo-Islamic architectural style, which significantly influenced later Mughal and regional constructions.

    Religious Traditions and Beliefs
        Islamic Influence and Cultural Exchange in Early India
            •	Islam’s Early Presence in India:
                •	Established in Sindh (8th century) and Punjab (10th century).
                •	Arab travelers settled in Kerala (8th–10th century).
            •	Cultural and Religious Exchanges:
                •	Arab travelers and Sufi saints spread ideas across India.
                •	Al-Biruni’s Kitab-ul-Hind introduced Hindu ideas to West Asia.
                •	Buddhist texts, Indian fables, astronomy, and medicine books were translated into Arabic.
                •	Indian yogis frequently visited West Asia.
            •	Influence of Indian Philosophy on Islam:
                •	Scholars debate the impact of Buddhism and Vedanta on Islamic thought.
                •	Buddhist monasteries, stupas, and images of the Buddha found in Afghanistan and Central Asia indicate strong Buddhist influence in these regions.
                •	Both Greek and Indian philosophies contributed to Islamic philosophical development.
            •	Rise of Sufism in India (12th century):
                •	Influenced both Muslims and Hindus, creating a shared spiritual platform.
                •	Early Sufis adopted Hindu rituals and yogic practices.
                •	Despite assimilation, their core ideology remained fundamentally Islamic.
        The Sufi Movement
            - Background and Development
                    •	10th century: A turning point in Islamic history due to:
                    •	The rise of the Turks after the decline of the Abbasid Caliphate.
                    •	Changes in religious ideas and beliefs:
                        •	Decline of Mutazila (rationalist philosophy).
                        •	Rise of orthodox schools based on the Quran and Hadith.
                        •	Growth of Sufi mystic orders.
                    •	Rationalists were accused of skepticism and atheism due to their monistic belief that God and the world were one, which was seen as heretical.
                •	Formation of Islamic Law Schools:
                    •	Orthodox traditions led to four major schools of Islamic law.
                    •	Hanafi school (most liberal) was adopted by the eastern Turks, who later brought it to India.
            - Early Sufism and Key Figures
                •	Origins and Motivations:
                    •	Early Sufis were mystics who emphasized deep devotion to God.
                    •	Rejected the extravagance and moral decay of the Islamic empire.
                    •	Maintained distance from state affairs.
                •	Notable Early Sufis:
                    •	Rabia (8th century): Emphasized love as the bond between God and the soul.
                    •	Mansur bin Hallaj (10th century): Advocated pantheism but was executed for heresy.
                    •	Al-Ghazzali (d. 1112):
                        •	Reconciled mysticism with Islamic orthodoxy.
                        •	Rejected rationalist philosophy, arguing that true knowledge of God comes only through revelation (Quran).
            - Organization of Sufism
                •	Formation of Sufi Orders (Silsilahs):
                    •	12 Sufi orders emerged.
                    •	Each order was led by a prominent mystic (pir).
                    •	Pirs lived in khangahs (hospices) with their disciples (murids).
                    •	Every pir appointed a successor (wali) to continue their work.
            - Hindu and Buddhist Influences on Sufism
                •	Shared Practices and Rituals:
                    •	Penance, fasting, breath control—similar to Buddhist and Hindu yogic traditions.
                    •	Buddhism was prevalent in Central Asia before Islam’s arrival, influencing Islamic mysticism.
                    •	Hindu yogis visited West Asia, and texts like Amrit-kund were translated into Persian.
                    •	Debate exists on whether Buddhist and Vedantic philosophies significantly shaped Sufism.
                •	Common Spiritual Ideas:
                    •	Similar beliefs on God’s nature, the soul, and the material world created a shared platform between Sufis and Hindu mystics.
                    •	This mutual understanding fostered religious tolerance.
            - Classification of Sufi Orders
                •	Two broad divisions:
                    •	Ba-shara Sufis: Followed Islamic law (shara).
                    •	Be-shara Sufis: Did not strictly follow Islamic law.
                    •	Both orders existed in India, with Be-shara Sufis (wandering saints) gaining a broader following, often revered by both Muslims and Hindus.
            - The Essence of Sufism
                •	Sufi teachings emphasized spiritual unity.
                •	Persian poet Sanai expressed this spirit in his poetry:
                    “Faith and infidelity, both are galloping on the way towards Him; And are exclaiming (together): He is one and none share His kingdom.”
        The Chishti and Suharwardi Silsilahs
            •	Chishti Order:
                •	Established in India by Khwaja Muinuddin Chishti around 1192, shortly after Prithvi Raj Chauhan’s defeat.
                •	Settled in Ajmer, an important political center with a significant Muslim population.
                •	Left no written records, but his influence grew through his successors.
            •	Prominent Chishti Saints:
                •	Shaikh Muinuddin Chishti’s disciples:
                    •	Bakhtiyar Kaki and his disciple Farid-ud-Din Ganj-i-Shakar (confined activities to Hansi and Ajodhan).
                    •	Farid-ud-Din was deeply respected in Delhi, and some of his verses were later included in the Adi-Granth of the Sikhs.
                •	Nizamuddin Auliya and Nasiruddin Chiragh-i-Delhi were the most famous Chishti saints.
                    •	Mingled freely with the lower classes, including Hindus.
                    •	Lived a simple, austere life and spoke Hindawi to connect with the masses.
                    •	Did not actively seek conversions, but many later attributed their conversion to their blessings.
                    •	Popularized sama (musical recitations) to foster devotion, often using Hindi verses.
                    •	Nizamuddin Auliya adopted yogic breathing techniques, earning the title of sidh (‘perfect’) from yogis.
            •	Chishti Expansion:
                •	After Nasiruddin Chiragh-i-Delhi’s death in the mid-14th century, the order lost a central figure in Delhi.
                •	Chishti saints dispersed, spreading their influence to eastern and southern India.
            •	Suharwardi Order:
                •	Entered India around the same time as the Chishtis but remained largely confined to Punjab and Multan.
                •	Prominent figures: Shaikh Shihabuddin Suharwardi and Hamid-ud-Din Nagori.
                •	Unlike the Chishtis, they:
                    •	Did not embrace poverty and accepted state service.
                    •	Held important positions in the ecclesiastical department.
            •	Key Differences:
                •	Chishtis preferred to stay aloof from state politics and avoided rulers and nobles.
                •	Suharwardis actively participated in state affairs.
                •	Despite differences, both orders fostered a spirit of peace and harmony among different religious and sectarian groups.
            •	Impact:
                •	Mecca remained the holiest site, but the rise of popular saints provided a local spiritual focus for the masses.
        The Bhakti Movement
            Origins and Early Development
                •	Stressed mystical union of the individual with God.
                •	Though its roots lie in the Vedas, Bhakti was not emphasized in the early period.
                •	Gained prominence with the rise of Buddhism, particularly Mahayana Buddhism, where Buddha was worshipped in his Avalokiteshvara (gracious) form.
                •	Around the same time, the worship of Vishnu developed.
                •	By the Gupta period, Bhakti was accepted as one of the three recognized paths to salvation (along with Jnana and Karma).
            Bhakti in South India (7th–12th Century)
                •	Saiva Nayanmars and Vaishnavite Alvars disregarded Jain and Buddhist austerities and preached personal devotion to God as a means of salvation.
                •	Opposed the rigid caste system and spread their message in local languages.
            Bhakti’s Spread to North India
                •	Despite connections between North and South India, Bhakti ideas spread slowly to the North.
                •	Challenge:
                    •	Nayanmars and Alvars used regional languages, while Sanskrit remained the dominant intellectual language in the North.
                •	Bhakti was carried to the North by scholars and saints, including:
                    •	Namadeva (early 14th century) – A Maharashtrian saint, originally a tailor and former bandit, wrote devotional poetry in Marathi and engaged with Sufi saints in Delhi.
                    •	Ramananda (14th–15th century) – A follower of Ramanuja, substituted Rama worship for Vishnu worship and rejected caste-based restrictions.
                    •	Enrolled disciples from all castes, including:
                        •	Ravidas (cobbler)
                        •	Kabir (weaver)
                        •	Sena (barber)
                        •	Sadhana (butcher)
                •	Why Bhakti Gained Popularity in the North:
                    •	Decline of Brahman prestige after Rajput defeats and the Turkish Sultanate’s establishment.
                    •	Nath Panthi movement questioned caste hierarchy and Brahman supremacy.
                    •	Sufi saints promoted equality and brotherhood, resonating with Bhakti ideas.
                    •	People sought a spiritual, emotional, and rational religion beyond rituals and ceremonies.
                    •	By the 15th–16th centuries, the Bhakti movement became widely accepted in North India.
            Key Figures of the Bhakti Movement
                Kabir (15th Century)
                    •	Uncertainty about his birth; legend says he was the son of a Brahman widow, raised by a Muslim weaver in Kashi.
                    •	His teachings combined Hindu and Islamic elements:
                        •	Emphasized one universal God (Allah, Sain, Sahib, etc.).
                        •	Rejected idol worship, pilgrimages, and ritualistic practices (including namaz).
                        •	Opposed asceticism and book knowledge as means of true wisdom.
                        •	Denounced the caste system and untouchability, advocating human equality.
                        •	Supported the poor and marginalized, though he was not a social reformer—focused on individual transformation.
                    •	Dr. Tara Chand’s View:
                        •	“Kabir’s mission was to preach a religion of love uniting all castes and creeds.”
                        •	Rejected ritualistic Hinduism and Islam in favor of spiritual unity.
                Guru Nanak (1469–1538)
                    •	Founder of Sikhism; born into a Khatri family in Talwandi (now Nankana Sahib, Pakistan).
                    •	Initially trained in Persian and accountancy, but was drawn towards spiritual contemplation.
                    •	Had a mystical vision, renounced worldly life, and composed hymns.
                        •	Sang them with rabab accompaniment by Mardana.
                    •	Undertook extensive travels across India, Sri Lanka, and even Mecca and Medina.
                    •	Core Teachings:
                        •	Emphasized one God and devotion as the path to salvation, beyond caste, creed, or sect.
                        •	Advocated purity of character and the need for a guru for spiritual guidance.
                        •	Rejected idol worship, pilgrimages, and external rituals.
                        •	Promoted a middle path, balancing spirituality with household responsibilities.
                    •	Did not intend to create a new religion but sought to bridge Hindu-Muslim divides.
                    •	His teachings later evolved into Sikhism, while Kabir’s followers formed the Kabir Panth sect.
            Impact and Resistance
                •	Bhakti saints challenged orthodox Hinduism and Islam, advocating spiritual unity and equality.
                •	Their influence extended to political and religious policies, notably influencing Akbar’s religious outlook.
                •	However, orthodox factions within both Hinduism and Islam resisted change.
                •	The 16th–18th centuries saw ongoing struggles between:
                    •	Liberal, non-sectarian forces (Bhakti, Sufi influences).
                    •	Traditional, orthodox elements reinforcing existing religious structures.
                •	Long-term Impact:
                    •	Though caste and orthodox practices persisted, Bhakti created a lasting shift in religious thought.
                    •	Fostered an inclusive spiritual environment influencing future reform movements.
        The Vaishnavite Movement
            •	Bhakti Movement and Vaishnavism
                •	Developed around the worship of Rama and Krishna, incarnations of Vishnu.
                •	Krishna’s childhood escapades and love for Radha became central themes.
                •	Used allegorically to depict the relationship between the individual soul and the supreme soul.
            •	Chaitanya and Kirtan Tradition
                •	Chaitanya popularized kirtans (musical gatherings) for mystic experience.
                •	Worship emphasized love, devotion, song, and dance, leading to ecstasy.
                •	Open to all, irrespective of caste or creed.
            •	Saint-Poets of Vaishnavism
                •	Their writings transcended caste and religious barriers.
                •	Prominent figures:
                    •	Narsinha Mehta (Gujarat)
                    •	Meera (Rajasthan)
                    •	Surdas (Western Uttar Pradesh)
                    •	Chaitanya (Bengal and Orissa)
                •	Inclusivity: Welcomed all devotees, regardless of caste or background.
            •	Chaitanya’s Influence and Journey
                •	Born in Nadia, a Vedantic rationalism center.
                •	At 22, initiated into the Krishna cult in Gaya.
                •	Traveled across India, including Vrindavan, reviving Krishna worship.
                •	Attracted Muslims and lower castes but did not reject scriptures or idol worship.
            •	Philosophical Approach
                •	Rooted in Vedantic monism, emphasizing unity of God and creation.
                •	Influenced by Vallabha, a Tailang Brahmin from the 15th–16th century.
                •	Humanistic approach: Focused on love and beauty in all forms.
                •	While they did not abolish caste, they softened its rigidity.
            •	Connection Between Bhakti and Sufism
                •	Bhakti concepts resembled those of Sufi saints and poets.
                •	15th century: Ibn-i-Arabi’s monistic ideas gained traction.
                •	Unity of Being (Tauhid-i-Wajudi): All beings are manifestations of the divine.
                •	Influence of Hindu traditions: Sufis engaged with yogis and saints.
                •	Use of Hindi and Sanskrit: Some Sufis, like Malik Muhammad Jaisi, composed in Hindi.
                •	Hindi Bhakti songs were more impactful on Sufis than Persian poetry.
                •	Abdul Wahid Bilgrami: Wrote Haqa’iq-i-Hind, interpreting Bhakti terms in Sufi mysticism.
            •	Common Platform for Religious Unity
                •	By the 15th–16th centuries, Bhakti and Sufi movements had created a shared spiritual space.
                •	Provided a foundation for Akbar’s concept of Tauhid (unity of all religions).

    Literature and Fine Arts
        Sanskrit Literature
            •	Continued Prominence
                •	Remained a vehicle for higher thought and literary expression.
                •	Production of works was immense, possibly surpassing the previous period.
                •	Flourished in various fields, including:
                    •	Philosophy (e.g., Ramanuja, Madhava, Vallabha).
                    •	Poetry, drama, fiction, medicine, astronomy, music.
                    •	Hindu law (Dharmashastras): Numerous commentaries and digests.
            •	Role in Dissemination of Knowledge
                •	Ideas spread rapidly across the country.
                •	Network of specialized schools and academies existed, even under Muslim rule.
                •	Benefited from the introduction of paper, aiding in:
                    •	Reproduction and distribution of texts.
                    •	Preservation of Ramayana and Mahabharata texts (11th–12th century).
            •	Major Legal Works
                •	Mitakshara of Vijayaneshwar (one of two principal Hindu law schools) – 12th century.
                •	Chandeshwar of Bihar – Notable 14th-century commentator.
                •	Most legal works produced in South India, Bengal, Mithila, and Western India.
            •	Jain Contributions
                •	The Jains actively contributed to Sanskrit literature.
                •	Hemachandra Suri was the most prominent Jain scholar.
            •	Limited Interaction with Islamic Culture
                •	Sanskrit literature largely ignored the Muslim presence.
                •	Minimal efforts to translate Islamic or Persian works.
                •	Exceptions:
                    •	Yusuf and Zulaikha (Persian love story by Jami) translated.
                    •	Some works on the astrolabe (used in navigation and astrology) translated.
        Arabic and Persian Literature
            Arabic Literature
                •	Arabic remained the primary language for:
                    •	Islamic scholarship and philosophy.
                    •	Scientific works, including some translations into Persian.
                    •	Digests of Islamic law, prepared in both Arabic and Persian.
                        •	Famous Persian digests: Compiled during Firuz Tughlaq’s reign.
                        •	Famous Arabic digest: Fatawa-z-Alamgiri (prepared under Aurangzeb).
            Introduction of Persian in India
                •	Turks introduced Persian in India during the 10th century.
                •	Persian had already become the literary and administrative language of Central Asia and Iran.
                •	Persian poets like Firdausi and Sadi flourished between the 10th and 14th centuries.
                •	Lahore became the first centre for Persian literary cultivation in India.
            Amir Khusrau: The Pioneer of Indo-Persian Literature
                •	Born in 1252 (Patiali, near Badayun, Western UP).
                •	Took pride in being Indian and praised the land:
                    •	“Hindustan is like heaven… greener and more beautiful than Khurasan.”
                •	Developed a unique Persian style called Sabaq-i-Hindi (Style of India).
                •	Wrote historical romances and poetry in multiple forms.
                •	Praised Indian languages, including Hindi (Hindavi).
                •	Participated in Sufi musical gatherings (Sama) with Nizamuddin Auliya.
                •	Died in 1325, soon after Nizamuddin Auliya’s death, and was buried in the same compound.
            Growth of Persian Historiography
                •	A strong tradition of history writing in Persian emerged.
                •	Notable historians:
                    •	Ziauddin Barani
                    •	Aff
                    •	Isami
            Persian as a Cultural Link
                •	Persian facilitated India’s cultural ties with Central Asia and Iran.
                •	Became the language of administration and diplomacy.
                •	Spread beyond North India as the Delhi Sultanate expanded south.
            Sanskrit-Persian Interchange
                •	Initially, minimal interaction between Sanskrit and Persian.
                •	Zia Nakhshabi (d. 1350) pioneered translations from Sanskrit to Persian:
                    •	Taz Nama (Book of the Parrot): Popular Sanskrit tale translated into Persian.
                    •	Kok Shastra (Indian treatise on sexology).
                •	Under Firuz Shah:
                    •	Sanskrit texts on medicine and music were translated into Persian.
                •	Sultan Zain-ul-Abidin of Kashmir:
                    •	Commissioned translations of Rajatarangini and Mahabharata into Persian.
                    •	Sponsored translations of Sanskrit medical and musical texts.
                •	Some scientific works (mathematics, astronomy, medicine) were also translated into Sanskrit during this period.
        Regional Languages
            Development of Regional Languages
                •	Several regional languages, such as Hindi, Bengali, and Marathi, originated around the 8th century, while Tamil was much older.
                •	By the 14th century, Amir Khusrau acknowledged their significance, stating:
                    •	“These languages have, from ancient times, been applied in every way to the common purposes of life.”
                •	The maturity of these languages and their use in literature became a striking feature of the medieval period.
            Factors Behind Their Growth
                •	Decline in Brahminical influence led to a decrease in Sanskrit’s prestige.
                •	Bhakti saints played a crucial role by using these languages in their teachings.
                •	Many early saints helped shape regional languages for literary purposes.
                •	Pre-Turkish regional kingdoms (e.g., Tamil, Kannada, Marathi regions) used local languages alongside Sanskrit for administration.
                •	Even under the Delhi Sultanate, Hindi-knowing accountants were appointed in revenue departments.
            Patronage and Administrative Use
                •	After the Delhi Sultanate’s decline, regional languages gained prominence alongside Persian in local administrations.
                •	South India:
                    •	Telugu literature thrived under Vijayanagara rulers’ patronage.
                •	Deccan:
                    •	Marathi was an administrative language in the Bahmani kingdom and later at Bijapur’s court.
                •	Bengal:
                    •	Nusrat Shah commissioned Mahabharata and Ramayana translations into Bengali.
                    •	Maladhar Basu translated the Bhagavata into Bengali under royal patronage.
            Role of Sufis in Regional Literature
                •	Sufi saints used Hindi Bhakti poetry in their musical gatherings.
                •	In Jaunpur, Sufi poets like Malik Muhammad Jaisi:
                    •	Wrote in Hindi, making Sufi concepts accessible to the common people.
                    •	Helped popularize Persian poetic forms, such as the masnavi.
        Fine Arts: Music and Cultural Integration
            Introduction of Persian and Central Asian Musical Traditions
                •	The Turks inherited a rich Arab musical tradition, further refined in Iran and Central Asia.
                •	They introduced several new musical instruments to India, including:
                    •	Rabab
                    •	Sarangi
                •	They also brought new musical modes and regulations.
                •	Indian music had earlier influenced musical developments at the Caliphs’ court in Baghdad, but systematic interaction began under the Delhi Sultanate.
            Amir Khusrau’s Contributions
                •	Amir Khusrau, a master of both music theory and practice, played a key role in musical integration.
                •	He introduced Perso-Arabic ragas, such as:
                    •	Aman
                    •	Ghora
                    •	Senam
                •	He is sometimes credited with inventing the sitar, though there is no concrete evidence.
                •	The tabla, often attributed to him, likely developed much later (late 17th or early 18th century).
            Music During the Sultanate Period
                •	Firuz Shah Tughlaq:
                    •	Regularly listened to music every Friday after namaz.
                    •	Under his reign, the Indian classical work Regadarpan was translated into Persian.
                •	Spread of Music:
                    •	Musical gatherings expanded from Sufi abodes to noble courts.
                •	Sultan Husain Sharqi of Jaunpur:
                    •	A great patron of music.
                    •	Pir Bodhan, a Sufi saint, was the second most renowned musician of the era.
                •	Gwalior as a Musical Hub:
                    •	Raja Man Singh of Gwalior was a notable patron.
                    •	His court produced the work Mar Kautihal, which documented new musical modes introduced by Muslims.
                Differentiation of North and South Indian Music
                    •	It is unclear when North and South Indian music diverged.
                    •	However, the distinction likely arose due to the incorporation of Perso-Arabic musical elements in North India.
                    •	A unique musical style, significantly influenced by Persian music, developed in Kashmir.
                Later Patronage and Mughal Influence
                    •	After conquering Jaunpur, Sikandar Lodi continued its musical traditions, lavishly supporting musicians.
                    •	This patronage of music was later carried forward by the Mughals.


- Struggle for Empire in North India—The Mughals and Afghans (1525-1555)
    The Political Landscape of Central and West Asia in the Fifteenth Century
        The Rise of Timur and His Empire
            •	Following the disintegration of the Mongol Empire in the 14th century, Timur reunited Iran and Turan under a single rule.
            •	His empire stretched from:
                •	The lower Volga to the Indus River
                •	Included Asia Minor (modern Turkey), Iran, Trans-Oxiana, Afghanistan, and part of Punjab
            •	Timur died in 1405, but his grandson Shahrukh Mirza (d. 1448) managed to preserve a large part of the empire.
            •	Under his patronage, Samarkand and Herat became major cultural centers of West Asia.
            •	The ruler of Samarkand held great prestige in the Islamic world.
        Decline of the Timurid Empire and the Rise of New Powers
            •	The Timurid tradition of partitioning the empire led to internal conflicts.
            •	As the Timurids weakened, two new forces emerged:
                1.	The Uzbeks:
                    •	A Turko-Mongol tribe that moved into Trans-Oxiana.
                    •	Though Muslims, they were looked down upon by the Timurids, who saw them as uncultured barbarians.
                2.	The Safavids of Iran:
                    •	A new Shiite dynasty descended from an order of saints tracing their ancestry to the Prophet.
                    •	They persecuted Sunnis, leading to religious conflicts with the Sunni Uzbeks.
            •	Meanwhile, further west, the Ottoman Turks were rising in power, seeking to dominate Eastern Europe, Iraq, and Iran.
            •	This set the stage for the sixteenth-century conflict between the three mighty empires:
                •	Uzbeks
                •	Safavids
                •	Ottomans
        Babur’s Struggles and the Uzbek Threat
            •	In 1494, Babur inherited Farghana, a small state in Trans-Oxiana, at just twelve years old.
            •	While the Timurid princes were engaged in internal battles, Babur attempted to capture Samarkand from his uncle.
            •	He briefly occupied the city twice but lost it both times.
            •	In his second attempt, the Uzbek chief Shaibani Khan was called in to remove Babur.
            •	Shaibani defeated Babur, conquered Samarkand, and then overran other Timurid kingdoms.
            •	Babur was forced to retreat to Kabul, which he conquered in 1504.
        Babur’s Attempts to Reclaim Samarkand and the Safavid-Uzbek Conflict
            •	For the next 14 years, Babur sought allies to reclaim his homeland from the Uzbeks.
            •	He sought help from his uncle, the ruler of Herat, but was unsuccessful.
            •	Ultimately, Herat also fell to Shaibani Khan, leading to direct conflict between:
                •	The Uzbeks, who controlled Trans-Oxiana.
                •	The Safavids, who claimed Herat and Khurasan.
            •	In 1510, in a famous battle near Merv, Shah Ismail of Iran defeated and killed Shaibani Khan.
        Babur’s Brief Rule in Samarkand and Final Uzbek Victory
            •	With Iranian support, Babur recaptured Samarkand.
            •	However, he struggled under Iranian control, as the Safavids treated him as a mere governor of an Iranian province, not an independent ruler.
            •	The Uzbeks quickly recovered, and Babur was once again ousted from Samarkand, forcing him to return to Kabul.
            •	Meanwhile, in 1514, Shah Ismail of Iran was defeated by the Ottoman Sultan, solidifying Uzbek dominance over Trans-Oxiana.
        Babur’s Shift Toward India
            •	With no hope of reclaiming Samarkand, Babur was ultimately forced to look toward India as his next conquest.

    Babur’s Motivations and Early Attempts to Conquer India
        Babur’s Long-Standing Desire to Conquer India
            •	Babur himself stated that from the time he obtained Kabul (1504) until his victory at Panipat (1526), he had never stopped thinking about conquering Hindustan.
            •	However, his plans were delayed due to:
                •	Apprehensions of his degs (loyal followers and soldiers)
                •	Disagreements between his brothers
            •	Like earlier Central Asian invaders, Babur was drawn to India’s immense wealth.
            •	His ancestor, Timur, had:
                •	Plundered a vast treasure from India.
                •	Taken skilled artisans who helped develop his empire and beautify his capital.
                •	Annexed parts of Punjab, which remained under Timur’s successors for generations.
            •	After conquering Afghanistan, Babur considered himself the rightful heir to these areas.
        Economic and Strategic Reasons for Babur’s Invasion
            •	Babur’s empire in Badakhshan, Qandhar, and Kabul was not wealthy enough to sustain his army and administration.
            •	Abul Fazl noted that in some territories, military and administrative costs exceeded revenue.
            •	With limited resources, Babur struggled to provide for his followers and feared an Uzbek attack on Kabul.
            •	India was seen as:
                •	A potential place of refuge.
                •	A strategic base for launching operations against the Uzbeks.
        Political Situation in Northwest India (Ideal Conditions for Babur’s Invasion)
            •	Sikandar Lodi had died in 1517, and his son Ibrahim Lodi had succeeded him.
            •	Ibrahim Lodi’s attempts to centralize power alienated:
                •	Afghan chiefs
                •	Rajputs
            •	One of the most powerful Afghan chiefs, Daulat Khan Lodi, was the governor of Punjab and nearly independent.
            •	He tried to maintain favor with Ibrahim Lodi by sending his son to court but also expanded his own power by annexing frontier regions like Bhira.
        Babur’s Early Campaigns in Punjab
            First Expedition (1518-19)
                •	Babur captured the powerful fort of Bhira.
                •	He then sent envoys to both Daulat Khan and Ibrahim Lodi, demanding the return of Turk-controlled territories.
                •	However, Daulat Khan detained Babur’s envoy in Lahore, refusing to either:
                    •	Grant an audience
                    •	Forward the message to Ibrahim Lodi
                •	After Babur returned to Kabul, Daulat Khan took advantage of his absence, occupied Bhira, and expelled Babur’s men.
            Second Expedition (1520-21)
                •	Babur crossed the Indus again and swiftly recaptured Bhira and Sialkot—both key gateways to India.
                •	Lahore also fell to him.
                •	He considered advancing further but was forced to retreat due to:
                    •	A revolt in Qandhar
                    •	A prolonged siege of Qandhar (lasting 1.5 years), which he successfully ended
                •	With Qandhar secured, Babur was once again free to focus on India.
        Invitation from Indian Nobles and Final Preparations
            •	Around this time, Babur received an embassy from Daulat Khan Lodi, led by his son Dilawar Khan.
            •	They invited Babur to invade India, arguing that:
                •	Ibrahim Lodi was a tyrant.
                •	He had lost the support of nobles.
            •	Additionally, it is believed that Rana Sanga of Mewar also sent a separate invitation urging Babur to invade.
            •	These appeals convinced Babur that the time was ripe to seize Punjab—if not all of India.
        Final Conquest of Punjab (1525)
            •	In 1525, while Babur was at Peshawar, he received news that:
                •	Daulat Khan had switched sides again.
                •	He had gathered 30,000-40,000 troops.
                •	He had ousted Babur’s men from Sialkot and was advancing toward Lahore.
            •	However, when Babur marched toward Lahore, Daulat Khan’s army quickly disbanded.
            •	Daulat Khan surrendered and was pardoned.
            •	Within three weeks of crossing the Indus, Babur became the undisputed master of Punjab.

    The Battle of Panipat (20 April 1526)
        Prelude to the Battle
            •	A conflict between Babur and Ibrahim Lodi was inevitable.
            •	Babur marched towards Delhi, and Ibrahim Lodi met him at Panipat with:
                •	100,000 men (though the actual number of fighting men was lower due to servants).
                •	1,000 elephants.
            •	Babur had crossed the Indus with 12,000 men, but his forces increased due to:
                •	Reinforcements from his army in India.
                •	Support from Hindustani nobles and soldiers in Punjab.
            •	Despite this, Babur’s army was still numerically inferior.
        Babur’s Battle Preparations
            •	To strengthen his position, Babur:
                •	Anchored one wing of his army against Panipat’s buildings.
                •	Protected the other wing with a ditch filled with tree branches.
                •	Constructed a defensive wall using lashed-together carts.
                •	Built breastworks between carts for resting guns and firing.
                •	This setup was called the Ottoman (Rumi) device, inspired by the Ottoman victory over Shah Ismail of Iran.
            •	Babur had two Ottoman master-gunners, Ustad Ali and Mustafa, which gave him a significant advantage.
            •	Although gunpowder was known in India, its widespread use in artillery only became common with Babur.
        The Battle Begins
            •	Ibrahim Lodi was unaware of Babur’s strongly fortified position.
            •	He had expected Babur to engage in mobile warfare, characteristic of Central Asians, involving rapid advances and retreats.
            •	After seven or eight days of skirmishing, Ibrahim Lodi finally mobilized for battle.
            •	His forces hesitated upon seeing Babur’s strong defenses.
            •	While Ibrahim was still reorganizing his troops, Babur’s flanking forces attacked from the side and rear.
        Babur’s Tactical Superiority
            •	Babur’s gunners effectively used firearms from the front.
            •	However, Babur credited much of his victory to his bowmen.
            •	Interestingly, he made little mention of Ibrahim Lodi’s elephants, suggesting they played a minor role in the battle.
        The Battle’s Climax
            •	Despite early setbacks, Ibrahim Lodi’s army fought bravely.
            •	The battle lasted two to three hours.
            •	Ibrahim Lodi fought to the end, surrounded by 5,900–6,000 men.
            •	More than 15,000 of Ibrahim Lodi’s soldiers were killed in the battle.
        Impact of the Battle
            Decisive Consequences
                •	The Battle of Panipat is considered one of the most decisive battles in Indian history.
                •	It:
                    •	Destroyed Lodi power.
                    •	Gave Babur control over Delhi and Agra.
                    •	Secured immense treasures stored by Ibrahim Lodi in Agra, solving Babur’s financial problems.
                    •	Left rich territories up to Jaipur open for Babur’s expansion.
            Challenges After the Victory
                •	Despite his success, Babur still had to fight two major battles to consolidate his rule:
                    1.	Against Rana Sanga of Mewar.
                    2.	Against the eastern Afghans.
                •	Thus, Panipat was not politically decisive, as further conflicts were necessary to secure Babur’s empire.
                •	Its real significance lay in:
                    •	Introducing modern warfare techniques (gunpowder and artillery) in India.
                    •	Opening a new phase in North India’s power struggle.
        Post-Battle Difficulties
            •	Babur faced multiple challenges after his victory:
                •	Many of his begs (nobles) were unwilling to stay in India for long.
                •	The hot climate and unfamiliar conditions increased their reluctance.
                •	The Indian population showed hostility, abandoning villages in fear of Mughal armies—a reaction to Timur’s past plundering.
            Babur’s Firm Stand
                •	Babur understood that India’s wealth was necessary to build a strong empire and satisfy his followers.
                •	He declared:
                    •	“Not for us the poverty of Kabul again!”
                    •	He firmly proclaimed his intention to stay in India.
                    •	Allowed those who wished to return to Kabul to leave, clearing internal tensions.
                •	However, Babur’s presence in India alarmed Rana Sanga, who began preparing for a confrontation.

    The Battle of Khanwa (1527)
        Background and Causes
            •	Conflict over Eastern Rajasthan and Malwa:
                •	Rana Sanga and Ibrahim Lodi had been competing for dominance in this region.
                •	After defeating Mahmud Khalji of Malwa, Rana Sanga’s influence extended up to Piliya Khar, near Agra.
                •	Babur’s establishment of an Indo-Gangetic empire threatened Rana Sanga’s ambitions.
                •	Sanga began preparations to either expel Babur or confine him to Punjab.
            •	Babur’s Accusation of Betrayal:
                •	Babur claimed Rana Sanga had invited him to India, promising to join him against Ibrahim Lodi.
                •	However, Sanga made no move while Babur conquered Delhi and Agra.
                •	It is unclear what precise promises Sanga made:
                    •	He may have hoped for prolonged warfare, allowing him to seize desired territories.
                    •	He may have assumed Babur, like Timur, would plunder Delhi and retreat.
                •	Babur’s decision to stay in India changed the political situation completely.
        Formation of the Anti-Babur Alliance
            •	Many Afghans, including Mahmud Lodi (Ibrahim Lodi’s younger brother), joined Sanga, hoping to restore Afghan rule in Delhi.
            •	Hasan Khan Mewati, the ruler of Mewat, also allied with Sanga.
            •	Almost all prominent Rajput rulers sent troops to fight under Sanga.
        Babur’s Response and Preparations
            •	Morale Crisis in Babur’s Army:
                •	Rana Sanga’s reputation and early victories against outlying Mughal posts (e.g., Bayana) demoralized Babur’s soldiers.
                •	To rally his troops, Babur:
                    •	Declared the war against Sanga a jihad.
                    •	On the eve of battle, smashed all wine jars and flasks to prove his religious commitment.
                    •	Banned the sale and consumption of wine across his domains.
                    •	Abolished customs taxes for Muslims.
            •	Strategic Fortifications at Khanwa:
                •	Babur chose a defensive site at Khanwa, 40 km from Agra.
                •	His defensive setup resembled Panipat’s strategy:
                    •	Wagons lashed together as an outer bastion.
                    •	A trench dug in front for added protection.
                    •	Gaps left for musketeers to fire and advance behind wheeled tripods.
        The Battle
            •	Sanga’s Forces and Initial Attacks:
                •	Babur estimated Sanga’s army at over 200,000, including:
                    •	10,000 Afghan cavalry.
                    •	A force of equal size from Hasan Khan Mewati.
                •	While these figures may be exaggerated, Babur’s army was definitely outnumbered.
                •	Sanga launched a fierce attack on Babur’s right, nearly breaching his defenses.
            •	Babur’s Counterattack and Victory:
                •	Mughal artillery inflicted heavy casualties on Sanga’s forces.
                •	As Sanga’s forces began to weaken, Babur:
                    •	Ordered a central charge, with troops advancing from behind their tripods.
                    •	Pushed forward artillery, supported by chained wagons.
                    •	Used his flanking parties to attack from the side and rear, similar to his tactics at Panipat.
                •	Sanga’s army was surrounded and suffered massive losses.
                •	Rana Sanga escaped but later died—poisoned by his own nobles, who feared continuing the war would be disastrous.
        Consequences of the Battle
            End of Sanga’s Dream of a Rajput Empire
                •	Rana Sanga was one of the bravest warriors of Rajasthan, but his death shattered the vision of a united Rajputana extending up to Agra.
            Babur’s Consolidation of Power
                •	Secured Mughal control over the Delhi-Agra region.
                •	Strengthened his hold by capturing Gwalior, Dholpur, and other forts east of Agra.
                •	Annexed large parts of Alwar, previously ruled by Hasan Khan Mewati.
            Further Campaigns and Setbacks
                •	Led a campaign against Medini Rai of Chanderi in Malwa.
                •	Chanderi fell after a fierce battle—the Rajput defenders fought to the last man, while their women committed jauhar.
                •	Babur’s plans for further expansion were cut short due to rising Afghan activities in eastern Uttar Pradesh.
    
    Consequences of the Afghan Resistance and Babur’s Final Campaign
        Afghan Resistance and Threats
            •	Despite their defeat, the Afghans in eastern Uttar Pradesh had not accepted Mughal rule.
            •	Afghan chiefs, initially loyal to Babur, were ready to revolt when given the opportunity.
            •	Nusrat Shah, the ruler of Bengal and son-in-law of Ibrahim Lodi, supported the Afghan resurgence.
            •	The Afghans expelled Mughal officials and advanced as far as Kanauj.
            •	Their primary weakness was the absence of a strong, unifying leader.
        Mahmud Lodi’s Leadership and Babur’s Response
            •	Mahmud Lodi, Ibrahim Lodi’s brother and a former commander at Khanwa, arrived in Bihar.
            •	The Afghans hailed him as their ruler, rallying behind him for another challenge to Mughal rule.
            •	Recognizing the growing threat, Babur marched east from Agra in early 1529.
        Battle of Ghagra (1529) and Its Aftermath
            •	Babur crossed the Ganga near Banaras and confronted the combined forces of the Afghans and Nusrat Shah of Bengal at the river Ghagra.
            •	Despite crossing the river and forcing the enemy to retreat, Babur could not secure a decisive victory.
            •	His declining health and concerns about Central Asia led him to negotiate peace.
        Peace Settlements and Babur’s Final Days
            •	Babur made a nominal claim of suzerainty over Bihar but left most of it under Afghan control.
            •	He signed a treaty with Nusrat Shah of Bengal, avoiding further conflict.
            •	Shortly after, Babur fell seriously ill and died near Lahore while on his way to Kabul.

    Significance of Babur’s Advent into India
        Strategic and Economic Impact
            •	Integrated Kabul and Qandhar into the Indian empire for the first time since the Kushan era.
            •	Secured India from external invasions for nearly 200 years by controlling key entry points.
            •	Strengthened India’s foreign trade, as Kabul and Qandhar served as caravan hubs for China and Mediterranean trade routes.
        Political and Military Contributions
            •	Destroyed the balance of power in north India by defeating the Lodis and the Rajput confederacy led by Rana Sanga.
            •	Took a major step toward establishing an all-India empire, though further consolidation was needed.
            •	Introduced advanced warfare by demonstrating the effectiveness of artillery and cavalry.
            •	Led to the rapid popularization of gunpowder and artillery in India, favoring rulers with strong resources and marking the decline of small kingdoms.
        Reinforcement of the Crown’s Prestige
            •	Re-established the authority of the Crown, which had weakened since the death of Firuz Tughlaq.
            •	Unlike the Lodis, Babur’s authority was unquestioned due to his lineage from Changez Khan and Timur.
            •	None of his nobles could claim an equal status, ensuring a stable monarchy.
        Personal Leadership and Character
            •	Earned the loyalty of his begs through personal courage and shared hardships.
            •	Led by example—once personally helped soldiers clear deep snow for passage during a harsh winter.
            •	Balanced a love for wine and camaraderie with strict discipline and high expectations from his nobles.
            •	Showed instances of extreme cruelty, such as making towers of skulls from enemies, though reflective of his era.
        Religious and Cultural Policies
            •	Though an orthodox Sunni, Babur was not a bigot and avoided religious conflicts in his court.
            •	His declaration of jihad against Rana Sanga was politically motivated rather than religious.
            •	No widespread evidence of temple destruction under his rule—mosques built at Sambhal and Ayodhya may have only been repairs.
        Patronage of Literature and Arts
            •	A prolific writer in Persian, Arabic, and Turkish, with the Tuzuk-i-Baburi being a literary classic.
            •	Translated Sufi works and wrote poetry (masnavi).
            •	Maintained connections with leading poets and artists of the time.
            •	Passionate about nature, documenting India’s flora and fauna in detail.
            •	Established the tradition of Mughal gardens with running water.
        Legacy and Concept of the Mughal State
            •	Defined the Mughal state as one based on a strong monarchy, free from religious bigotry.
            •	Emphasized cultural and artistic patronage alongside military and administrative strength.
            •	Set a precedent for his successors, shaping the future of Mughal rule in India.

    Humayun’s Conquest of Gujarat and Struggle with Sher Shah
        Challenges Faced by Humayun
            •	Succeeded Babur in December 1530 at the age of 23.
            •	Faced multiple problems:
                •	Unstable administration and precarious finances.
                •	The Afghans were not subdued and still hoped to expel the Mughals.
                •	The Timurid tradition of partitioning the empire among brothers threatened unity.
            •	Babur had advised kindness towards brothers but opposed partitioning the empire.
        Kamran’s Expansion and Its Impact
            •	At the time of Humayun’s accession, the empire included Kabul, Qandhar, and Badakhshan.
            •	Kamran, Humayun’s younger brother, controlled Kabul and Qandhar but sought more territory.
            •	He marched on Lahore and Multan, occupying them.
            •	Humayun, to avoid civil war, accepted this and formally granted Punjab and Multan to Kamran.
            •	This allowed Humayun to focus on the eastern threats without worrying about the western frontier.
        Conflict with the Afghans and Sher Khan
            •	The Afghan power was growing rapidly in the east.
            •	In 1532, at Dadrah, Humayun defeated the Afghans, who had conquered Bihar and overrun Jaunpur.
            •	Besieged Chunar, a key fort controlling routes to eastern India.
            •	Sher Khan, an Afghan leader, held Chunar and negotiated peace by:
                •	Retaining the fort while pledging loyalty to the Mughals.
                •	Sending his son as a hostage.
            •	Humayun, anxious about Bahadur Shah’s rising power, accepted the deal.
        Bahadur Shah of Gujarat – A Rising Threat
            •	Bahadur Shah, nearly the same age as Humayun, was ambitious and capable.
            •	Came to power in 1526, conquered Malwa, and then besieged Chittor.
            •	Fear of Mughal intervention forced him to sign a treaty with the Rajputs.
            •	Humayun, meanwhile, spent time building Dinpanah in Delhi, which was criticized as a distraction.
            •	However, Dinpanah was meant to:
                •	Serve as a second capital if Agra was threatened.
                •	Impress both friends and enemies.
            •	In the meantime, Bahadur Shah conquered Ajmer and overran eastern Rajasthan.
        Battle Against Bahadur Shah
            •	Bahadur Shah gave refuge to Mughal enemies and supplied arms to Tatar Khan, cousin of Ibrahim Lodi.
            •	Tatar Khan planned an invasion of Agra with 40,000 troops, but Humayun easily defeated him.
            •	Determined to eliminate the threat from Gujarat, Humayun invaded Malwa.
            •	Bahadur Shah avoided battle, abandoning Chittor and fortifying Mandsor.
            •	Humayun’s cautious tactics forced Bahadur Shah to flee to Mandu, leaving behind:
                •	Spiked guns (rendering them useless).
                •	Wealth and military equipment.
            •	Humayun captured Mandu, then pursued Bahadur Shah to Champaner, scaling its fort walls himself.
            •	Bahadur Shah fled to Ahmedabad and finally to Kathiawar.
            •	As a result, Malwa and Gujarat, along with their rich treasures, fell under Mughal control.
        Loss of Gujarat and Malwa
            •	Gujarat was placed under Askari, Humayun’s younger brother.
            •	Humayun retired to Mandu, considering it a central and favorable location.
            •	Problems arose:
                •	People’s deep loyalty to Gujarati rule.
                •	Askari’s inexperience and divided Mughal nobility.
                •	Popular uprisings and military counterattacks.
            •	Bahadur Shah’s power quickly revived, and Askari was forced to retreat to Champaner.
            •	Fear arose that Askari might attempt to seize Agra or form his own empire.
            •	To prevent this, Humayun:
                •	Abandoned Malwa and pursued Askari.
                •	Overtook him in Rajasthan, reconciled, and returned to Agra together.
            •	Meanwhile, both Gujarat and Malwa were lost.
        Outcome of the Gujarat Campaign
            •	The campaign did not expand Mughal territory but neutralized Bahadur Shah as a threat.
            •	Allowed Humayun to focus on Sher Khan and the Afghans.
            •	Bahadur Shah later drowned in a scuffle with the Portuguese, permanently ending the threat from Gujarat.

    Humayun’s Struggle Against Sher Shah and His Downfall
        - Strengthening of Sher Khan’s Position (1535–1537)
            •	During Humayun’s Malwa campaign (Feb 1535 – Feb 1537), Sher Khan consolidated his power.
            •	He became the undisputed ruler of Bihar, attracting Afghan support from across India.
            •	Though he continued to profess loyalty to the Mughals, he systematically planned their expulsion.
            •	He maintained close ties with Bahadur Shah of Gujarat, receiving heavy subsidies.
            •	With these resources, he built a large, efficient army, including 1,200 elephants.
            •	Shortly after Humayun’s return to Agra, Sher Khan used his strengthened army to defeat the Bengal king and force him to pay an indemnity of 1,300,000 dinars.
        - Humayun’s Chunar Campaign and Sher Khan’s Bengal Conquest
            •	Humayun, realizing the strategic importance of Chunar, besieged the fort towards the end of the year.
            •	The fort, strongly defended by Afghans, took six months to capture despite master-gunner Rumi Khan’s efforts.
            •	Meanwhile, Sher Khan seized the powerful Rohtas Fort through treachery, ensuring his family’s safety.
            •	He then invaded Bengal a second time and captured its capital, Gaur.
            •	By this maneuvering, Sher Khan completely outwitted Humayun.
        - Humayun’s Miscalculation and the Bengal Expedition
            •	Humayun failed to recognize that he lacked the necessary strength to confront Sher Khan.
            •	Sher Khan offered a deal: he would surrender Bihar and pay an annual tribute of 10 lakh dinars if allowed to retain Bengal.
            •	Humayun rejected this offer, unwilling to concede Bengal’s wealth, trade significance, and strategic position.
            •	The wounded Bengal king, seeking refuge with Humayun, encouraged resistance but soon succumbed to his injuries.
            •	Humayun thus undertook the Bengal campaign alone, leading to a disastrous sequence of events.
        - Disaster at Chausa (March 1539)
            •	Sher Khan strategically allowed Humayun to advance into Bengal unopposed, intending to cut off his communications.
            •	Humayun reached Gaur and established order but remained unaware of Sher Khan’s movements.
            •	Meanwhile, his younger brother Hindal attempted to seize the Mughal throne at Agra, isolating Humayun further.
            •	After a stay of 3–4 months, Humayun began his return journey.
            •	Despite monsoon challenges, discontent among nobles, and constant Afghan attacks, Humayun managed to reach Chausa near Buxar.
            •	His brother Kamran, who had marched from Lahore to Agra to suppress Hindal’s rebellion, refused to send reinforcements.
            •	Sher Khan’s Deception and Humayun’s Tactical Mistake
                •	Misled by a false peace offer from Sher Khan, Humayun crossed to the eastern bank of the Karamasa River.
                •	The Afghan horsemen took advantage of this mistake and launched a surprise attack.
                •	The battle turned into a catastrophe for the Mughals.
                •	Humayun barely escaped, swimming across the river with the help of a water carrier.
                •	Sher Khan captured immense booty, and around 7,000 Mughal soldiers, including prominent nobles, perished.
        - Final Defeat at Kanauj (May 1540) and Humayun’s Exile
            •	After Chausa, Humayun’s only hope lay in unity among the Timurid princes.
            •	Kamran commanded a seasoned army of 10,000 troops in Agra but refused to aid Humayun, doubting his leadership.
            •	Humayun, in turn, refused to entrust Kamran with military command, fearing a power grab.
            •	The growing distrust led Kamran to withdraw to Lahore with most of his troops.
            •	The weakened Mughal army faced Sher Khan at Kanauj in May 1540.
            •	Despite fierce resistance from Humayun’s brothers, Askari and Hindal, the Mughals suffered a decisive defeat.
            •	This battle sealed Sher Khan’s victory and left Humayun without a kingdom.
            •	Wandering and Seeking Support (1540–1545)
                •	With Kabul and Qandhar under Kamran’s control, Humayun wandered across Sindh and neighboring regions for over two years.
                •	He sought assistance from various rulers, including Maldeo of Marwar, but was unsuccessful.
                •	His brothers turned against him, even attempting to imprison or kill him.
                •	Despite these hardships, Humayun displayed remarkable resilience and fortitude.
                •	Eventually, he sought refuge in Iran, where he gained the support of the Persian ruler.
                •	With Persian aid, he recaptured Qandhar and Kabul in 1545.
        - Causes of Humayun’s Defeat
            •	Underestimation of Afghan Strength: 
                - Humayun failed to grasp the resilience of Afghan power in North India.
            •	Lack of Local Support: 
                - Unlike the Afghans, the Mughals did not win over local rulers and zamindars, leaving them numerically weak.
            •	Disunity Among the Timurids: 
                - Though initially loyal, Humayun’s brothers turned against him after Sher Khan’s victories.
            •	Strategic and Tactical Mistakes: 
                - His ill-planned Bengal campaign and misjudgment at Chausa exposed his military weaknesses.
            •	Superiority of Sher Khan: 
                - Sher Khan proved to be a more capable general and strategist than Humayun.
        - Humayun’s Later Years and Death
            •	Humayun’s life was marked by extreme reversals—falling from power and later regaining it.
            •	In 1555, following the collapse of the Sur empire, he managed to recover Delhi.
            •	However, his return to power was short-lived.
            •	In 1556, he died after falling from the first floor of his library in Delhi.
            •	His favorite wife commissioned a magnificent mausoleum for him near the Delhi fort.
            •	The tomb, known as Humayun’s Tomb, introduced a new architectural style in North India, particularly its grand marble dome.

    Sher Shah and the Sur Empire (1540-1555)
        Early Life and Rise to Power
            •	Sher Shah was born as Farid, son of a small jagirdar in Jaunpur.
            •	He gained administrative experience by managing his father’s jagir.
            •	After the defeat and death of Ibrahim Lodi, he emerged as an important Afghan sardar.
            •	The title “Sher Khan” was given to him for either killing a tiger or for his services.
            •	Before Babur’s death, he became the de facto ruler of Bihar and played a key role in Afghan politics.
        Expansion of the Sur Empire
            •	Sher Shah’s empire was the largest in North India since Muhammad bin Tughlaq, stretching from Bengal to the Indus (excluding Kashmir).
            •	In the west, he conquered Malwa and most of Rajasthan.
        Malwa Campaign and the Puran Mal Incident
            •	Malwa was in a weak state and could not resist Sher Shah.
            •	Puran Mal, the Rajput ruler of Chanderi, vacated his fort under a promise of safe conduct.
            •	However, he and 1,000 Rajput followers were treacherously attacked and killed along with their families.
            •	Contemporary theologians justified this act, claiming no faith was needed with infidels.
        Conflict with Maldeo of Marwar
            •	Maldeo (ruler of Marwar, since 1532) expanded his control over western and northern Rajasthan.
            •	He clashed with Mewar and other neighboring rulers.
            •	His conquest of Bikaner forced Kalyan Das, Bhim, and Biram Deo to seek refuge at Sher Shah’s court.
            •	Maldeo’s centralizing ambitions were seen as a threat by Delhi and Agra.
        Battle of Samel (1544)
            •	Rajput and Afghan forces clashed at Samel (between Ajmer and Jodhpur).
            •	Maldeo withdrew after suspecting disloyalty among his sardars, influenced by Sher Shah’s ruse of dropping fake letters near his camp.
            •	Some Rajput sardars refused to retreat and attacked Sher Shah’s center.
            •	Despite early Rajput success, superior Afghan numbers and gunfire led to their defeat.
        Aftermath and Rajasthan’s Conquest
            •	The battle sealed Rajasthan’s fate.
            •	Sher Shah besieged and captured Ajmer and Jodhpur.
            •	Maldeo took refuge in Siwana and died shortly afterward.
            •	The Rana of Mewar surrendered, sending the keys of Chittor to Sher Shah, who established outposts up to Mount Abu.
            •	Within ten months, Sher Shah overran nearly all of Rajasthan.
        Final Campaign and Death (1545)
            •	Sher Shah’s last campaign was against Kalinjar, a crucial Bundelkhand fort.
            •	During the siege, a gun explosion severely injured him.
            •	He died in 1545, shortly after hearing of the fort’s capture.
        Succession and Fall of the Sur Empire
            •	Islam Shah (Sher Shah’s second son) ruled until 1553.
            •	He was capable, but his reign was troubled by rebellions and Afghan tribal conflicts.
            •	Fear of a Mughal invasion further weakened his rule.
            •	His premature death led to a civil war among his successors.
            •	This allowed Humayun to reclaim his empire in 1555 after defeating the Afghans in two major battles.
        Sher Shah’s Legacy
            Architecture and City Building
                •	He was a great builder:
                •	His tomb at Sasaram is regarded as a masterpiece of architecture.
                •	He built a new city near Delhi, of which only Purana Qila (Old Fort) and its mosque survive.
            Patronage of Literature
                •	Sher Shah patronized scholars and poets.
                •	Notable works during his reign include Padmavat by Malik Muhammad Jaisi.
            Religious Policies
                •	Sher Shah was not a bigot, as reflected in his social and economic policies.
                •	Neither he nor Islam Shah depended on the ulama, though they respected them.
                •	However, religious justifications were used for political actions, as seen in:
                    •	The treacherous killing of Puran Mal after he vacated Raisen Fort on the promise of safety.
                •	He did not introduce new liberal policies:
                    •	Jizyah continued to be collected from Hindus.
                    •	His nobility was almost exclusively Afghan.
        Empire features and contribution to India: Governance, Economy, and Legacy
            Continuity with the Delhi Sultanate
                •	The Sur Empire can be seen as a continuation and culmination of the Delhi Sultanate, with the rule of Babur and Humayun acting as an interregnum.
                •	Sher Shah centralized power, continuing the administrative machinery of the Sultanate period.
            Law and Order
                •	Sher Shah re-established law and order throughout his empire.
                •	He sternly dealt with robbers, dacoits, and zamindars who refused to pay revenue or disobeyed government orders.
                •	His historian Abbas Khan Sarwani noted that zamindars were so cowed down that no one dared to rebel or harass travelers.
            Trade, Communication, and Infrastructure
                Roads and Sarais
                    •	Sher Shah revived and extended the Grand Trunk Road from the Indus River to Bengal.
                    •	He built roads connecting Agra, Jodhpur, Chittor, and Gujarat’s seaports.
                    •	Another major road linked Lahore to Multan, serving as a gateway to West and Central Asia.
                    •	1,700 sarais (inns) were built at 8 km intervals, providing:
                        •	Fortified lodging for travelers.
                        •	Separate accommodations for Hindus and Muslims.
                        •	Brahmins appointed to provide food for Hindus and grain for their horses.
                    •	Many sarais evolved into market towns and also served as dak-chowkis (news stations).
                Customs Reforms
                    •	Sher Shah streamlined customs duties, allowing payments only at:
                        •	Sikrigali (Bengal) for goods from Bengal and Bihar.
                        •	The Indus for goods from West and Central Asia.
                    •	No additional levies were imposed on roads, ferries, or towns.
                    •	Merchants were respected, and if one died, his property was not confiscated.
                Currency and Standardization
                    •	He introduced a uniform currency, including gold, silver, and copper coins.
                    •	The silver rupee became the standard currency for centuries.
                    •	Weights and measures were standardized, facilitating trade and commerce.
            Administration
                Administrative Divisions
                    •	Pargana (district level): Managed by a shiqdar (administrator) and a munsif (revenue officer).
                    •	Sarkar (province level): Managed by a shiqdar-i-shiqdaran and a munsif-i-munsifan.
                    •	These divisions were not new, only the titles changed.
                Over-Centralization of Power
                    •	Sher Shah’s direct involvement in governance was both a strength and a weakness.
                    •	While he worked tirelessly and personally toured the empire, his over-centralization created difficulties after his death.
            Land Revenue System
                •	Sher Shah insisted on land measurement before fixing the state’s share, set at one-third of the produce.
                •	Peasants could pay in cash or kind, though cash was preferred.
                •	All records of land, crops, and payments were maintained in a document called “patea” for transparency.
                •	A cess was collected to safeguard against famine and natural disasters.
                •	He was concerned about the peasantry, believing that excessive oppression would lead to desertion of villages and economic decline.
            Military Reforms
                •	Sher Shah directly recruited soldiers and maintained proper records.
                •	His army included:
                    •	150,000 cavalry
                    •	25,000 infantry
                    •	5,000 elephants
                    •	A well-equipped artillery park
                •	He established cantonments across the empire and maintained strong garrisons.
            Justice System
                •	Sher Shah believed that “Justice is the most excellent of religious rites”.
                •	He punished oppressors regardless of their rank.
                •	Village panchayats and zamindars handled local cases, while qaadis (judges) were appointed for judicial matters.
            Successor: Islam Shah (1545-1553)
                •	Islam Shah, Sher Shah’s son and successor, attempted further reforms:
                    •	Codified laws to minimize reliance on special interpreters of Islamic law.
                    •	Reduced the power of nobles and ensured cash payments for soldiers.
                •	However, after his premature death, most of his reforms were undone.
        Conclusion: The Afghan State and Mughal Takeover
            •	The Sur Empire remained an Afghan state, structured around race and tribal identity.
            •	A fundamental shift occurred only under Akbar, marking the beginning of a new era.

- Consolidation of the Mughal Empire: The Age of Akbar
    When Humayun was retreating from Bikaner, he was gallantly offered shelter and help by the Rana of Amarkot. It was at Amarkot, in 1542, that Akbar, the greatest of the Mughal rulers, was born. When Humayun fled to Iran, young Akbar was captured by his uncle, Kamran. He treated the child well on the whole. Akbar was reunited with his parents after the capture of Qandhar. When Humayun died, Akbar was at Kalanaur in Punjab, commanding against the Afghan rebels there. He was crowned at Kalanaur in 1556 at the young age of thirteen years and four months.

    Akbar succeeded to a difficult position. The Afghans were still strong beyond Agra, regrouping their forces under the leadership of Hemu for a final showdown. Kabul had been attacked and besieged. Sikandar Sur, the defeated Afghan ruler, was loitering in the Siwalik Hills. However, Bairam Khan, the tutor of the prince and a loyal and favorite officer of Humayun, rose to the occasion. He became the Wakil of the kingdom, with the title of Khan-i-Khanan, and rallied the Mughal forces. The threat from the side of Hemu was considered the most serious. The area from Chunar to the border of Bengal was under the domination of Adil Shah, a nephew of Sher Shah. Hemu, who had started life as a superintendent of markets under Islam Shah, had rapidly risen under Adil Shah. He had not lost a single one of the twenty-two battles in which he had fought. Adil Shah had appointed him the war leader with the title of Vikramajit and entrusted him with the task of expelling the Mughals. Hemu captured Agra, and with an army of 50,000 cavalry, 500 elephants, and a strong artillery, he marched upon Delhi.

    In a well-contested battle, Hemu defeated the Mughals near Delhi and occupied the city. However, Bairam Khan took energetic steps to meet the situation. His bold stand put heart into his army, and it marched on Delhi before Hemu could consolidate his position. The battle between the Mughals and the Afghan forces led by Hemu took place once again at Panipat (5 November 1556). Although Hemu’s artillery had been captured earlier by a Mughal detachment, the tide of battle was in favor of Hemu when an arrow hit him in the eye and he fainted. The leaderless Afghan army was defeated, and Hemu was captured and executed. Thus, Akbar had virtually to reconquer his empire.

    Early Tasks—Confrontation with the Nobles (1556-67)
        Bairam Khan remained at the helm of affairs of the empire for almost four years. During this period, he kept the nobility fully under control. The danger to Kabul was averted, and the territories of the empire were extended from Kabul up to Jaunpur in the east and Ajmer in the west. Gwalior was captured, and forces were sent to conquer Ranthambhor and Malwa.

        Meanwhile, Akbar was approaching the age of maturity. Bairam Khan had offended many powerful persons while he held supreme power. They complained that Bairam Khan was a Shia, and that he was appointing his own supporters and Shias to high offices while neglecting the old nobles. These charges were not very serious in themselves because Bairam was known for his liberal religious views. However, Bairam Khan had become arrogant and failed to realize that Akbar was growing up. There was friction on small points which made Akbar realize that he could not leave the affairs of the state in someone else’s hands for any length of time.

        Akbar played his cards deftly. He left Agra on the pretext of hunting and reached Delhi. From Delhi, he issued a farman dismissing Bairam Khan from his office and calling upon all the nobles to come and submit to him personally. Once Bairam Khan realized that Akbar wanted to take power into his own hands, he was prepared to submit, but his opponents were keen to ruin him. They heaped humiliation upon him until he was goaded to rebel. The rebellion distracted the empire for almost six months. Finally, Bairam Khan was forced to submit. Akbar received him cordially and gave him the option of serving at the court or anywhere outside it, or retiring to Mecca. Bairam Khan chose to go to Mecca. However, on his way, he was assassinated at Patan near Ahmedabad by an Afghan who bore him a personal grudge. Bairam’s wife and young child were brought to Akbar at Agra. Akbar married Bairam Khan’s young wife, who was his cousin. He brought up Bairam’s child as his own son. This child later became famous as Abdur Rahim Khan-i-Khanan and held some of the most important offices and commands in the empire.

        Akbar’s confrontation with Bairam Khan and the treatment accorded to his family subsequently show some typical traits of Akbar’s character. He was unrelenting once he had made up his mind about a course of action, but was prepared to go out of his way in being generous to an opponent who had submitted to him.

        During Bairam Khan’s rebellion, groups and individuals in the nobility had become politically active. They included Akbar’s foster mother, Maham Anaga, and her relations. Though Maham Anaga soon withdrew from politics, her son, Adham Khan, was an impetuous young man who assumed independent airs when sent to command an expedition against Malwa. Removed from command, he laid claim to the post of the wazir, and when this was not conceded, he stabbed the acting wazir in his office. Akbar was enraged and had him thrown down to his death from the parapet of the fort (1561).

        However, it was many years before Akbar was to establish his authority fully. The Uzbeks formed a powerful group in the nobility. They held important positions in eastern Uttar Pradesh, Bihar, and Malwa. Although they had served the empire well by subduing the powerful Afghan groups in those areas, they had become arrogant and were defying the young ruler. Between 1561 and 1567, they broke out in rebellion several times, forcing Akbar to take the field against them. Each time Akbar was induced to pardon them. When they again rebelled in 1565, Akbar was so exasperated that he vowed to make Jaunpur his capital until he had rooted them out. Meanwhile, a rebellion by the Mirzas, who were Timurids and related to Akbar by marriage, threw the areas west of modern Uttar Pradesh into confusion. Encouraged by these rebellions, Akbar’s half-brother, Mirza Hakim, who had seized control of Kabul, advanced into Punjab and besieged Lahore. The Uzbek rebel nobles formally proclaimed him their ruler.

        This was the most serious crisis Akbar had to face since Hemu’s capture of Delhi. However, Akbar’s grit and a certain amount of luck enabled him to triumph. From Jaunpur, he marched to Lahore, forcing Mirza Hakim to retire. Meanwhile, the rebellion of the Mirzas was crushed, with the Mirzas fleeing to Malwa and then to Gujarat. Akbar marched back from Lahore to Jaunpur. Crossing the river Yamuna near Allahabad at the height of the rainy season, he surprised the rebels led by the Uzbek nobles and completely routed them (1567). The Uzbek leaders were killed in the battle, thus bringing their protracted rebellion to an end. All the rebellious nobles, including those among them who had been dreaming of independence, were cowed down. Akbar was now free to concentrate on the expansion of the empire.

    Early Expansion of the Empire (1560-76)
        During Bairam Khan’s regency, the territories of the Mughal empire expanded rapidly. Apart from Ajmer, an important conquest during this period was that of Malwa. Malwa was ruled at that time by a young prince, Baz Bahadur. His accomplishments included a mastery of music and poetry. Stories about the romance of Baz Bahadur and Rupmati, who was famous for her beauty as well as for music and poetry, are well known. During his time, Mandu had become a celebrated center for music. However, the army had been neglected by Baz Bahadur. The expedition against Malwa was led by Adham Khan, the son of Akbar’s foster mother, Maham Anga. Baz Bahadur was badly defeated in 1561, and the Mughals took valuable spoils, including Rupmati. However, she preferred to commit suicide rather than be dragged to Adham Khan’s harem. Due to the senseless cruelties of Adham Khan and his successor, there was a reaction against the Mughals, which enabled Baz Bahadur to recover Malwa.

        After dealing with Bairam Khan’s rebellion, Akbar sent another expedition to Malwa. Baz Bahadur had to flee, and for some time, he took shelter with the Rana of Mewar. After wandering about from one area to another, he finally repaired to Akbar’s court and was enrolled as a Mughal mansabdar. The extensive country of Malwa thus came under Mughal rule.

        At about the same time, Mughal arms overran the kingdom of Garh-Katanga. The kingdom of Garh-Katanga included the Narmada valley and the northern portions of present-day Madhya Pradesh. It had been welded together by Aman Das, who flourished in the second half of the fifteenth century. Aman Das had helped Bahadur Shah of Gujarat in the conquest of Raisen and had received from him the title of Sangram Shah.

        The kingdom of Garh-Katanga included a number of Gond and Rajput principalities. It was the most powerful kingdom established by the Gonds.
