But he soon came back and enrolled himself in the powerful Habshi (Abyssinian) party, which was opposed to Chand Bibi.

        After the fall of Ahmadnagar, Malik Ambar found a Nizam Shahi prince and, with the tacit support of the ruler of Bijapur, set him up as Murtaza Nizam Shah II, with himself as the Peshwa—a title that had been common in Ahmadnagar much earlier. Malik Ambar gathered around him a large band of Maratha troopers or bargis. The Marathas were adept at rapid movements, plundering, and cutting off the supplies of enemy troops. Although this guerrilla mode of warfare was traditional with the Marathas in the Deccan, the Mughals were not used to it. With the help of the Marathas, Ambar made it difficult for the Mughals to consolidate their position in Berar, Ahmadnagar, and Balaghat.

        The Mughal commander in the Deccan at the time was Abdur Rahim Khan-i-Khanan, a shrewd and wily politician as well as an able soldier. He inflicted a crushing defeat on Ambar in 1601 in Telangana at a place called Nander. However, he decided to make friends with Ambar, considering it desirable that there should be some stability in the remaining Nizam Shahi kingdom. In turn, Ambar also found it useful to cultivate the friendship of Khan-i-Khanan, as it enabled him to deal with his internal rivals.

        However, after the death of Akbar, when the position of the Mughals in the Deccan became weak due to differences among the Mughal commanders, Ambar unleashed a fierce campaign to expel the Mughals from Berar, Balaghat, and Ahmadnagar. In this enterprise, he was helped by Ibrahim Adil Shah, the ruler of Bijapur, who considered it necessary that the Nizam Shahi state should continue as a buffer between Bijapur and the Mughals. He gave Ambar the powerful fort of Qandahar in Telangana for the residence of his family and for storing treasures, provisions, etc. He also lent him 10,000 horsemen, for whose support a definite tract of territory was set apart.

        The treaty was cemented by a marriage alliance between the daughter of one of the leading Ethiopian nobles of Bijapur and Malik Ambar’s son. The marriage was celebrated in 1609 with great rejoicing. Adil Shah gave a handsome dowry to the bride and spent about Rs. 80,000 on fireworks alone.

        Fortified with the support of Bijapur and with the active aid of the Marathas, Ambar soon forced Khan-i-Khanan to retreat to Burhanpur. Thus, by 1619, all the gains made by Akbar in the Deccan were lost. Although Jahangir sent Prince Parvez to the Deccan with a large army, he could not meet the challenge posed by Malik Ambar. Even Ahmadnagar was lost, and Parvez had to conclude a disgraceful peace with Ambar.

        The affairs of Malik Ambar continued to prosper, and the Mughals were not able to reassert themselves as long as he had the solid support of the Marathas and other elements in the Deccan. However, in time, Malik Ambar became arrogant and alienated his allies. The Khan-i-Khanan, who had again been posted as the Mughal viceroy of the Deccan, took advantage of the situation and won over several Habshi and Maratha nobles, such as Jagdev Rai, Babaji Kate, and Udaji Ram.

        Jahangir himself was well aware of the value of the Marathas, for he observed in his memoirs that the Marathas “are a hardy lot and are the center of resistance in that country.” With the help of the Maratha sardars, Khan-i-Khanan inflicted a crushing defeat on the combined forces of Ahmadnagar, Bijapur, and Golconda in 1616. The Mughals occupied the new Nizam Shahi capital, Khirki, and burned all its buildings before they left. This defeat shook the Deccani alliance against the Mughals. However, Ambar continued his resistance.

        To carry forward Khan-i-Khanan’s victory, Jahangir sent a grand army under his son, Prince Khurram (later Shah Jahan), and himself moved to Mandu to support the prince (1618). Faced with this threat, Ambar had no option but to submit. However, it is significant that in the treaty, Jahangir did not try to enlarge the conquests made by Akbar in the Deccan. This was not due to any military weakness on the part of Jahangir, as has sometimes been imagined, but due to deliberate policy. Apparently, Jahangir did not want to extend Mughal commitments in the Deccan or become too deeply embroiled in its affairs. Moreover, he was still hopeful that his moderation would enable the Deccani states to settle down and live in peace with the Mughals. As part of this policy, Jahangir tried to win over Bijapur to his side and sent a gracious farman to Adil Shah, calling him “son.”

        Despite these reverses, Ambar continued to lead the Deccani struggle against the Mughals, and there was no peace in the Deccan. However, two years later, the combined Deccani forces again suffered a severe defeat at the hands of the Mughals. Ambar had to restore all the Mughal territories and another 14 kos of adjoining territory. The Deccani states had to pay an indemnity of Rs. 5,00,000. The credit for these victories was given to Prince Khurram.

        The united front of the Deccani states against the Mughals soon crumbled. Old rivalries among the Deccani states resurfaced. Ambar conducted a series of campaigns against Bijapur for the recovery of Sholapur, which was a bone of contention between the two states. Through a rapid movement, Ambar reached the Bijapur capital, burnt the new capital of Nauraspur built by Ibrahim Adil Shah, and forced him to seek shelter in a fort. This might be regarded as the climax of Ambar’s power.

        Although Ambar showed remarkable military skill, energy, and determination, his achievements were short-lived due to his inability or unwillingness to come to terms with the Mughals. The chief significance of the rise of Ambar, however, was that it represented a clear recognition of the importance of the Marathas in Deccani affairs. The success of the Marathas under the leadership of Malik Ambar gave them confidence, enabling them to play an independent role later on.

        Malik Ambar tried to improve the administration of the Nizam Shahi state by introducing Todar Mal’s system of land revenue. He abolished the old system of giving land on contract (ijara), which was ruinous for the peasants, and adopted the zabti system.

        After 1622, when the Deccan was in turmoil due to the rebellion of Prince Khurram against Jahangir, Malik Ambar was able to recover many of the old territories that had been ceded to the Mughals. Jahangir’s attempt to consolidate the Mughal position in the Deccan was thus frustrated. However, the long-term benefits of reopening the dispute with the Mughals were doubtful. It led to a situation where Shah Jahan decided that he had no alternative but to extinguish Ahmadnagar as an independent state. Malik Ambar breathed his last in 1626 at the ripe age of 80. But the bitter fruits of his legacy had to be reaped by his successors.

    EXTINCTION OF AHMADNAGAR AND ACCEPTANCE OF MUGHAL SUZERAINTY BY BIJAPUR AND GOLCONDA
        Shah Jahan ascended the throne in 1628. Having commanded two expeditions to the Deccan as a prince and spent a considerable period in the Deccan during his rebellion against his father, Shah Jahan had extensive experience and personal knowledge of the Deccan and its politics.

        Shah Jahan’s first concern as a ruler was to recover the territories in the Deccan that had been lost to the Nizam Shahi ruler. For this purpose, he deputed an old and experienced noble, Khan-i-Jahan Lodi. However, Khan-i-Jahan Lodi failed in his mission and was recalled to the court. Shortly afterward, he rebelled and joined the Nizam Shah, who deputed him to expel the Mughals from the remaining portions of Berar and Balaghat. Granting asylum to a leading Mughal noble in this manner was a challenge that Shah Jahan could not ignore. It was clear that even after Malik Ambar’s death, his policy of refusing to recognize the Mughal position in Berar and Balaghat was being continued by the Nizam Shahi ruler.

        Shah Jahan, therefore, concluded that there could be no peace for the Mughals in the Deccan as long as Ahmadnagar remained an independent state. This was a major departure from the policy followed by Akbar and Jahangir. However, Shah Jahan was not keen on extending Mughal territories in the Deccan beyond what was absolutely necessary. He, therefore, wrote to the Bijapur ruler, offering to cede to him roughly one-third of the Ahmadnagar state if he cooperated with the Mughals in the projected campaign against Ahmadnagar. This was a shrewd move by Shah Jahan to isolate Ahmadnagar diplomatically and militarily. He also sent feelers to various Maratha sardars to join Mughal service.

        At first, Shah Jahan was successful in his overtures. Malik Ambar had defeated and killed some prominent Bijapuri nobles during his campaigns. The Adil Shah was also still smarting from the humiliation of the burning of Nauraspur and the annexation of Sholapur by Malik Ambar. He, therefore, accepted Shah Jahan’s proposal and posted an army at the Nizam Shahi border to cooperate with the Mughals.

        Around this time, Jadhav Rao, a prominent Maratha noble who had defected to the Mughals during Jahangir’s reign but later returned to the service of the Nizam Shah, was treacherously murdered on a charge of conspiring with the Mughals. As a result, Shahji Bhonsale, his son-in-law (and the father of Shivaji), defected to the Mughal side along with his relatives. Shah Jahan accorded him a mansab of 5000 and granted him jagirs in the Poona region. Several other prominent Maratha sardars also joined Shah Jahan.

        In 1629, Shah Jahan deputed large armies against Ahmadnagar—one to operate in the west in the Balaghat region and the other in the east in the Telangana region. The emperor himself moved to Burhanpur to coordinate their movements. Under relentless pressure, large parts of the Ahmadnagar state were brought under Mughal occupation. Parenda, one of the last outposts of the kingdom, was besieged. The Nizam Shah now sent a pitiful appeal to the Adil Shah, stating that most of the kingdom was under Mughal occupation and that if Parenda fell, it would mean the end of the Nizam Shahi dynasty—after which, he warned, it would be Bijapur’s turn.

        A strong group in the Bijapur court had been uneasy about the steady Mughal advance in Ahmadnagar. In fact, the Bijapuri forces at the border had merely observed the situation without taking an active part in the Mughal operations. The Mughals, on their part, had refused to hand over to the Adil Shah the areas allotted to him under the agreement. As a result, the Adil Shah made a sudden reversal in policy and decided to help the Nizam Shah, who agreed to return Sholapur to him. This shift in the political situation forced the Mughals to raise the siege of Parenda and retreat.

        However, the internal situation in Ahmadnagar soon turned in favor of the Mughals. Fath Khan, the son of Malik Ambar, had recently been appointed Peshwa by the Nizam Shah in the hope that he could induce Shah Jahan to make peace. Instead, Fath Khan opened secret negotiations with Shah Jahan and, at his instance, murdered the Nizam Shah and placed a puppet ruler on the throne. He also read the khutba and struck the sikka in the name of the Mughal emperor. As a reward, Fath Khan was taken into Mughal service, and the jagir around Poona, which had previously been allotted to Shahji, was transferred to him. As a result, Shahji defected from the Mughal side. These events took place in 1632.

        After Fath Khan’s surrender, Shah Jahan appointed Mahabat Khan as Mughal viceroy of the Deccan and returned to Agra. Mahabat Khan, faced with the combined opposition of Bijapur and the local Nizam Shahi nobles, including Shahji, found himself in a very difficult situation. Parenda surrendered to Bijapur. The Adil Shah made a strong bid for the fort of Daulatabad, offering a large sum of money to Fath Khan for surrendering the fort. Elsewhere as well, the Mughals struggled to hold their positions.

        It thus became clear that the Mughals and Bijapur were engaged in a contest to divide the prostrate body of Ahmadnagar between them. The Adil Shah sent a large army under Randaula Khan and Murari Pandit for the surrender of Daulatabad and for provisioning its garrison. Shahji also joined Bijapur’s service to harass the Mughals and cut off their supplies. However, their combined efforts were unsuccessful. Mahabat Khan closely besieged Daulatabad and forced the garrison to surrender in 1633. The Nizam Shah was imprisoned in Gwalior, marking the end of the Nizam Shahi dynasty.

        However, even this did not resolve the problems facing the Mughals. Following Malik Ambar’s example, Shahji found a Nizam Shahi prince and raised him as a ruler. The Adil Shah sent a force of 7,000–8,000 horsemen to aid Shahji and persuaded many Nizam Shahi nobles to surrender their forts to him. Many disbanded Nizam Shahi soldiers joined Shahji, whose force swelled to 20,000 horses. With these, he harassed the Mughals and took control of large portions of the Ahmadnagar state.

        Shah Jahan now decided to give personal attention to the problems of the Deccan. He realized that the crux of the issue was Bijapur’s attitude. He, therefore, deputed a large army to invade Bijapur and also sent feelers to the Adil Shah, offering to revive the earlier accord of dividing Ahmadnagar’s territory between Bijapur and the Mughals.

        The policy of using both force and diplomacy, combined with Shah Jahan’s advance to the Deccan, brought about another change in Bijapur’s politics. The leaders of the anti-Mughal faction, including Murari Pandit, were removed and killed. A new treaty (ahdnama) was signed with Shah Jahan. Under this treaty, the Adil Shah agreed to recognize Mughal suzerainty, pay an indemnity of twenty lakh rupees, and refrain from interfering in Golconda’s affairs, which was brought under Mughal protection. Any future disputes between Bijapur and Golconda were to be arbitrated by the Mughal emperor.

        Shah Jahan completed the Deccan settlement by entering into a treaty with Golconda as well. The Qutb Shah agreed to include Shah Jahan’s name in the khutba and to exclude the Iranian emperor’s name. The annual tribute of four lakh huns previously paid to Bijapur was remitted. Instead, Golconda was required to pay two lakh huns annually to the Mughal emperor in return for his protection.

        The treaties of 1636 with Bijapur and Golconda were statesmanlike. In effect, they enabled Shah Jahan to achieve Akbar’s ultimate objectives. Mughal suzerainty was now accepted across the region, allowing the Deccani states to expand southward.

    In the decade following the treaties of 1636, Bijapur and Golconda overran the rich and fertile Karnataka area, from the river Krishna to Tanjore and beyond. This area was ruled by a number of petty principalities. Many of them, such as the Nayaks of Tanjore, Jinji, and Madurai, owed nominal allegiance to the Rayal, the former ruler of Vijayanagar. A series of campaigns were conducted by Bijapur and Golconda against these states. With the help of Shah Jahan, they agreed to divide the territories and the spoils gained by their armies in the proportion of two-thirds to Bijapur and one-third to Golconda.

    Despite frequent quarrels between the two, the task of conquest progressed. Thus, in a short span of time, the territories of these two states more than doubled, and they reached the climax of their power and prosperity. If the rulers had been able to consolidate their hold over the areas they had conquered, the Deccan would have seen a long era of peace. However, rapid expansion weakened whatever internal cohesion these states had.

    Ambitious nobles such as Shahji and his son Shivaji in Bijapur, and Mir Jumla, the leading noble in Golconda, started carving out spheres of influence for themselves. The Mughals, too, found that the balance of power in the Deccan had been upset and demanded a price for their benevolent neutrality during the expansionist phase of these states.

    These developments came to a head in 1656 following the death of Muhammad Adil Shah and the arrival of Aurangzeb as the Mughal viceroy of the Deccan. These events will be dealt with in a subsequent chapter.

    CULTURAL CONTRIBUTION OF THE DECCAN STATES
        The Deccani states made significant cultural contributions. Ali Adil Shah (d. 1580) loved to hold discussions with Hindu and Muslim saints and was called a Sufi. He invited Catholic missionaries to his court even before Akbar had done so. He had an excellent library and appointed the well-known Sanskrit scholar, Waman Pandit, as its curator. The patronage of Sanskrit and Marathi was continued by his successors.

        The successor of Ali Adil Shah, Ibrahim Adil Shah II (1580–1627), ascended the throne at the age of nine. He was deeply concerned for the poor and earned the title ‘Aba Baba’ or ‘Friend of the Poor.’ He had a keen interest in music and composed a book called Kitab-i-Nauras, in which songs were set to various musical modes or ragas.

        He built a new capital, Nauraspur, where he invited a large number of musicians to settle. In his songs, he frequently invoked the goddess of music and learning, Saraswati. Due to his broad approach, he was called ‘Jagat Guru’ (World Teacher). He extended patronage to all, including Hindu saints and temples. This included grants to Pandharpur, the center of the worship of Vithoba, which became an important hub of the Bhakti movement in Maharashtra. The broad and tolerant policies of Ibrahim Adil Shah II were continued by his successors.

        The important role played by Maratha families in the service of the Ahmadnagar state has already been mentioned. The Qutb Shahs also employed both Hindus and Muslims in military, administrative, and diplomatic roles. Under Ibrahim Qutb Shah (d. 1580), Murari Rao rose to the position of Peshwa, second only to Mir Jumla, the wazir (prime minister). The Nayakwaris, who formed a military-cum-landed elite, remained powerful in the kingdom ever since the dynasty’s foundation. From 1672 until Golconda’s absorption into the Mughal Empire in 1687, the state’s administrative and military affairs were dominated by the brothers Madanna and Akkanna.

        Golconda became a major intellectual center, attracting many literary figures. Sultan Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah, a contemporary of Akbar, was an avid patron of literature and architecture. He was not only a great patron of art and literature but also a poet of high caliber. He wrote in Dakhini Urdu, Persian, and Telugu and left behind an extensive diwan (collection of poetry). He was the first to introduce a secular note in poetry. Apart from praising God and the Prophet, he wrote about nature, love, and the social life of his time.

        The growth of Urdu in its Dakhini form was a significant development of this period. The successors of Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah and many other poets and writers adopted Urdu as a literary language. In addition to Persian, these writers borrowed elements from Hindi and Telugu, incorporating forms, idioms, themes, and vocabulary. Urdu was also patronized at the Bijapur court, where the poet laureate Nusrati, who flourished in the mid-seventeenth century, wrote a romantic tale about Prince Manohar of Kanak Nagar and Madhu Malati. From the Deccan, Urdu spread to North India in the eighteenth century.

    Architecture and Art
        In the field of architecture, Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah constructed many buildings, the most famous being the Char Minar. Completed in 1591–92, it stood at the center of the newly founded city of Hyderabad. The structure features four lofty arches facing different directions, with four four-story minarets rising to a height of 48 meters. Its double-screened arches have intricate carvings.

        The rulers of Bijapur maintained a high standard and impeccable taste in architecture. The most famous Bijapuri buildings of this period are:
            •	Ibrahim Rauza – A mausoleum built for Ibrahim Adil Shah, showcasing Deccani architecture at its finest.
            •	Gol Gumbaz – Built in 1660, it features the largest single dome ever constructed. The structure is well-balanced, with a massive dome complemented by tall, tapering minarets at the corners. It is said that a whisper on one side of the huge main hall can be heard clearly at the other end.

        Painting also flourished in the Deccan, reaching its peak during the reign of Ibrahim Adil Shah II (1580–1627).

        Thus, the Deccani states overcame the sectarian violence of the sixteenth century and maintained high standards of communal harmony. They made notable contributions in the fields of music, literature, architecture, and painting.


- India in the First Half of the Seventeenth Century

    Political and Administrative Developments in India
        The first half of the seventeenth century in India was, on the whole, an era of progress and growth. During this period, the Mughal Empire was ruled by two capable rulers—Jahangir (1605–1627) and Shah Jahan (1628–1658). In southern India, too, as we have seen, the states of Bijapur and Golconda provided stability, peace, and cultural growth. The Mughal rulers consolidated the administrative system that had developed under Akbar. They maintained alliances with the Rajputs and sought to further broaden the political base of the empire by forming ties with powerful groups such as the Afghans and the Marathas. They adorned their capitals with beautiful buildings, many constructed in marble, and sought to make the Mughal court a center of cultural life in the country.

        The Mughals played a crucial role in stabilizing India’s relations with neighboring Asian powers such as Iran, the Uzbeks, and the Ottoman Turks, thereby opening up greater avenues for India’s foreign trade. Trade concessions granted to various European trading companies were also aimed at promoting foreign trade. However, several negative aspects emerged during this period. The growing prosperity of the ruling class did not filter down to peasants and workers, whose lives remained hard and miserable. The Mughal ruling elite remained largely oblivious to the advancements in science and technology occurring in the West. The problem of succession to the throne created instability, posing a threat to both the political system and economic and cultural growth.

        Jahangir, the eldest son of Akbar, succeeded to the throne without difficulty, as his younger brothers had died during Akbar’s lifetime due to excessive drinking. However, shortly after Jahangir’s accession, his eldest son, Khusrau, rebelled against him. A struggle between father and son for the throne was not unusual in those times—Jahangir himself had rebelled against Akbar, disturbing the empire for some time. Khusrau’s rebellion, however, was short-lived. Jahangir defeated him in a battle near Lahore, and he was soon captured and imprisoned.

        We have already seen how Jahangir brought an end to the conflict with Mewar, which had lasted for four decades, and the struggle in the Deccan against Malik Ambar, who refused to accept the settlement made by Akbar. In the east, conflict persisted. Although Akbar had weakened the Afghan power in Bengal, Afghan chiefs still controlled various regions. They had the support of several Hindu rulers, such as the rajas of Jessore, Kamrup (western Assam), and Cachar. Towards the end of his reign, Akbar had recalled Raja Man Singh, the governor of Bengal, to the court. During his absence, the Afghan chief Usman Khan and others took the opportunity to rebel. Jahangir initially sent Man Singh back to Bengal, but the situation continued to worsen.

        In 1608, Jahangir appointed Islam Khan, the grandson of the Sufi saint Shaikh Salim Chishti, as the governor of Bengal. Though young, Islam Khan handled the situation with great energy and foresight. He won over many local zamindars, including the Raja of Jessore, and established his headquarters at Dacca, which was strategically located for dealing with the rebels. To maintain control over the region, the provincial capital was soon transferred from Rajmahal to Dacca, which began to develop rapidly. Islam Khan first targeted Sonargaon, which was under the control of Musa Khan and his confederates, known as the Barah Bhuiyan (Twelve Zamindars). After three years of campaigning, Sonargaon was captured, and Musa Khan surrendered. He was sent to the Mughal court as a prisoner. Next, Islam Khan turned his attention to Usman Khan, who was defeated in a fierce battle. With the fall of Usman Khan, the Afghan resistance was crushed, and the remaining rebels soon surrendered. The principalities of Jessore and Kamrup were annexed, firmly establishing Mughal control over eastern Bengal.

        Like Akbar, Jahangir understood that lasting conquests could not be sustained by force alone but required the goodwill of the people. He treated the defeated Afghan chiefs and their followers with consideration and sympathy. Many detained princes and zamindars of Bengal were released and allowed to return to their territories. Even Musa Khan was eventually freed, and his estates were restored. As a result, after a long period of conflict, peace and prosperity returned to Bengal. More Afghans were also inducted into the Mughal nobility, further integrating them into the empire. The leading Afghan noble under Jahangir was Khan-i-Jahan Lodi, who rendered distinguished service in the Deccan.

        By 1622, Jahangir had subdued Malik Ambar, ended the prolonged conflict with Mewar, and pacified Bengal. At the age of 51, he seemed poised to enjoy a long period of peace and stability. However, two critical developments changed the course of events: the Persian conquest of Qandhar, which was a major blow to Mughal prestige, and Jahangir’s failing health, which triggered a succession struggle among his sons and led to intense power struggles among the nobles. These developments thrust Nur Jahan, Jahangir’s influential wife, into the political arena, where she played a key role in court politics during the final years of his reign.

    Nur Jahan
        The story of Nur Jahan’s life, her first marriage with an Iranian, Sher Afgan, and his death in a clash with the Mughal governor of Bengal, as well as her stay in Agra with an elderly relation of Jahangir and her marriage to Jahangir four years later (1611), are well known. Sober historians do not believe that Jahangir was responsible for the death of her first husband. Jahangir’s chance meeting with her in the Meena Bazar and subsequent marriage was not unusual. Her family was respectable, and her father, Itimad-ud-Daula, had been made joint diwan by Jahangir in the first year of his reign. After a brief eclipse due to the involvement of one of his sons in Khusrau’s rebellion, he was restored to his position. Having proved his capability in this office, and following Nur Jahan’s marriage to Jahangir, he was elevated to the post of chief diwan. Other members of the family also benefited from this alliance, with their mansabs being increased. Itimad-ud-Daula proved to be competent and loyal, wielding considerable influence in state affairs until his death ten years later. Nur Jahan’s brother, Asaf Khan, was also a learned and able man. He was appointed as khan-i-saman, a post reserved for nobles in whom the emperor had full confidence. He married his daughter to Khurram (Shah Jahan), who became his father’s favorite following Khusrau’s rebellion and imprisonment.

        Some modern historians believe that Nur Jahan, along with her father and brother, and in alliance with Khurram, formed a group or “junta” that managed Jahangir, making it impossible for anyone to advance in their career without their support. This allegedly led to a division of the court into two factions—the Nur Jahan junta and its opponents. It is further argued that Nur Jahan’s political ambitions ultimately led to a rift between her and Shah Jahan, driving him to rebel against his father in 1622, as he believed that Jahangir was entirely under Nur Jahan’s influence. However, other historians dispute this view. They argue that until 1622, when Jahangir’s health declined, all significant political decisions were taken by Jahangir himself, as evidenced by his autobiography. The precise political role of Nur Jahan during this period remains unclear. Coins were issued in her name, and she was given the title of Badshah Begum. Important nobles used to call on her to apprise her of events and seek her intervention with the emperor. She dominated the royal household and introduced new fashions inspired by Persian traditions. Due to her influence, Persian art and culture gained great prestige at court. Nur Jahan was Jahangir’s constant companion, even accompanying him on hunting expeditions, as she was an excellent rider and a skilled shot. Given her position, many people sought her intervention with the emperor. Under Mughal rule, no woman had previously attained such an important position. However, Jahangir was not entirely dependent on the junta or on Nur Jahan, as evidenced by the continued promotions of nobles outside the junta’s influence. The rise of Shah Jahan was due to his personal qualities and achievements rather than Nur Jahan’s backing. Shah Jahan had his own ambitions, of which Jahangir was well aware. In any case, no ruler could afford to allow a noble or a prince to become too powerful, as this would challenge his authority. This was the fundamental reason for the conflict between Jahangir and Shah Jahan.

    Shah Jahan’s Rebellion
        The immediate cause of the rebellion was Shah Jahan’s refusal to proceed to Qandhar, which had been besieged by the Persians. Shah Jahan feared that the campaign would be long and arduous and that intrigues would be plotted against him in his absence from court. Hence, he made several demands, including full command of the army, which comprised veterans from the Deccan, complete control over Punjab, and authority over several important forts. Jahangir was enraged by this defiant stance. Convinced that the prince was contemplating rebellion, he wrote harsh letters and took punitive measures, further aggravating the situation and leading to an open breach. From Mandu, where he was stationed, Shah Jahan made a sudden dash toward Agra in an attempt to capture the treasures stored there. Shah Jahan had the full support of the Deccan army and all the nobles stationed there. Gujarat and Malwa had declared their loyalty to him, and he had the backing of his father-in-law, Asaf Khan, along with several important nobles at court. However, in a battle near Delhi, Shah Jahan was defeated by forces led by Mahabat Khan. He was saved from total defeat by the valorous stand of the Mewar contingent. Another army was dispatched to wrest Gujarat from his control, and Shah Jahan was eventually forced out of Mughal territories. He sought refuge with his former enemies, the Deccan rulers, but later crossed into Orissa, took the governor by surprise, and soon brought Bengal and Bihar under his control.

        Mahabat Khan was again sent to subdue Shah Jahan. He took decisive measures, forcing Shah Jahan to retreat to the Deccan. This time, Shah Jahan allied with Malik Ambar, who was once again at war with the Mughals. However, soon afterward, Shah Jahan wrote letters of apology to Jahangir. Jahangir, recognizing the need for reconciliation, decided to pardon his ablest and most energetic son. As part of the settlement, two of Shah Jahan’s sons, Dara and Aurangzeb, were sent to the Mughal court as hostages, and a tract in the Deccan was assigned to Shah Jahan for his expenses. This agreement was finalized in 1626.

    The Aftermath of Shah Jahan’s Rebellion
        Shah Jahan’s rebellion distracted the empire for four years, resulting in the loss of Qandhar and encouraging the Deccanis to recover territories previously surrendered to the Mughals during Akbar’s reign. The rebellion also exposed a fundamental flaw in the Mughal system—a successful prince often became a rival power center, especially when it was believed that the reigning monarch was unable or unwilling to exercise supreme authority. Shah Jahan repeatedly claimed that with Jahangir’s failing health, all effective power had slipped into the hands of Nur Jahan Begum, though this claim is debatable since Asaf Khan, his father-in-law, was the imperial diwan. Moreover, though physically weak, Jahangir remained mentally sharp, and no major decision was taken without his consent.

        Jahangir’s illness also heightened the risk of ambitious nobles attempting to seize power. This fear materialized when Mahabat Khan, who had played a significant role in quelling Shah Jahan’s rebellion, felt sidelined at court. Summoned to provide accounts, he arrived with a trusted group of Rajputs and seized the emperor at a crucial moment while the royal camp was crossing the river Jhelum on its way to Kabul. Nur Jahan, who had not been captured, managed to escape across the river but failed in her initial attempts to rescue Jahangir. Determined, she later surrendered herself to Mahabat Khan to remain close to her husband. Within six months, exploiting Mahabat Khan’s mistakes and the growing resentment of his Rajput soldiers, she successfully turned most nobles against him. Realizing his precarious position, Mahabat Khan abandoned Jahangir and fled from court. He later joined Shah Jahan in the Deccan, where the prince was waiting for another opportunity.

        Nur Jahan’s defeat of Mahabat Khan was one of her greatest victories, achieved through her resilience and political acumen. However, her triumph was short-lived, as within a year, Jahangir passed away near Lahore in 1627. The shrewd Asaf Khan, who had been carefully preparing for Shah Jahan’s succession, swiftly took control. Supported by the diwan, the chief nobles, and the army, he placed Nur Jahan under virtual house arrest and summoned Shah Jahan. Upon reaching Agra, Shah Jahan was enthroned with great celebration. To secure his position, he ordered the execution of his imprisoned brother, cousins, and other rivals. This precedent, along with earlier instances of sons rebelling against their fathers, set a dangerous trend for the Mughal dynasty. Shah Jahan himself would later suffer the consequences of the very actions he had initiated.

        After Shah Jahan’s accession, Nur Jahan was granted a settlement and lived in retirement until her death eighteen years later. She was buried in Lahore. Shah Jahan’s reign (1628–1658) was marked by extensive administrative, military, and cultural activities. Having examined his Deccan policy, we now turn to the foreign policy of the Mughals, which reached its zenith under his rule.

    FOREIGN POLICY OF THE MUGHALS
        We have seen how, following the breakup of the Timurid Empire in the second half of the fifteenth century, three powerful empires—the Uzbek, the Safavid, and the Ottoman—established themselves in Transoxiana (Central Asia), Iran, and Turkey. The Uzbeks were the natural enemies of the Mughals, having been responsible for the expulsion of Babur and other Timurid princes from Samarkand and the adjoining area, including Khurasan. At the same time, the Uzbeks clashed with the rising power of the Safavids, who claimed Khurasan. The Khurasanian plateau linked Iran with Central Asia, and the trade routes to China and India passed across it. It was natural for the Safavids and the Mughals to ally against the Uzbek threat, especially as there were no significant frontier disputes between them, except for Qandhar. The Uzbeks attempted to exploit sectarian differences with the Safavid rulers of Iran, who had ruthlessly persecuted the Sunnis. Both the Uzbek and the Mughal rulers were Sunnis. However, the Mughals were too broadminded to be swayed by sectarian differences. Annoyed by the Mughal alliance with a Shia power, namely Iran, the Uzbeks occasionally incited the fanatic Afghan and Baluchi tribesmen living in the northwest frontier tracts between Peshawar and Kabul against the Mughals.

        The most powerful empire in West Asia at the time was that of the Ottoman Turks. The Ottoman, or Usmanli, Turks—so named after their first ruler, Usman (d. 1326)—had overrun Asia Minor and Eastern Europe and had also conquered Syria, Egypt, and Arabia by 1529. They had received the title “Sultan of Rum” from the shadowy caliph residing in Cairo. Later, they also assumed the title of Padshah-i-Islam.

        The rise of a Shiite power in Iran made the Ottoman Sultans conscious of the danger to their eastern flank, fearing that the expansion of the Safavids would encourage Shiism in their own territories. In 1514, the Turkish Sultan defeated the Shah of Iran in a famous battle. The Ottomans clashed with Iran over the control of Baghdad and also for the northern Iranian territories around Erivan. They gradually extended their influence over the coastal regions around Arabia and attempted to oust the Portuguese from the Persian Gulf and the Indian Ocean.

        The Ottoman threat from the west made the Persians eager to befriend the Mughals, particularly when they faced an aggressive Uzbek power in the east. The Mughals refused the Uzbek proposals for a tripartite Ottoman-Mughal-Uzbek alliance against the Persians, as it would have upset the Asian balance of power and left them vulnerable to the might of the Uzbeks. An alliance with Iran was also beneficial in promoting trade with Central Asia. Had the Mughals possessed a stronger navy, they might have sought a closer alliance with Turkey, which was also a naval power engaged in struggles against the European navies in the Mediterranean. However, the Mughals were wary of a closer relationship with Turkey, as they were unwilling to acknowledge the superiority claimed by the Turkish Sultan as the successor to the caliph. These were some of the factors that shaped the foreign policy of the Mughals.

    Akbar and the Uzbeks
        In 1510, following the defeat of the Uzbek chief Shaibani Khan by the Safavids, Babur briefly regained control of Samarkand. However, Babur had to abandon the city after the Uzbeks inflicted a decisive defeat on the Persians. Nevertheless, the assistance extended to him by the Persian monarch established a tradition of friendship between the Mughals and the Safavids. Later, Humayun also received help from the Safavid monarch, Shah Tahmasp, when he sought refuge at his court after being ousted from India by Sher Shah.

        The territorial power of the Uzbeks grew rapidly in the 1570s under Abdullah Khan Uzbek. In 1572–73, Abdullah Khan seized Balkh, which, along with Badakhshan, had served as a buffer between the Mughals and the Uzbeks. In 1577, Abdullah Khan sent an embassy to Akbar, proposing to partition Iran. After the death of Shah Tahmasp in 1576, Iran was plunged into anarchy and disorder. Abdullah Khan urged Akbar to lead an expedition from India into Iran so that they could jointly liberate Iraq, Khurasan, and Fars from the “innovators” (Shias). However, Akbar was not swayed by this sectarian appeal. A strong Iran was essential to keep the restless Uzbeks in check. At the same time, Akbar had no desire to engage in conflict with the Uzbeks unless they directly threatened Kabul or his Indian possessions. This was the key to Akbar’s foreign policy. Abdullah Khan also approached the Ottoman Sultan, proposing a tripartite alliance of Sunni powers against Iran. In response, Akbar sent an embassy to Abdullah Khan, stating that differences in law and religion were not sufficient grounds for conquest. Regarding the difficulties faced by pilgrims traveling to Mecca, Akbar pointed out that with the conquest of Gujarat, a new route for Hajj pilgrims had been established. He also emphasized the long-standing friendship with Iran and rebuked Abdullah Khan for making derogatory remarks about the Safavids.

        Akbar’s growing interest in Central Asian affairs was evident in his decision to grant refuge at his court to the Timurid prince Mirza Sulaiman, who had been ousted from Badakhshan by his grandson. Abul Fazl records that the Khyber Pass was made suitable for wheeled traffic and that, due to fear of the Mughals, the gates of Balkh were usually kept closed. To forestall an invasion of Badakhshan, Abdullah Khan instigated unrest among the northwest frontier tribes through his agent, Jalal, a religious fanatic. The situation became so serious that Akbar had to personally move to Attock. It was during these operations that he lost one of his closest friends, Raja Birbal, in a battle in the Khyber Pass.

        In 1585, Abdullah Khan suddenly conquered Badakhshan. Both Mirza Sulaiman and his grandson sought refuge at Akbar’s court and were granted suitable mansabs (official ranks). Meanwhile, with the death of his half-brother Mirza Hakim in 1585, Akbar annexed Kabul to his dominions. Thus, the Mughal and Uzbek frontiers now directly bordered each other.

        Abdullah Khan sent another embassy, which Akbar received while stationed at Attock on the Indus River. Akbar’s continued presence near the frontier unsettled Abdullah Khan. Meanwhile, the Uzbeks succeeded in capturing most of the territories they had coveted in Khurasan from Iran.

        Under these circumstances, Akbar found it best to negotiate with the Uzbek chief. He dispatched an envoy to Abdullah Khan with a letter and a verbal message. It appears that an agreement was reached, defining the Hindukush as the boundary between the two empires. This implied that the Mughals relinquished their claims over Badakhshan and Balkh—territories ruled by Timurid princes until 1585. In return, the Uzbeks refrained from laying claim to Kabul and Qandhar. Though neither side formally abandoned its territorial claims, the agreement provided the Mughals with a defensible frontier along the Hindukush. Akbar completed this objective in 1595 by acquiring Qandhar.

        From 1586 onward, Akbar remained in Lahore to monitor the situation. He returned to Agra only after the death of Abdullah Khan Uzbek in 1598. Following Abdullah’s death, the Uzbek power fragmented into warring principalities and ceased to pose a significant threat to the Mughals for a considerable period.

    Relations with Iran and the Question of Qandhar
        The dread of Uzbek power was the most potent factor that brought the Safavids and the Mughals together, despite the Uzbek attempt to raise anti-Shia sentiments against Iran and the Mughal dislike of the intolerant policies adopted by the Safavid rulers. The only trouble spot between the two was Qandhar, the possession of which was claimed by both on strategic and economic grounds, as well as considerations of sentiment and prestige. Qandhar had been a part of the Timurid Empire and had been ruled by Babur’s cousins, the rulers of Herat, until they were ousted by the Uzbeks in 1507.

        Strategically, Qandhar was vital for the defense of Kabul. The fort of Qandhar was considered one of the strongest in the region and was well supplied with water. Situated at the junction of roads leading to Kabul and Herat, Qandhar dominated southern Afghanistan and occupied a position of immense strategic importance. A modern commentator has observed:

            “The Kabul-Ghazni-Qandhar line represented a strategic and logical frontier; beyond Kabul and Khyber, there was no natural line of defense. Moreover, the possession of Qandhar made it easier to control the Afghan and Baluch tribes.”

        After the conquest of Sindh and Baluchistan by Akbar, the strategic and economic importance of Qandhar for the Mughals increased. Qandhar was a rich and fertile province and was the hub of the movement of men and goods between India and Central Asia. The trade from Central Asia to Multan via Qandhar, and from there down the Indus River to the sea, steadily gained importance because the roads across Iran were frequently disturbed due to wars and internal commotions. Akbar sought to promote trade along this route and pointed out to Abdullah Uzbek that it was an alternative route for pilgrims and goods traffic to Mecca. Taking all these factors into account, it would appear that Qandhar was not as important to the Persians as it was to the Mughals. For Iran, Qandhar was more of an outpost—an important one, no doubt—rather than a vital bastion in a defense system.

        In the early phase, however, the dispute over Qandhar did not affect good relations between the two countries. Qandhar came under Babur’s control in 1522 when the Uzbeks were threatening Khurasan once again.

        Shah Tahmasp captured Qandhar, taking advantage of the confusion following Humayun’s death. Akbar made no effort to regain it until the Uzbeks under Abdullah Uzbek posed a renewed threat to both Iran and the Mughals. The Mughal conquest of Qandhar (1595) was not part of an agreement between Akbar and the Uzbeks to partition the Persian Empire, as some modern historians have argued. Instead, it was undertaken to establish a viable defensive line in the northwest against a possible Uzbek invasion, since Khurasan had by then fallen under Uzbek control and Qandhar was cut off from Persia.

        Relations between Iran and the Mughals continued to be cordial despite the Mughal conquest of Qandhar. Shah Abbas I (ruled 1588–1625), perhaps the greatest of the Safavid rulers, was keen to maintain good relations with the Mughals. There was a regular exchange of embassies and costly gifts, including rarities, between him and Jahangir. Shah Abbas also established close diplomatic and commercial relations with the Deccani states, but this was not objected to by Jahangir. Neither side felt threatened, and there exists an imaginary portrait by a court artist showing Jahangir and Shah Abbas embracing, with a globe of the world beneath their feet. Culturally, too, the two countries grew even closer during this period, with the active involvement of Nur Jahan. However, the alliance ultimately proved more beneficial to Shah Abbas than to Jahangir, as it led the latter to neglect cultivating friendships with the Uzbek chiefs, believing himself secure in the friendship of his “brother,” Shah Abbas.

        In 1620, Shah Abbas politely requested the restoration of Qandhar and prepared for an attack. Jahangir was caught off guard, as he was diplomatically isolated and militarily unprepared. Hasty preparations for the defense of Qandhar were made, but Prince Shah Jahan put forward impossible demands before agreeing to march. As a result, Qandhar passed into Persian hands in 1622. Although Shah Abbas attempted to erase the bitterness over this loss by sending a lavish embassy to Jahangir and offering facile explanations—which Jahangir formally accepted—the cordiality that had marked Mughal-Iranian relations came to an end.

        After the death of Shah Abbas (1629), disturbances broke out in Iran. Taking advantage of this, and after resolving his Deccan affairs, Shah Jahan persuaded Ali Mardan Khan, the Persian governor of Qandhar, to defect to the Mughal side in 1638.

    SHAH JAHAN’S BALKH CAMPAIGN
        The conquest of Qandhar was only a means to an end. Shah Jahan was more concerned with the serious danger of recurrent Uzbek attacks on Kabul and their intrigues with the Baluch and Afghan tribes. At the time, both Bokhara and Balkh had come under the control of Nazr Muhammad. Nazr Muhammad and his son, Abdul Aziz, were ambitious and had launched a series of attacks with the help of Afghan tribesmen to gain control of Kabul and Ghazni.

        However, soon afterward, Abdul Aziz led a rebellion against his father, and only Balkh remained under the control of Nazr Muhammad, who appealed to Shah Jahan for help. Secure from the Persian side, Shah Jahan accepted the appeal with alacrity. He moved from Lahore to Kabul and deputed a large army under Prince Murad to assist Nazr Muhammad. The army, consisting of 50,000 cavalry and 10,000 infantry—including musketeers, rocketeers, and gunners, along with a contingent of Rajputs—left Kabul in mid-1646.

        Shah Jahan had carefully instructed Prince Murad to treat Nazr Muhammad with great consideration and to restore Balkh to him if he behaved with modesty and submission. Furthermore, if Nazr Muhammad expressed a desire to regain Samarqand and Bokhara, the prince was to assist him in every possible way. Clearly, Shah Jahan wanted a friendly ruler in Bokhara who looked to the Mughals for support.

        However, Murad’s impetuosity ruined the plan. He marched on Balkh without waiting for instructions from Nazr Muhammad and ordered his men to enter the fort where Nazr Muhammad was residing. Uncertain of the prince’s intentions, Nazr Muhammad fled. The Mughals were forced to occupy Balkh and hold it against a sullen and hostile population. Moreover, there was no viable alternative to Nazr Muhammad’s rule. Abdul Aziz, Nazr Muhammad’s son, rallied the Uzbek tribes against the Mughals in Transoxiana and mustered an army of 120,000 men across the river Oxus.

        Meanwhile, Prince Murad, who was eager to return home, was replaced by Prince Aurangzeb. The Mughals made no effort to defend the Oxus, as it was easily fordable. Instead, they stationed pickets at strategic locations and kept the main force together to respond swiftly to any threat. Abdul Aziz crossed the Oxus, but in a running battle outside the gates of Balkh (1647), the Mughals routed the Uzbeks.

        The Mughal victory at Balkh paved the way for negotiations with the Uzbeks. Abdul Aziz’s supporters gradually dispersed, and he sought reconciliation with the Mughals. Meanwhile, Nazr Muhammad, who had taken refuge in Persia, also sought Mughal assistance to regain his throne. After careful consideration, Shah Jahan decided in favor of restoring Nazr Muhammad. However, he was first required to make an apology and humble submission to Prince Aurangzeb. This proved to be a mistake, as the proud Uzbek ruler was unwilling to demean himself, especially since he knew the Mughals could not hold Balkh indefinitely.

        After waiting in vain for Nazr Muhammad to appear personally, the Mughals evacuated Balkh in October 1647 as winter approached and supplies ran low. The retreat nearly turned into a rout, with hostile Uzbek bands harassing them. Though the Mughals suffered grievous losses, Aurangzeb’s leadership prevented a disaster.

        The Balkh campaign of Shah Jahan has been a subject of considerable debate among modern historians. From the foregoing account, it is clear that Shah Jahan was not attempting to fix the Mughal frontier along the so-called ‘scientific line’ of the Amu Darya (Oxus), as the river was hardly a defensible boundary. Nor was he motivated by the desire to conquer Samarqand and Farghana, the ancestral homelands of the Mughals, despite frequent references to them. Shah Jahan’s objective was to secure a friendly ruler in Balkh and Badakhshan—regions bordering Kabul that had been ruled by Timurid princes until 1585. He believed this would help contain the unrest among Afghan tribes around Ghazni and in the Khyber Pass.

        Militarily, the campaign was a success—the Mughals conquered Balkh and defeated Uzbek attempts to reclaim it. This marked the first significant victory of Indian arms in the region, and Shah Jahan had reason to celebrate. However, the Mughals lacked the resources to maintain long-term control over Balkh. Politically, sustaining Mughal influence in Balkh proved difficult due to persistent Persian hostility and an unfriendly local population.

        Ultimately, while the Balkh campaign temporarily bolstered the prestige of Mughal arms, it yielded little political advantage. Perhaps it would have been more beneficial for the Mughals, saving considerable expenditure in men and money, if Shah Jahan had firmly adhered to the Kabul-Ghazni-Qandhar line established by Akbar. Nevertheless, as the ruler of Balkh, Nazr Muhammad remained friendly toward the Mughals for the rest of his life, and diplomatic exchanges continued between the two courts.

        Thus, the Balkh expedition did succeed in preventing the rise of a united Uzbek state, which could have posed a serious threat to Mughal control over Kabul.

    Mughal-Persian Relations—The Last Phase
        The setback in Balkh led to a revival of Uzbek hostility in the Kabul region and Afghan tribal unrest in the Khyber-Ghazni area, emboldening the Persians to attack and conquer Qandhar in 1649. This was a significant blow to Shah Jahan’s pride, prompting him to launch three major campaigns, one after another, under royal princes to recover Qandhar. The first attack was led by Aurangzeb, the hero of Balkh, with an army of 50,000. Though the Mughals defeated the Persians outside the fort, they failed to capture it due to strong Persian resistance.

        A second attempt by Aurangzeb three years later also failed. The most ambitious effort was made the following year (1653) under Dara Shikoh, Shah Jahan’s favorite son. Despite his boastful claims, Dara was unable to force the fort into surrender, even with a large army. An attempt to breach the fort using two of the empire’s largest cannons also proved ineffective.

        The Mughal failure at Qandhar did not indicate weakness in their artillery, as some historians have suggested. Rather, it highlighted the inherent strength of Qandhar’s fortifications when defended by a determined commander and the limitations of medieval artillery against strong forts. This experience was similar to that of the Mughals in the Deccan. It may be argued that Shah Jahan’s attachment to Qandhar was more sentimental than strategic, as the weakening of both the Uzbeks and the Safavids had reduced Qandhar’s earlier significance. The loss of Qandhar itself was less damaging than the repeated Mughal failures, which hurt their prestige. However, this should not be overstated, as the Mughal Empire remained at its height during Aurangzeb’s reign. Even the proud Ottoman Sultan sent an embassy to Aurangzeb in 1680, seeking his support.

        Aurangzeb decided not to pursue the futile contest over Qandhar and quietly resumed diplomatic relations with Iran. However, in 1668, Shah Abbas II of Iran insulted the Mughal envoy, made disparaging remarks about Aurangzeb, and even threatened an invasion. This led to heightened Mughal military activity in Punjab and Kabul, but before any conflict could arise, Shah Abbas II died. His successors were weak rulers, and Persia ceased to pose a threat to India until Nadir Shah emerged more than fifty years later.

        On the whole, the Mughals succeeded in maintaining a scientific frontier in the northwest, based on the Hindukush, with Qandhar as its outer bastion. Their foreign policy primarily focused on India’s defense, with diplomacy reinforcing their military strategy. Friendship with Persia remained central despite temporary setbacks over Qandhar. The often-proclaimed ambition of reclaiming the Mughal homelands was more of a diplomatic ploy than a serious endeavor. The combination of military and diplomatic measures effectively shielded India from foreign invasions for a long period.

        Additionally, the Mughals insisted on relations of equality with the leading Asian powers of the time. They maintained parity with the Safavids, who claimed a privileged position due to their lineage from the Prophet, and with the Ottoman Sultans, who bore the title Padshah-i-Islam and positioned themselves as successors to the Caliphs of Baghdad. The Mughals also used their foreign policy to promote India’s commercial interests. Kabul and Qandhar served as the twin gateways for India’s trade with Central Asia.

    Growth of Administration: Mansabdari System and the Mughal Army
        The administrative machinery and revenue system established by Akbar continued under Jahangir and Shah Jahan with minor modifications. However, significant changes occurred in the functioning of the mansabdari system.

        Under Akbar, mansabdars were paid at an average rate of Rs. 240 per annum per sawar for maintaining their contingents. During Jahangir’s reign, this amount was reduced to Rs. 200 per annum. Additionally, mansabdars were permitted to retain 5% of their total salary bill to cover various contingent expenses. The Mughal military favored mixed contingents, incorporating soldiers from Irani and Turani Mughals, Indian Afghans, and Rajputs in fixed proportions to curb tribal or ethnic exclusivity. However, in special cases, certain Mughal or Rajput mansabdars were allowed to recruit exclusively from their own ethnic groups.

        Several modifications were made during this period. There was a general tendency to reduce salaries. Jahangir introduced the du-aspah sih-aspah system, allowing selected nobles to maintain a larger number of troopers without increasing their sawar rank. This system required a mansabdar with this distinction to maintain, and be paid for, double the number of troopers indicated by his sawar rank. For example, a mansabdar with a zat rank of 3000 and sawar rank of 3000 du-aspah sih-aspah would be required to maintain 6000 troopers. Normally, no mansabdar was given a sawar rank higher than his zat rank.

        During Shah Jahan’s reign, further changes were introduced to drastically reduce the number of sawars a noble was required to maintain. A noble was expected to maintain only one-third of his sawar rank, and in some cases, only one-fourth. For instance, a noble with a zat rank of 3000 and a sawar rank of 3000 would need to maintain just 1000 troopers. If his rank included the du-aspah sih-aspah distinction, he would maintain 2000 troopers.

        Although mansabdars were theoretically paid in rupees, their salaries were typically disbursed through jagirs. Most mansabdars preferred jagirs, as cash payments were often delayed and accompanied by bureaucratic harassment. Additionally, control over land was a symbol of social prestige. By implementing a structured system of ranks and regulations, the Mughals bureaucratized the nobility. However, they could not completely sever the nobility’s feudal attachment to land, which remained a significant challenge.

        To allocate jagirs, the revenue department maintained a register of assessed income (jama), recorded not in rupees but in dams (40 dams equaled one rupee). This document, known as jama-dami, provided income estimates for various regions. As the number of mansabdars increased and financial constraints grew, even these modifications were deemed insufficient. Drastic salary cuts would have provoked discontent among nobles, which the rulers could not afford. Consequently, a new scaling device was introduced to further reduce the quota of troopers and horses a noble had to maintain.

        The salaries of mansabdars were adjusted using a month-scale, reducing their obligations accordingly. Salaries were calculated for 10 months, 8 months, 6 months, or even less. For example, a mansabdar with a zat rank of 3000 and a sawar rank of 3000, who was required to maintain 1000 troopers under the earlier one-third rule, would now maintain only 1800 horses under a 10-month scale and just 1000 horses under a 5-month scale. However, it was rare for allowances to fall below 5 months or exceed 10 months.

        This month-scale system applied not only to jagirs but also to cash salaries. Under Shah Jahan, agricultural expansion and increased cash crop production led to a rise in jama-dami (jagir income). However, this increase was proportional to the overall price rise. Notably, many Marathas inducted into Mughal service were given mansabs on a 5-monthly basis or even less, allowing them a high rank in name while effectively reducing their cavalry obligations. This severe reduction in the availability of remounts weakened the efficiency of the Mughal cavalry.

        The mansabdari system was complex and required the proper functioning of both the dagh (branding) system and the jagirdari system. If the dagh system was poorly enforced, the state suffered financial losses. Similarly, if jama-dami was exaggerated or jagirdars failed to receive their due salaries, discontent would arise, leading to poor military maintenance. However, under Shah Jahan, the system functioned effectively due to his meticulous administration and careful selection of competent officials, particularly wazirs. Strict discipline, clear promotion policies, and a well-structured rewards system ensured that the Mughal nobility remained loyal and efficient in both administration and military affairs, securing and expanding the empire.

    The Mughal Army
        The cavalry, as we have noted, was the principal arm of the Mughal army, and the mansabdars provided the overwhelming proportion of it. In addition to the mansabdars, the Mughal emperors used to entertain individual troopers called ahadis. The ahadis have been called gentlemen-troopers and received much higher salaries than other troopers. They were a highly trusted corps, being recruited directly by the emperors and having their own muster-master. An ahadi mustered up to five horses, though sometimes two of them shared a horse. The duties of ahadis were of a miscellaneous character. Most clerks of the imperial offices, the painters of the court, and the foremen in the royal karkhanas belonged to this corps. Many were appointed as adjutants and carriers of imperial orders. In Shah Jahan’s reign, they numbered 7,000 and were often sent into the fighting line, where they were well distributed over different parts of the army. Many of them worked as skilled musketeers (banduq-andaz) and bowmen (tir-andaz).

        In addition to the ahadis, the emperors maintained a corps of royal bodyguards (walashahis) and armed palace guards. They were cavalrymen but served on foot in the citadel and the palace.

        The footmen (piyadgan) formed a numerous but miscellaneous body. Many of them consisted of matchlock-bearers (banduqchis) and received salaries ranging between three and seven rupees a month. This was the infantry proper. But the foot soldiers also included porters, servants, news runners, swordsmen, wrestlers, and slaves. The slaves, though not as numerous as during the Sultanate period, were clothed and fed by the emperor or by a prince. Sometimes a slave could become a gentleman-trooper, but generally, foot soldiers had a low status.

        The Mughal emperors had a large stable of war elephants and also a well-organized park of artillery. The artillery consisted of two sections—heavy guns, which were used for defending or assaulting forts, and light artillery, which was highly mobile and moved with the emperor whenever he wanted. The Mughals were keen on improving their artillery, and at first, many Ottomans and Portuguese were employed in the artillery department. By the time of Aurangzeb, Mughal artillery had improved considerably, and foreigners found employment in the artillery department with difficulty.

        The big guns were sometimes extravagantly large in size, but as a modern writer says, “These huge guns made more noise than they did harm; they could not be fired many times in a day, and were very liable to burst and destroy the men in charge.” However, the Frenchman Bernier, who accompanied Shah Jahan to Lahore and Kashmir, found the light artillery, called the ‘artillery of the stirrup,’ to be extremely well-appointed. He says: “It consisted of fifty small field pieces, all of brass; each piece mounted on a well-made and handsomely painted carriage, containing two ammunition boxes, and drawn by two fine horses, with a third horse in reserve.” Artillery or swivel guns were also mounted on elephants and camels.

        It is difficult to estimate the strength of the Mughal army. Under Shah Jahan, it consisted of about 2,00,000 cavalry, excluding the men working in the districts and with faujdars. It rose to 2,40,000 under Aurangzeb. The infantry under Shah Jahan, excluding the non-fighting people, was placed at 40,000 and may have been maintained at a similar figure under Aurangzeb.

        How efficient was the Mughal army compared to the neighboring West and Central Asian states and the European states of the time? It is difficult to answer this question, though a number of European travelers, such as Bernier, have made adverse remarks about the efficiency of the Mughal army. A careful analysis shows that his remarks were really directed towards the Mughal infantry, which had no drill or discipline, was ill-organized and ill-led, and resembled a rabble. The development of the infantry had taken a different path in Europe. With the development of the flint-gun, the infantry became a formidable fighting force during the seventeenth century and could even outclass the cavalry, as the Indian powers were to realize to their cost during the eighteenth century.

        The success of the Mughals against the Uzbeks, who could match themselves with the Persians at the time of the Balkh campaigns, suggests that the Mughal army was not inferior to the Central Asian and Persian armies in an open contest. However, the armies of these states were backward compared to the Europeans, particularly in the fields of infantry and artillery. Though somewhat deficient in artillery, by the time of Aurangzeb, the Mughals had apparently caught up with the Asian powers—though not with the European sea-going powers. The Mughals, like other Asian powers (except Turkey and the Sultanate of Oman), were deficient in the naval sphere, particularly in sea warfare. The army as a whole, particularly the cavalry, was closely linked with the jagirdari system, which, in turn, was based on the feudal system of land relations prevalent in the country. Ultimately, the strength and efficiency of one depended on the other.

- Economic and Social Life under the Mughals
    Economic and Social Conditions
        The Mughal Empire reached its territorial zenith by the end of the seventeenth century. During this period, it had to face many political and administrative problems, some of which we have already discussed. In the economic and social spheres, the period from the advent of Akbar to the end of the seventeenth century may be treated as one since there were no fundamental changes, although there were important social and economic developments which we shall try to analyze.

        Standard of Living: Pattern of Village Life and the Masses

        During this period, many European traders and travelers came to India, and some of them have left accounts of the social and economic conditions of the country. In general, they have emphasized the wealth and prosperity of India and the ostentatious lifestyle of the ruling classes on the one hand, and on the other, the grinding poverty of the ordinary people—the peasants, artisans, and laborers. Babur was struck by the scanty clothes worn by the ordinary people. He observed that “peasants and people of low standing go about naked.” He then goes on to describe the angota or decency cloth worn by men and the sari worn by women. His impression has been corroborated by later European travelers. Ralph Fitch, who came to India towards the end of the sixteenth century, says that at Banaras, “the people go naked save a little cloth bound about their middle.” De Laet wrote that the laborers had insufficient clothing to keep themselves warm and cozy during winter. However, Fitch observed, “In the winter, which is our May, the men wear quilted gowns of cotton and quilted caps.”

        Similar remarks have been made about the use of footwear. Nikitin observed that the people of the Deccan went barefooted. A modern author, Moreland, says that he did not find a shoe mentioned anywhere north of the Narmada River, except in Bengal, and ascribes it to the high cost of leather.

        As far as housing and furniture were concerned, little need be said. The mud houses in which the villagers lived were not different from those at present. They had hardly any furniture except cots and bamboo mats and used earthen utensils made by the village potter. Copper and bell-metal plates and utensils were expensive and generally not used by the poor.

        Regarding food, rice, millet, and pulses (what Pelsaert and De Laet called dicheri) formed the staple diet, along with fish in Bengal and the coastal areas, and meat in the southern peninsula. In North India, chapatis made of wheat or coarse grains, along with pulses and green vegetables, were common. The ordinary people, it is said, ate their main meal in the evening and chewed pulses or other parched grains during the day. Ghee and oil were much cheaper than food grains then and seem to have been a staple part of the poor man’s diet. However, salt and sugar were more expensive.

        Thus, while people had fewer clothes to wear and shoes were too costly, on balance, they ate better. With more grazing land, they could keep more cattle, so more milk and milk products must have been available.

        The standard of living ultimately depended upon income and wages. It is difficult to determine the income of the large mass of peasants in real terms, for money hardly entered into transactions in the villages. The village artisans were paid for their services through commodities fixed by custom. It is difficult to compute the average size of a peasant’s landholding. The information available to us shows that there was a great deal of inequality in villages. The peasants who did not have their own plows and bullocks often tilled the land of the zamindars or the upper castes and could barely eke out a living. The landless peasants and laborers often belonged to the class of people called “untouchables” or famins.

        Whenever there was a famine—and famines were frequent—it was this class of peasants and village artisans who suffered the most. The peasants who owned the land they tilled were called khudkasht. They paid land revenue at customary rates. Some of them had many plows and bullocks, which they let out to their poorer brethren, the tenants or muzarian, who generally paid land revenue at a higher rate. These two groups formed the largest section of cultivators in the village.

        Thus, village society was highly unequal. The khudkasht, who claimed to be the original settlers of the village, often belonged to a single dominant caste or castes. These castes not only dominated village society but also exploited the weaker sections. In turn, they were often exploited by the zamindars.

        It has been estimated that the population of India at the beginning of the seventeenth century was about 125 million. Hence, there was plenty of cultivable land available, and it may be surmised that a peasant would cultivate as much land as his means and family circumstances would allow, subject to social restraints. Unlike many other countries in Asia and Africa, India had a well-diversified economy, cultivating a large variety of crops such as wheat, rice, gram, barley, pulses, and bajra, as well as cash crops like cotton, indigo, chay (red dye), sugarcane, and oilseeds. These crops paid land revenue at a higher rate and had to be paid for in cash. Hence, they were often called cash crops or superior crops. The peasants not only shifted cultivation from one crop to another depending on prices but were also willing to adopt new crops if they found it profitable. During the seventeenth century, two new crops—tobacco and maize—were introduced. Silk and tusser cultivation became so widespread in Bengal that there was no longer a need to import silk from China. The adoption of potatoes and red chilies happened in the eighteenth century.

        Despite the lack of new agricultural techniques, Indian agriculture was, on balance, efficient and played a significant role in the growth of the manufacturing sector and trade during the period. In medieval times, a peasant was not dispossessed of his land as long as he paid land revenue. He could also sell his land if he found a buyer, provided the community raised no objections. His children inherited his land as a matter of right after his death. However, state dues were heavy, sometimes amounting to nearly half of his produce, leaving the ordinary peasant with barely enough to survive. This made it difficult for them to invest in land improvement or cultivation expansion.

        Although the life of the peasant was hard, he had enough to eat and meet his basic needs. His lifestyle was dictated partly by the seasons and partly by customs and traditions, including fairs, pilgrimages, and ceremonies. The landless and a section of artisans, including menials, had much harder lives. However, not all peasants lived at this low level. Resident cultivators (khudkasht) had larger landholdings and often owned many plows and oxen for cultivation. They could also rent out part of their land to ordinary cultivators (muzarian) on profitable terms.

        These sections, along with village zamindars, invested in agricultural expansion and improvement.

        In cities, the largest section of the population consisted of the poor—artisans, servants, slaves, soldiers, and manual workers. According to European travelers, the salary of the lowest-grade servant was less than two rupees a month. The bulk of workers and foot soldiers began at less than three rupees a month. It has been calculated that a man could feed his family on two rupees a month. Moreland, writing in the early twentieth century, observed that real wages had remained unchanged since Mughal times—while workers had a more balanced diet, clothes, sugar, and other items had become more expensive.

        Moreland concluded that the conditions of the Indian people had not worsened under British rule. However, this needs to be examined in a broader context. While Europe saw vast increases in wealth and real wages during this period, India stagnated, if not declined, under British rule.

    The Ruling Classes: The Nobles and Zamindars
        The nobility, along with the landed gentry, the zamindars, formed what may be called the ruling class in medieval India. Socially and economically, the Mughal nobility formed a privileged class. Theoretically, the doors of the Mughal nobility were open to everyone. In practice, persons belonging to aristocratic families, whether they were Indians or foreigners, had a decided advantage.

        To begin with, the bulk of the Mughal nobles were drawn from the homeland of the Mughals—Turan—and from its neighboring areas, such as Tajikistan, Khurasan, and Iran. Although Babur was a Turk, the Mughal rulers never followed a narrow racist policy. Babur tried to win the leading Afghan nobles to his side, but they proved to be restless and untrustworthy and soon defected. The tussle between the Mughals and the Afghans continued in Bihar and Bengal even under Akbar. However, from the time of Jahangir, more Afghans began to be recruited into the nobility. Indian Muslims, known as Shaikhzadas or Hindustanis, were also given service.

        From the time of Akbar, Hindus also began to be inducted into the nobility on a regular basis. The largest section among them consisted of the Rajputs. Initially, among the Rajputs, the Kachhwahas predominated. According to modern calculations, the proportion of Hindus in the nobility under Akbar in 1594 was about 16 percent. However, these figures do not provide an adequate idea of the position and influence of Hindus. Both Raja Man Singh and Raja Birbal were personal friends and close companions of Akbar, while in revenue administration, Raja Todar Mal held a place of great influence and honor. The Rajputs who were recruited to the nobility were either hereditary rajas or belonged to aristocratic families related to or allied with the raja. Thus, their incorporation into the nobility strengthened its aristocratic character.

        Despite this, the nobility did provide an avenue of promotion and distinction to individuals from the lower sections of society. Many Kayasthas and Khatris were employed at various levels in the central and provincial governments, and a few of them were elevated to the position of nobles. Some individuals of humble origins even attained the status of mansabdars.

        The nobility attained considerable stability under Jahangir and Shah Jahan. Both these monarchs paid careful attention to the organization of the nobility through the mansabdari system. Rules and regulations were established for orderly promotions, discipline, and the recruitment of competent people into the imperial service.

        The Mughal nobles, as mentioned earlier, received salaries that were extremely high by any standards. This, along with the liberal policy of the Mughal emperors in matters of faith and the stable political conditions in India, attracted many talented individuals from foreign lands to the Mughal court. As a result, there was a reverse brain drain. Due to the influx of Iranis, Turanis, and others in search of service at the Mughal court, a French traveler, Bernier, remarked that the Mughal nobility consisted of “foreigners who enticed each other to the court.” However, modern research has shown this statement to be inaccurate. While talented individuals continued to migrate to India and rise to prominence in Mughal service, they all settled in India permanently. Thus, just like in earlier periods, India continued to provide a home for many people from outside. These immigrants quickly assimilated into Indian society and culture while retaining some of their distinctive traits. This contributed to the richness and diversity of Indian culture.

        Under Jahangir and Shah Jahan, the majority of the nobles were already those born in India. Simultaneously, the proportion of Afghans, Indian Muslims (Hindustanis), and Hindus in the nobility continued to rise. A new section among the Hindus that entered the nobility during this period were the Marathas. Jahangir was the first monarch to recognize the importance of the Marathas in the Deccan and sought to win them over to his side. This policy was continued by Shah Jahan. Among the Maratha sardars who served Shah Jahan was Shahji, the father of Shivaji, although he later defected. Aurangzeb also recruited many Marathas and Deccani Muslims into his service.

        We shall discuss Mughal relations with the Marathas in a later section. However, it is worth noting that while Hindus formed roughly 24 percent of the nobility under Shah Jahan, their proportion rose to about 33 percent during the second half of Aurangzeb’s reign, with their total number increasing four and a half times. Among the Hindu nobles, the Marathas made up more than half during Aurangzeb’s reign.

        Although the Mughal nobles received extremely high salaries, their expenses were also substantial. Each noble maintained a large retinue of servants and attendants, as well as a sizable stable of horses, elephants, and other forms of transport. Many of them maintained large harems, which was considered normal for a man of status in those times. The nobles imitated the Mughal emperors in leading a highly ostentatious lifestyle.
