However, internal disputes led to the division of the kingdom in the early seventeenth century, which invited Mughal intervention in Assam at the request of the Kuch ruler. The Mughals defeated the breakaway kingdom and, with the help of Kuch forces, occupied the western Assam Valley up to the Bar Nadi in 1612. The Kuch ruler became a Mughal vassal. This brought the Mughals into direct contact with the Ahoms, who ruled eastern Assam beyond the Bar Nadi. After a prolonged war with the Ahoms, who had sheltered a prince from the defeated dynasty, a treaty was finally signed in 1638, fixing the Bar Nadi as the boundary between the two powers. As a result, Guwahati came under Mughal control.

        During Aurangzeb’s reign, a long-drawn-out war between the Mughals and the Ahoms ensued. The war began when the Ahom rulers attempted to expel the Mughals from Guwahati and its surrounding areas to consolidate their control over Assam. Mir Jumla, whom Aurangzeb had appointed as the governor of Bengal, sought to make his mark by bringing Cooch Bihar and the entire Assam region under Mughal rule. First, he attacked Cooch Bihar, which had rejected Mughal suzerainty, and annexed the entire kingdom to the Mughal Empire. He then invaded the Ahom kingdom, occupying the Ahom capital, Garhgaon, and holding it for six months. He eventually penetrated deep into the Ahom kingdom, forcing the Ahom king to sign a humiliating treaty in 1663. The treaty required the Ahom ruler to send his daughter to the Mughal harem, pay a hefty war indemnity, and provide an annual tribute of 20 elephants. As a result, the Mughal boundary was extended from the Bar Nadi to the Bharali River.

        However, Mir Jumla died soon after this apparent victory. The advantages of a forward policy in Assam remained doubtful, as the region was not wealthy and was surrounded by warlike tribes such as the Nagas in the mountains. Furthermore, the Ahom power remained unbroken, and enforcing the treaty proved beyond Mughal capabilities. In 1667, the Ahoms renewed their struggle, recovering the ceded territories and recapturing Guwahati. Earlier, the Mughals had also been expelled from Cooch Bihar. Consequently, all of Mir Jumla’s gains were quickly lost. A prolonged and sporadic war with the Ahoms followed for over a decade and a half. Raja Ram Singh, the ruler of Amber, was given command of the Mughal forces, but he lacked sufficient resources for the task. Eventually, the Mughals were forced to retreat further west, abandoning Guwahati.

        The events in Assam highlighted the limits of Mughal power in distant regions and the skill and determination of the Ahoms, who effectively used guerrilla warfare tactics. Similar strategies would later be successfully employed by other opponents of the Mughals. However, the prolonged Mughal invasion and subsequent warfare weakened the Ahom monarchy, ultimately leading to the decline and fragmentation of the Ahom empire.

        The Mughals had more success elsewhere. Shaista Khan, who succeeded Mir Jumla as the governor of Bengal after his setback at the hands of Shivaji, proved to be a capable administrator and general. He modified Mir Jumla’s aggressive policy, first securing a settlement with the ruler of Cooch Bihar. Next, he turned his attention to southern Bengal, where the Magh (Arakanese) pirates had been terrorizing the region up to Dacca from their base in Chittagong. The land up to Dacca had been depopulated, and trade and industry had suffered significant setbacks. Shaista Khan built a navy to counter the Arakanese pirates and captured the island of Sondip as a base of operations against Chittagong. He also won over the Firingis (Portuguese mercenaries) with financial inducements and other favors. The Arakanese navy near Chittagong was routed, and many of their ships were captured. In early 1666, Chittagong was stormed and captured by the Mughals. The destruction of the Arakanese navy allowed for the expansion of trade and commerce, playing a crucial role in Bengal’s economic growth. This victory also contributed to the expansion of agriculture in eastern Bengal.

        In Orissa, the rebellion of the Afghan Pathans was crushed, and Balasore was reopened to commerce.

    Peasant Revolts and Movements for Regional Independence:
        Jats, Afghans, and Sikhs
            Within the empire, Aurangzeb had to deal with several difficult political problems, such as the challenges posed by the Marathas in the Deccan, the Jats and Rajputs in North India, and the Afghans and Sikhs in the northwest. Some of these problems were not new and had also been faced by Aurangzeb’s predecessors. However, they took on a different character under Aurangzeb. The nature of these movements also varied.

            In the case of the Rajputs, it was primarily a problem of succession. For the Marathas, it was a question of local independence. The conflict with the Jats had a peasant-agrarian background. The only movement in which religion played a powerful role was the Sikh movement. Both the Jat and Sikh movements ultimately culminated in the establishment of independent regional states. The struggle of the Afghans was tribal in character, but even there, the sentiment of setting up a separate Afghan state was at work.

            Thus, economic and social factors, as well as the sentiment of regional independence—which remained strong—were major factors in shaping these movements. Religion also played an undeniable role. It has sometimes been argued that all these movements, except the Afghan one, represented a Hindu reaction against Aurangzeb’s narrow religious policies. In a country where the overwhelming majority of the population consisted of Hindus, any movement that clashed with the predominantly Muslim central government could be seen as a challenge to Islam. Likewise, the leaders of these rebel movements could use religious slogans or symbols to broaden their appeal. Hence, religion must be seen as part of societal and political movements.

        Jats and Satnamis
            The first section to come into conflict with the Mughal government was the Jats of the Agra-Delhi region, who lived on both sides of the Yamuna River. The Jats were mostly peasant cultivators, with only a few of them being zamindars. With a strong sense of brotherhood and justice, the Jats often came into conflict with the government and resorted to rebellion, taking advantage of the difficult terrain. Conflicts with the Jats had already occurred during the reigns of Jahangir and Shah Jahan over the collection of land revenue. Since the imperial road to the Deccan and western seaports passed through the Jat region, the Mughal government took these rebellions seriously and adopted stern measures.

            In 1669, the Jats of the Mathura region revolted under the leadership of a local zamindar, Gokla. The rebellion spread rapidly among the peasants of the area, prompting Aurangzeb to personally march from Delhi to quell it. Although the Jat forces had grown to 20,000, they were no match for the organized imperial army. In a fierce battle, the Jats were defeated, and Gokla was captured and executed. However, the movement was not completely crushed, and discontent continued to simmer. Meanwhile, in 1672, another armed conflict erupted between the peasants and the Mughal state at Narnaul, near Mathura. This time, the conflict involved a religious sect called the Satnamis. The Satnamis were mostly peasants, artisans, and lower-caste people, described by a contemporary writer as “goldsmiths, carpenters, sweepers, tanners, and other ignoble beings.” They did not observe distinctions of caste and rank or between Hindus and Muslims and followed a strict code of conduct. What began as a minor clash with a local official soon escalated into an open rebellion. Once again, the emperor had to personally lead a campaign to suppress it. Interestingly, local Hindu zamindars, many of whom were Rajputs, sided with the Mughals in this conflict.

            In 1685, the Jats revolted again under the leadership of Rajaram. This time, they were better organized and adopted guerrilla warfare tactics, combining them with plundering. Aurangzeb enlisted the help of Raja Bishan Singh, the Kachhwaha ruler, to suppress the uprising. Bishan Singh was appointed faujdar of Mathura, and the entire area was granted to him as a zamindari. The conflict between the Jats and Rajputs over zamindari rights further complicated the issue. Most of the primary zamindars (cultivating peasants who owned land) were Jats, while the intermediary zamindars (who collected land revenue) were Rajputs. The Jats put up strong resistance, but by 1691, Rajaram and his successor, Churaman, were compelled to submit. However, unrest among the Jat peasants persisted, and their plundering activities made the Delhi-Agra road unsafe for travelers. Later, in the eighteenth century, taking advantage of Mughal civil wars and the weakening of central authority, Churaman was able to carve out a separate Jat principality in the region, ousting the Rajput zamindars. Thus, what initially started as a peasant uprising gradually transformed into the formation of a Jat-ruled state.

        The Afghans
            Aurangzeb came into conflict with the Afghans as well. Conflict with the hardy Afghan tribesmen who lived in the mountainous region between Punjab and Kabul was not new. Akbar had to fight against the Afghans and, in the process, lost the life of his close friend and confidant, Raja Birbal. Conflict with the Afghan tribesmen also occurred during the reign of Shah Jahan. These conflicts were partly economic and partly political and religious. With little means of livelihood in the rugged mountains, the Afghans had no option but to prey on caravans or enroll in the Mughal armies. Their fierce love of freedom made service in the Mughal armies difficult. The Mughals generally kept them content by paying subsidies, but the growth of population or the rise of an ambitious leader could lead to a breach of this tacit agreement.

            During Aurangzeb’s reign, a new stirring among the Pathans took place. In 1667, Bhagu, the leader of the Yusufzai tribe, proclaimed a person named Muhammad Shah as king, who claimed descent from an ancient royal lineage, and declared himself his wazir. It appears that among the Afghans, as among the Jats, the ambition of setting up a separate state of their own had begun to rise. A religious revivalist movement called the Raushanai, which emphasized a strict ethical life and devotion to a chosen pir, had provided an intellectual and moral background to the movement.

            Gradually, Bhagu’s movement spread, and his followers began ravaging and plundering the Hazara, Attock, and Peshawar districts, bringing traffic in the Khyber Pass to a standstill. To clear the Khyber and crush the uprising, Aurangzeb deputed the chief bakhshi, Amir Khan, with a Rajput contingent. After a series of hard-fought battles, the Afghan resistance was broken. However, to keep them in check, Maharaja Jaswant Singh, the ruler of Marwar, was appointed as thanedar of Jamrud in 1671.

            There was a second Afghan uprising in 1672. This time, the leader of the opposition was the Afridi chief, Akmal Khan, who proclaimed himself king, read the khutba, and struck coins in his name. He declared war against the Mughals and summoned all the Afghans to join him. According to a contemporary writer, with a following “more numerous than ants and locusts,” they closed the Khyber Pass.

            Moving forward to clear the Pass, Amir Khan advanced too far and suffered a disastrous defeat in the narrow defile. He barely managed to escape with his life, but 10,000 men perished, and cash and goods worth two crores were looted by the Afghans. This defeat encouraged other tribesmen, including Khushhal Khan Khattak, a sworn enemy of Aurangzeb who had previously suffered imprisonment under him, to join the resistance.

            In 1674, another Mughal noble, Shujaat Khan, suffered a disastrous rout in the Khyber. However, he was rescued by a heroic band of Rathores sent by Jaswant Singh. At last, in the middle of 1674, Aurangzeb himself went to Peshawar and remained in the region until the end of 1675. Through force and diplomacy, the Afghan united front was broken, and peace was gradually restored.

            The Afghan uprising demonstrates that the sentiments of resistance to Mughal rule and the urge for regional freedom were not confined to sections of Hindus, such as the Jats and Marathas. Additionally, the Afghan uprising helped reduce Mughal pressure on Shivaji during a crucial period. It also made it difficult, if not impossible, for the Mughals to pursue an aggressive forward policy in the Deccan until 1676—by which time Shivaji had crowned himself and entered into an alliance with Bijapur and Golconda.

        The Sikhs
            Although there had been some clashes between the Sikh Gurus and the Mughals under Shah Jahan, there was no conflict between the Sikhs and Aurangzeb until 1675. In fact, conscious of the growing importance of the Sikhs, Aurangzeb had tried to engage Ram Rai, the elder son of Guru Har Rai, at the court. However, Guru Har Rai was displeased with Ram Rai and nominated his younger son, Har Kishan, as his successor, even though he was only six years old at the time. Har Kishan died soon after and was succeeded in 1664 by Guru Tegh Bahadur. Ram Rai put forward his claim to the gaddi both before the accession of Guru Har Kishan and after his death. Aurangzeb did not interfere and granted land at Dehra Dun to Ram Rai to build his gurudwara there. However, Ram Rai mostly remained in Delhi and continued to intrigue against Guru Tegh Bahadur, attempting to poison the emperor’s mind against him. After his succession, Guru Tegh Bahadur came to Delhi, but to escape Ram Rai’s machinations, he journeyed to Bihar and served with Raja Ram Singh of Amber in Assam until 1671. However, in 1675, Guru Tegh Bahadur was brought to Delhi along with five of his followers. Various accusations were made against him, and he was asked to renounce his faith, which he refused. As punishment, he was beheaded.

            Various reasons have been put forward to explain Aurangzeb’s action. According to a poetic work by Guru Gobind Singh, the son and successor of Guru Tegh Bahadur, he gave up his life in defense of the Hindu faith after meeting some Brahmins from Kashmir who sought his support. However, there are no detailed records of this meeting. Another later tradition states that the Guru was protesting against the oppression of the Mughal governor of Kashmir, Sher Afgan, and the large-scale forced conversion of Hindus. However, the Mughal governor of Kashmir until 1671 was Saif Khan, a well-known builder of bridges and a liberal administrator who even appointed a Hindu advisor. His successor, Iftikhar Khan, was known for his anti-Shia stance, but there are no historical references to his persecution of Hindus. In fact, no local Kashmiri histories, including one written by Narayan Kaul in 1710, mention such persecution.

            Another tradition suggests that Guru Tegh Bahadur was executed because his enemies and rivals, such as Ram Rai, convinced Aurangzeb to ask him to perform a miracle to prove his divine powers. When the Guru refused, action was taken against him. However, this seems unlikely, as Aurangzeb was away from Delhi from early 1675 to March 1676, dealing with the Afghan rebels. Therefore, he could not have summoned the Guru at Ram Rai’s suggestion.

            Later Persian sources, which seem to justify the official action, claim that Guru Tegh Bahadur had a large following and moved around Punjab alongside Hafiz Adam, a follower of Shaikh Ahmad Sirhindi, extorting money from villagers. These sources also state that a local waqia-navis (intelligence reporter) warned the emperor that if action was not taken against the Guru, it could lead to disturbances or even a rebellion. However, the reliability of these accounts is questionable, as Hafiz Adam had died much earlier. Additionally, some Persian sources claim the Guru was executed in Lahore rather than Delhi.

            It appears that, for Aurangzeb, the beheading of the Guru was primarily a law-and-order matter. However, another Persian source contradicts this, stating that peasants in conflict with revenue collectors, jagirdars, or zamindars often sought refuge with the Guru, who protected them. This suggests that rather than extorting money, as alleged, the Guru was emerging as a champion against injustice and oppression.

            Aurangzeb’s emphasis on sharia, his destruction of newly built temples, and even the demolition of some old temples in places like Mathura and Varanasi—as punishment for local rebellions or in response to complaints from qazis about Hindu teachings being accessible to Muslims—had created an atmosphere of heightened religious tension. In such a scenario, any conflict with a respected religious leader was bound to have larger repercussions.

            Regardless of the reasons, Aurangzeb’s execution of Guru Tegh Bahadur was unjustifiable from any perspective and demonstrated a narrow-minded approach. The event forced the Sikhs to retreat to the Punjab hills and led to the Sikh movement gradually transforming into a military brotherhood. Guru Gobind Singh played a major role in this transformation. He showed remarkable organizational ability and, in 1699, founded the Khalsa, a militarized Sikh order. Before this, Guru Gobind Singh had established his headquarters at Makhowal, or Anandpur, in the foothills of Punjab. Initially, the local Hindu hill rajas attempted to use the Guru and his followers in their internal conflicts. However, as the Guru’s power grew, clashes erupted between him and the hill rajas. The Guru generally triumphed, and the formation of the Khalsa further strengthened his position. An open breach between the Guru and the hill rajas occurred in 1704, when a coalition of rajas attacked Anandpur. Unable to defeat him, they sought Mughal intervention.

            This conflict was not primarily religious; it was partly a result of local rivalries between the Hindu hill rajas and the Sikhs and partly due to the growing strength of the Sikh movement. Aurangzeb, concerned about the Guru’s increasing power, had earlier instructed the Mughal faujdar to “admonish the Guru.” He later wrote to the governor of Lahore and the faujdar of Sirhind, Wazir Khan, urging them to support the hill rajas against Guru Gobind Singh. The Mughal forces besieged Anandpur, but the Sikhs fought bravely and repelled several attacks. Eventually, the Mughals and their allies blockaded the fort, forcing the Guru to negotiate. Allegedly, Wazir Khan offered him safe passage. However, when the Guru’s forces were crossing a swollen stream, Wazir Khan’s men suddenly attacked. Two of the Guru’s sons were captured and, upon refusing to convert to Islam, were executed in Sirhind. The Guru lost two more sons in another battle. After these tragic losses, he withdrew to Talwandi and was largely left undisturbed.

            It remains uncertain whether Wazir Khan acted on Aurangzeb’s direct orders in executing the Guru’s sons. In fact, Aurangzeb later wrote to the governor of Lahore, advising him to reconcile with Guru Gobind Singh. When the Guru wrote to Aurangzeb in the Deccan, describing the events, the emperor invited him for a meeting. In late 1706, Guru Gobind Singh set out for the Deccan, but Aurangzeb died before they could meet. Some believe the Guru had hoped to persuade Aurangzeb to restore Anandpur to him.

            Although Guru Gobind Singh could not establish a separate Sikh state during his lifetime, he laid the foundation for its future realization. His leadership demonstrated how an egalitarian religious movement could, under specific circumstances, evolve into a political and militaristic force, ultimately steering towards regional independence.

            It is doubtful whether the dastardly action of Wazir Khan against the sons of the Guru was carried out at the instance of Aurangzeb. Aurangzeb, it seems, was not keen to destroy the Guru and wrote to the governor of Lahore to “conciliate the Guru.” When the Guru wrote to Aurangzeb in the Deccan, apprising him of the events, Aurangzeb invited him to meet him. Towards the end of 1706, the Guru set out for the Deccan and was on the way when Aurangzeb died. According to some, he had hoped to persuade Aurangzeb to restore Anandpur to him.

            Although Guru Gobind Singh was not able to withstand Mughal might for long or establish a separate Sikh state, he created a tradition and also forged a weapon for its realization later on. It also showed how an egalitarian religious movement could, under certain circumstances, turn into a political and militaristic movement and subtly move towards regional independence.

        RELATIONS WITH THE RAJPUTS—BREACH WITH MARWAR AND MEWAR
            We have seen how Jahangir settled the long-drawn-out conflict with Mewar in 1613. Jahangir continued Akbar’s policy of granting favors to the leading Rajput rajas and entering into matrimonial relations with them. Shah Jahan maintained the alliance with the Rajputs. During his reign, Rajput contingents served with distinction in such far-flung areas as the Deccan, Balkh in Central Asia, and Qandhar. However, no Rajput raja was appointed governor of a province, and no further matrimonial alliances were made with the leading Rajput rajas—though Shah Jahan himself was the son of a Rathor princess.

            Perhaps, with the Rajput alliance having been consolidated, it was felt that further matrimonial ties with the leading rajas were no longer necessary. However, Shah Jahan accorded high honor to the heads of the two leading Rajput houses, Jodhpur and Amber. Raja Jaswant Singh, the ruler of Marwar, was highly favored by Shah Jahan. Both he and Jai Singh held the ranks of 7000/7000 at the time of Aurangzeb’s accession.

            Aurangzeb attached great value to the alliance with the Rajputs. He tried to secure the active support of the Maharana of Mewar and raised his mansab from 5000/5000 to 6000/6000. Although Jaswant Singh had fought against him at Dharmat, defected during the campaign against Shuja, and invited Dara to his dominions, Aurangzeb pardoned him and restored his previous mansab. He was also appointed to important commands, including the governorship of Gujarat. Jai Singh remained Aurangzeb’s close friend and confidant until his death in 1667.

            Jaswant Singh, who had been deputed to handle Afghan affairs in the northwest, died towards the end of 1678. The Maharaja had no surviving male heir, raising the question of succession to the gaddi. There was a longstanding Mughal tradition that in the case of a disputed succession, the state was brought under Mughal administration (khalisa) to ensure law and order before being handed over to the chosen successor. In 1650, when there was a succession dispute in Jaisalmer, Shah Jahan first took the state under khalisa and then sent Jaswant Singh at the head of an army to install the emperor’s chosen candidate.

            Another reason for bringing Marwar under khalisa was that the Maharaja, like many Mughal nobles, owed large sums of money to the state. Many Rajputs, whom Jaswant Singh had offended or whose territories had been granted to him in jagir, were eager to use the absence of a ruler on the gaddi of Jodhpur to create disturbances.

            Anticipating resistance from the Rathors, Aurangzeb allotted two parganas in Marwar for the maintenance of Jaswant Singh’s family and supporters. He assembled a strong army and marched to Ajmer to enforce his orders. Rani Hadi, Jaswant Singh’s chief queen, who had objected to handing over Jodhpur to the Mughals—since it was the watan (homeland) of the Rathors—had no option but to submit. A diligent search was made for any hidden treasures Jaswant Singh might have possessed, and Mughal officials were posted throughout Marwar. Large numbers of temples, including old ones, were demolished or sealed. The Mughals behaved as conquerors and treated Marwar as hostile territory.

            It is difficult to justify this action. However, Aurangzeb did not intend to retain Marwar for its strategic importance in linking Delhi with the Gujarat seaports, as suggested by modern historian Jadunath Sarkar. Two sons were born in Lahore to two of Jaswant Singh’s queens after his death. Their claim to the gaddi was strongly supported. However, before returning to Delhi, Aurangzeb decided to award the sikka (throne) of Jodhpur to Inder Singh, the grandson of Jaswant Singh’s elder brother, Amar Singh, in return for a succession fee of thirty-six lakhs of rupees.

            Perhaps Aurangzeb was influenced by the argument that Shah Jahan had committed an injustice by passing over Amar Singh in favor of his younger brother, Jaswant Singh. He may also have wanted to avoid a minority administration in Marwar. Some modern historians claim Aurangzeb offered Jodhpur to Ajit Singh, the posthumous son of Jaswant Singh, on the condition that he convert to Islam. However, contemporary sources provide no evidence of this. According to the Hukumat-ni-Bahi, a contemporary Rajasthani work, Aurangzeb offered Ajit Singh a mansab when he was presented at the court in Agra and declared that the two parganas of Sojat and Jaitaran in Marwar would continue as his jagir. Thus, Aurangzeb was effectively contemplating a division of Marwar between two branches of the family.

            The Rathor sardars, led by Durgadas, rejected this compromise, considering it against the best interests of the state. Angered by their refusal, Aurangzeb ordered that the princes and their mothers be confined in the fort of Nurgarh. Alarmed, the Rathor sardars staged a daring escape from Agra with one of the princes and crowned him Ajit Singh at Jodhpur amidst great rejoicing.

            Aurangzeb might have accepted that Inder Singh had no support among the Rathors. Instead, he set Inder Singh aside for ‘incompetence’ but refused to recognize Ajit Singh, branding him a ‘pretender.’ He summoned strong forces from across the empire and once again marched to Ajmer. The Rathor resistance was crushed, and Jodhpur was occupied. Durgadas fled with Ajit Singh to Mewar, where the Rana provided him a secret hideout.

            At this point, Mewar entered the war on Ajit Singh’s side. Rana Raj Singh, who had initially supported Aurangzeb, gradually turned against him. He had sent 5,000 troops under a trusted commander to Jodhpur to support Rani Hadi’s claim. He was opposed to Mughal interference in Rajput succession disputes and resented Mughal efforts to detach Dungarpur, Banswara, and other vassal states from Mewar. The immediate cause of his opposition, however, was Aurangzeb’s military occupation of Marwar and his rejection of Ajit Singh’s claim.

            Aurangzeb struck the first blow, attacking Mewar in November 1679. A strong Mughal detachment reached Udaipur and even raided the Rana’s camp. The Rana had retreated deep into the hills to conduct guerrilla warfare against the Mughals. The war soon reached a stalemate. The Mughals could neither penetrate the hills nor counter the Rajputs’ guerrilla tactics. The war became highly unpopular, and Aurangzeb’s admonitions had little effect on his commanders.

            At last, Prince Akbar, Aurangzeb’s eldest son, tried to exploit the situation by rebelling against his father. In alliance with the Rathor chief Durgadas, he marched on Ajmer (January 1681), where Aurangzeb was vulnerable, with his best troops engaged elsewhere. However, Prince Akbar delayed, allowing Aurangzeb to stir up dissension in his camp through false letters. Prince Akbar was forced to flee to Maharashtra, and Aurangzeb was relieved.

            The Mewar campaign now became secondary for Aurangzeb. He patched up a treaty with Rana Jagat Singh (Rana Raj Singh had since died). The new Rana was forced to surrender some parganas in lieu of jizyah and was granted a mansab of 5000 in exchange for a promise of loyalty and non-interference in Ajit Singh’s claim. Aurangzeb promised that Ajit Singh would receive a mansab and his father’s raj upon reaching adulthood.

            This agreement satisfied none of the Rajputs. The Mughals retained control over Marwar, and sporadic warfare continued until 1698 when Ajit Singh was finally recognized as Marwar’s ruler. However, the Mughals refused to relinquish control of Jodhpur, and tensions persisted until Aurangzeb’s death in 1707.

            Aurangzeb’s policy towards Marwar and Mewar was clumsy and counterproductive. It weakened the Mughal-Rajput alliance at a critical time, damaged Mughal military prestige, and highlighted Aurangzeb’s rigid, obstinate nature. Although it did not indicate a determination to subvert Hinduism—since large numbers of Marathas entered the nobility after 1679—it contributed to the strain on the empire. However, the real conflict lay in the Deccan.


- Climax and Disintegration of the Mughal Empire—II
    THE RISE OF THE MARATHAS
        We have already seen that the Marathas held important positions in the administrative and military systems of Ahmadnagar and Bijapur, and that their power and influence in governmental affairs grew as the Mughals advanced towards the Deccan. Both the Deccani sultans and the Mughals sought their support, and Malik Ambar used them extensively in his army as loose auxiliaries. Although several influential Maratha families—the Mores, the Ghatages, the Nimbalkars, etc.—exercised local authority in some areas, the Marathas did not have large, well-established states like the Rajputs. The credit for establishing such a large state goes to Shahji Bhonsale and his son, Shivaji. As we have seen, Shahji acted as a kingmaker in Ahmadnagar for some time and defied the Mughals. However, by the Treaty of 1636, Shahji yielded the territories he controlled. He joined the service of Bijapur and directed his efforts toward Karnataka. Taking advantage of unsettled conditions, Shahji attempted to establish a semi-independent principality at Bangalore, similar to how Mir Jumla, the leading noble of Golconda, sought to carve out a principality on the Coromandel coast. Several other chiefs, such as the Abyssinian chiefs (the Sidis) on the western coast, acted in a similar manner. This forms the backdrop to Shivaji’s attempt to carve out a large principality around Poona.

    EARLY CAREER OF SHIVAJI
        Shahji left the Poona jagir to his neglected senior wife, Jija Bai, and his minor son, Shivaji. Shivaji demonstrated his mettle at a young age when, at just 18, he overran several hill forts near Poona—Rajgarh, Kondana, and Torna—between 1645 and 1647. With the death of his guardian, Dadaji Kondadeo, in 1647, Shivaji became his own master, gaining full control of his father’s jagir.

        Shivaji truly began his career of conquest in 1656 when he conquered Javli from the Maratha chief Chandra Rao More. The Javli kingdom and the accumulated treasure of the Mores were significant, and Shivaji acquired them through treachery. The conquest of Javli made him the undisputed master of the Mavala region, or the highlands, and cleared his path to the Satara area and the coastal strip, the Konkan. Mavali foot soldiers became a strong component of his army. With their help, he strengthened his position by acquiring a further series of hill forts near Poona.

        The Mughal invasion of Bijapur in 1657 saved Shivaji from Aurangzeb. He then changed sides and made deep inroads into Mughal territory, seizing rich booty. When Aurangzeb reached an agreement with the new Bijapur ruler in preparation for the Mughal civil war, he pardoned Shivaji as well. However, Aurangzeb distrusted Shivaji and advised the Bijapur ruler to expel him from the Bijapuri territories he had seized. If the ruler wished to employ him, he suggested that Shivaji be stationed in Karnataka, far from the Mughal frontiers.

        With Aurangzeb preoccupied in the north, Shivaji resumed his conquests at the expense of Bijapur. He launched an offensive into the Konkan, the coastal strip between the Ghats and the sea, seizing its northern part. He also captured several other hill forts. Determined to counter him, Bijapur sent a premier noble, Afzal Khan, at the head of 10,000 troops with orders to capture Shivaji by any means necessary. Treachery was common in those days, and both Afzal Khan and Shivaji had resorted to it on several occasions. Shivaji’s forces, unused to open battle, hesitated to engage this powerful noble in direct combat. Afzal Khan invited Shivaji for a personal meeting, promising to secure his pardon from the Bijapuri court. Convinced that this was a trap, Shivaji went prepared and, in a cunning but daring manner, murdered Afzal Khan in 1659. Shivaji’s forces then routed the leaderless Bijapuri army, capturing all its goods, equipment, and artillery. Flushed with victory, the Maratha troops overran the powerful fort of Panhala and advanced into South Konkan and the Kolhapur districts, making extensive conquests.

        Shivaji’s exploits made him a legendary figure. His fame spread, and he was credited with magical powers. People from the Maratha regions flocked to join his army, and even Afghan mercenaries who had previously served Bijapur enlisted under him. Meanwhile, Aurangzeb anxiously observed the rise of Maratha power near the Mughal frontiers. He instructed the new Mughal governor of the Deccan, Shaista Khan, who was related to Aurangzeb by marriage, to invade Shivaji’s dominions.

        Initially, the war went badly for Shivaji. Shaista Khan occupied Poona in 1660 and made it his headquarters. He then dispatched detachments to wrest control of the Konkan from Shivaji. Despite harassment from Shivaji’s forces and the bravery of Maratha defenders, the Mughals secured control over North Konkan. Driven into a corner, Shivaji took a bold step. He infiltrated Shaista Khan’s camp in Poona and, under the cover of night, attacked the Khan in his harem in 1663, killing his son and one of his captains while wounding the Khan himself. This daring attack disgraced Shaista Khan, and Shivaji’s reputation soared once again. In anger, Aurangzeb transferred Shaista Khan to Bengal, refusing to grant him an audience before his departure, a customary practice at the time.

        Meanwhile, Shivaji launched another audacious move. In 1664, he attacked Surat, the premier Mughal port, looting it extensively and returning home laden with treasure.


    Treaty of Purandar and Shivaji’s Visit to Agra
        After the failure of Shaista Khan, Aurangzeb deputed Raja Jai Singh of Amber, one of his most trusted advisers, to deal with Shivaji. Full military and administrative authority was conferred upon Jai Singh so that he was not dependent on the Mughal viceroy in the Deccan and could deal directly with the emperor.

        Unlike his predecessors, Jai Singh did not underestimate the Marathas. He made careful diplomatic and military preparations, appealing to all of Shivaji’s rivals and opponents and even attempting to win over the Sultan of Bijapur to isolate Shivaji. Marching to Poona, Jai Singh decided to strike at the heart of Shivaji’s territories—Fort Purandar, where Shivaji had lodged his family and treasure. Jai Singh closely besieged Purandar (1665), beating off all Maratha attempts to relieve it. With the fall of the fort imminent and no relief likely from any quarter, Shivaji opened negotiations with Jai Singh. After intense bargaining, the following terms were agreed upon:
            1.	Out of 35 forts held by Shivaji, 23 forts, along with surrounding territory yielding a revenue of four lakh huns annually, were to be surrendered to the Mughals, while the remaining 12 forts, with an annual income of one lakh huns, were to be left under Shivaji’s control on the condition of his service and loyalty to the Mughal throne.
            2.	Territory worth four lakh huns per year in the Bijapuri Konkan, which Shivaji already controlled, was granted to him. Additionally, Bijapur territory worth five lakh huns per year in the uplands (Balaghat), which Shivaji was to conquer, was also granted to him. In return, he was to pay 40 lakh huns in installments to the Mughals.

        Shivaji requested to be excused from personal service. Instead, a mansab of 5000 was granted to his minor son, Sambhaji. However, Shivaji promised to personally join any Mughal campaign in the Deccan.

        Jai Singh cleverly created a rift between Shivaji and the ruler of Bijapur. However, the success of this scheme depended on Mughal support for Shivaji in recovering from Bijapur the land he had surrendered to the Mughals. This proved to be the fatal flaw. Aurangzeb remained suspicious of Shivaji and doubted the wisdom of a joint Mughal-Maratha attack on Bijapur. In contrast, Jai Singh had grander ambitions—he saw the alliance with Shivaji as the first step in the conquest of Bijapur and the entire Deccan. Once that was accomplished, he believed Shivaji would have no choice but to remain an ally of the Mughals, writing to Aurangzeb, “We shall hem Shivaji in like the center of a circle.”

        However, the Mughal-Maratha expedition against Bijapur failed. Shivaji, who had been deputed to capture Fort Panhala, was also unsuccessful. Seeing his grand scheme collapse, Jai Singh persuaded Shivaji to visit the emperor at Agra. He hoped that if Shivaji and Aurangzeb could reconcile, the emperor might allocate greater resources for a renewed invasion of Bijapur. But the visit proved to be a disaster. Shivaji felt insulted when he was ranked among mansabdars of 3000—a rank previously granted to his minor son. Moreover, during the emperor’s birthday celebrations, Aurangzeb did not even grant Shivaji an audience. Angered by this treatment, Shivaji stormed out and refused imperial service. Such an incident had never occurred before, and a strong faction at court urged Aurangzeb to punish Shivaji severely to uphold imperial dignity.

        Since Shivaji had come to Agra on Jai Singh’s assurance, Aurangzeb sought his advice. Jai Singh strongly advocated for leniency. However, before a decision could be made, Shivaji dramatically escaped from detention in 1666. The manner of his escape is well known and need not be repeated here. Aurangzeb always blamed himself for his carelessness in allowing Shivaji to escape. There is little doubt that Shivaji’s visit to Agra was a turning point in Mughal-Maratha relations. Though Shivaji remained inactive for two years after returning home, the visit demonstrated that, unlike Jai Singh, Aurangzeb placed little value on an alliance with Shivaji. To him, Shivaji was merely a petty zamindar (landholder). As subsequent events proved, Aurangzeb’s failure to recognize Shivaji’s importance and his refusal to forge a strong alliance with him were among the greatest political mistakes of his reign.

    FINAL BREACH WITH SHIVAJI—SHIVAJI’S ADMINISTRATION AND ACHIEVEMENTS
        Aurangzeb virtually goaded Shivaji into resuming his career of conquest by insisting upon a narrow interpretation of the Treaty of Purandar, even though, with the failure of the expedition against Bijapur, the bottom had dropped out of the treaty. Shivaji could not reconcile himself to the loss of 23 forts and territory worth four lakh hons a year to the Mughals without any compensation from Bijapur. He renewed his contest with the Mughals, sacking Surat a second time in 1670. Over the next four years, he recovered a large number of his forts, including Purandar, from the Mughals and made deep inroads into Mughal territories, especially Berar and Khandesh. The Mughal preoccupation with the Afghan uprising in the northwest helped Shivaji. He also renewed his contest with Bijapur, securing Panhala and Satara through bribes and raiding the Kanara region at leisure.

        In 1674, Shivaji formally crowned himself at Raigarh. He had come a long way from being a petty jagirdar at Poona. By now, he was the most powerful among the Maratha chiefs, and by virtue of the extent of his dominions and the size of his army, he could claim a status equal to that of the weakened Deccani sultans. The formal coronation had several purposes. It placed him on a pedestal much higher than any of the Maratha chiefs, some of whom had continued to see him as an upstart. To strengthen his social position further, Shivaji married into some of the leading old Maratha families—the Mohites, the Shirkes, etc. A formal declaration was also made by the presiding priest, Gaga Bhatta, that Shivaji was a high-class Kshatriya. Finally, as an independent ruler, it now became possible for Shivaji to enter into treaties with the Deccani sultans on a footing of equality and not as a rebel. This was also an important step in the further growth of Maratha national sentiment.

        In 1676, Shivaji undertook a bold new venture. With the active aid and support of the brothers Madanna and Akhanna at Hyderabad, he launched an expedition into the Bijapuri Karnataka. Shivaji was given a grand welcome by the Qutb Shah at his capital, and a formal agreement was reached. The Qutb Shah agreed to pay a subsidy of one lakh hons (five lakh rupees) annually to Shivaji, and a Maratha ambassador was to reside at his court. The territory and the booty gained in Karnataka were to be shared. The Qutb Shah supplied a contingent of troops and artillery to aid Shivaji and also provided money for his army’s expenses. The treaty was highly favorable to Shivaji and enabled him to capture Jinji and Vellore from Bijapuri officials and conquer much of the territories held by his half-brother, Ekoji. Although Shivaji had assumed the title of Haindava-Dharmoddharak (Protector of the Hindu Faith), he plundered the Hindu population of the area mercilessly. Returning home laden with treasure, Shivaji refused to share anything with the Qutb Shah, thus straining their relations.

        The Karnataka expedition was the last major campaign of Shivaji. The base at Jinji, built by him, later proved to be a haven of refuge for his son, Rajaram, during Aurangzeb’s all-out war on the Marathas.

        Shivaji died in 1680, shortly after his return from the Karnataka expedition. Meanwhile, he had laid the foundations of a sound administrative system. His system of administration was largely borrowed from the practices of the Deccani states. Although he designated eight ministers, sometimes called the Ashtapradhan, it was not in the nature of a council of ministers, as each minister was directly responsible to the ruler. The most important ministers were the Peshwa, who looked after finances and general administration, and the Sar-i-Naubat (Senapati), which was a post of honor generally given to one of the leading Maratha chiefs. The Majumdar was the accountant, while the Waqia Navis was responsible for intelligence, postal matters, and household affairs. The Surunavis or Chitnis assisted the king with correspondence. The Dabir was the master of ceremonies and also assisted the king in dealings with foreign powers. The Nyayadhish and Panditrao were in charge of justice and charitable grants.

        More important than the appointment of these officials was Shivaji’s organization of the army and revenue system. He preferred to give cash salaries to regular soldiers, though sometimes the chiefs received revenue grants (saranjam). Strict discipline was maintained in the army; women and dancing girls were not allowed to accompany the soldiers. The plunder taken by each soldier during campaigns was strictly accounted for. The regular army (paga), consisting of about 30,000 to 40,000 cavalry, as distinct from the loosely organized auxiliaries (silahdars), was supervised by Havaldars, who received fixed salaries. The forts were carefully supervised, with Mavali foot soldiers and gunners appointed to guard them. We are told that three men of equal rank were placed in charge of each fort to guard against treachery.

        Shivaji’s revenue system appears to have been patterned after Malik Ambar’s system. A new revenue assessment was completed by Annaji Datto in 1679. It is incorrect to think that Shivaji abolished the zamindari (deshmukhi) system or that he did not award jagirs (mokasa) to his officials. However, he strictly supervised the mirasdars, those with hereditary rights in land. Describing the situation, Sabhasad, who wrote in the eighteenth century, states that these sections paid the government only a small part of their collections. “In consequence, the mirasdars grew and strengthened themselves by building bastions, castles, and strongholds in the villages, enlisting footmen and musketeers… This class had become unruly and seized the country.” Shivaji destroyed their bastions and forced them to submit.

        Shivaji supplemented his income by levying a contribution on neighboring Mughal territories. This contribution, which amounted to one-fourth of the land revenue, came to be called chauth (one-fourth).

        Shivaji was not only an able general, a skillful tactician, and a shrewd diplomat, but he also laid the foundation of a strong state by curbing the power of the deshmukhs. His army was an effective instrument of his policies, with rapid movement being its most important feature. The army depended for its salaries to a considerable extent on plunder from neighboring areas. However, the state cannot be called merely a “war-state.” While it was regional in character, it had a definite popular base. To that extent, Shivaji was a popular king who represented the assertion of local will against Mughal encroachments.

    AURANGZEB AND THE DECCAN STATES (1658-87)
        The relations between Aurangzeb and the Deccan states can be divided into three phases.
            •	The first phase lasted until 1668, during which the main objective was to recover from Bijapur the territories of the Ahmadnagar state, which had been surrendered to it by the treaty of 1636.
            •	The second phase lasted until 1684, during which the primary threat in the Deccan was the Marathas. Efforts were made to pressure Bijapur and Golconda into aligning with the Mughals against Shivaji and later against his son, Sambhaji. During this period, the Mughals gradually encroached upon the territories of the Deccan states while simultaneously attempting to bring them under complete Mughal domination.
            •	The final phase began when Aurangzeb abandoned hopes of securing the cooperation of Bijapur and Golconda against the Marathas. Convinced that the destruction of the Marathas required the conquest of Bijapur and Golconda first, he decided to subjugate these states.

        The treaty of 1636, under which Shah Jahan had ceded one-third of the Ahmadnagar territories as a bribe to dissuade Bijapur and Golconda from supporting the Marathas—along with a promise that the Mughals would “never ever” conquer these states—was later disregarded by Shah Jahan himself. In 1657-58, both Golconda and Bijapur faced the threat of Mughal annexation. Golconda was forced to pay a hefty indemnity, while Bijapur had to surrender the Nizam Shahi territories granted to it in 1636.

        The justification for these actions was that both states had made extensive conquests in Karnataka and that the Mughals were entitled to “compensation” since these states were regarded as Mughal vassals. The argument was that their conquests had been possible only due to the Mughals’ benevolent neutrality. However, the real reason was financial: the cost of maintaining Mughal armies in the Deccan was high, and the revenues from the territories under Mughal control were insufficient to cover these expenses. For a long time, the shortfall was met through subsidies from the treasuries of Malwa and Gujarat.

        The revival of a policy of limited expansion in the Deccan had far-reaching consequences, which neither Shah Jahan nor Aurangzeb seemed to fully appreciate. It permanently destroyed trust in Mughal treaties and assurances, making a “union of hearts” against the Marathas impossible. Aurangzeb pursued this policy with great perseverance for a quarter of a century, but with little success.

        THE FIRST PHASE (1658-68)
            Upon ascending the throne, Aurangzeb faced two major challenges in the Deccan:
                1.	The rising power of Shivaji.
                2.	Persuading Bijapur to relinquish the territories it had acquired under the treaty of 1636.

            Kalyani and Bidar had been secured in 1657, while Parenda was obtained through bribery in 1660. However, Sholapur still remained under Bijapur’s control. After his accession, Aurangzeb ordered Jai Singh to punish both Shivaji and the Adil Shah of Bijapur. This directive reflected Aurangzeb’s confidence in Mughal military superiority and his underestimation of his opponents. However, Jai Singh, an astute politician, warned Aurangzeb, “It would be unwise to attack both these foes at the same time.”

            Jai Singh was the only Mughal statesman of this period who advocated an aggressive forward policy in the Deccan. He believed that the Maratha problem could not be resolved without Mughal expansion in the region—a conclusion that Aurangzeb himself reached two decades later.

            While planning his invasion of Bijapur, Jai Singh wrote to Aurangzeb, “The conquest of Bijapur is the preface to the conquest of all Deccan and Karnataka.” However, Aurangzeb hesitated to commit to this bold strategy. The possible reasons for his reluctance included:
                •	The threat posed by the ruler of Iran in the northwest.
                •	The long and arduous nature of a Deccan campaign, which would require the emperor’s personal presence.
                •	The risk of leaving a large Mughal army under the command of an ambitious noble or prince, a lesson Shah Jahan had learned to his misfortune.
                •	The constraints imposed by Shah Jahan’s continued existence, which prevented Aurangzeb from embarking on a distant military campaign.

            With limited resources at his disposal, Jai Singh’s Bijapur campaign of 1665 was bound to fail. The campaign rekindled the united front of the Deccan states against the Mughals, as the Qutb Shah of Golconda sent a large force to aid Bijapur. The Deccan states employed guerrilla tactics, luring Jai Singh towards Bijapur while devastating the countryside, thereby depriving the Mughals of supplies.

            Jai Singh’s campaign suffered from logistical shortcomings: he lacked siege artillery, and a prolonged siege was impossible. His eventual retreat proved costly, yielding neither money nor additional territory. The failure of this campaign, coupled with Aurangzeb’s criticisms, hastened Jai Singh’s death in 1667. The following year (1668), the Mughals secured the surrender of Sholapur through bribery, marking the end of the first phase of Mughal expansion in the Deccan.

        THE SECOND PHASE (1668-84)
            Between 1668 and 1676, the Mughals largely remained inactive in the Deccan. A new political dynamic emerged with the rise of Madanna and Akhanna, two talented brothers who virtually controlled the administration of Golconda from 1672 until the state’s annexation in 1687. They sought to establish a tripartite alliance between Golconda, Bijapur, and Shivaji. However, factional conflicts within Bijapur and Shivaji’s growing ambitions frequently disrupted this alliance.

            The factions at Bijapur pursued inconsistent policies, aligning either with or against the Mughals based on their immediate interests. Meanwhile, Shivaji alternated between looting Bijapur and supporting it against the Mughals. Despite his concerns over the growing Maratha power, Aurangzeb appeared determined to limit Mughal territorial expansion in the Deccan. Consequently, repeated efforts were made to install a pro-Mughal faction in Bijapur that would oppose Shivaji and remain independent of Golconda’s influence.

            In pursuit of this objective, the Mughals undertook a series of military interventions, the details of which are of little significance. The net result of these efforts was the reassertion of the united front of the three Deccan powers against the Mughals.

            A final, desperate attempt was made by Diler Khan, the Mughal viceroy, in 1679-80 to capture Bijapur. However, the Mughal forces were once again outmatched by the combined strength of the Deccan states. A significant new factor in this conflict was the emergence of the Karnataki matchlock-armed foot soldiers. Thirty thousand of these troops, sent by the Berar chief Prem Naik, played a decisive role in repelling the Mughal siege of Bijapur. Additionally, Shivaji dispatched a large force to aid Bijapur and launched raids into Mughal territories.

            As a result, Diler Khan’s campaign failed to achieve its objectives, instead exposing Mughal territories to repeated Maratha incursions. Consequently, he was recalled by Aurangzeb.

        The Third Phase (1684-87)
            Thus, the Mughals achieved little during 1676-80. When Aurangzeb reached the Deccan in 1681 in pursuit of his rebel son, Prince Akbar, he initially concentrated his forces against Sambhaji, the son and successor of Shivaji, while making renewed efforts to detach Bijapur and Golconda from their alliance with the Marathas. However, his efforts did not yield results different from the earlier ones. The Marathas were the only shield against the Mughals, and the Deccani states were not prepared to discard it.

            Aurangzeb now decided to force the issue. He called upon the Adil Shah, as a vassal, to supply provisions to the imperial army, allow Mughal forces free passage through his territory, and provide a contingent of 5,000 to 6,000 cavalry for the war against the Marathas. He also demanded the expulsion of Sharza Khan, the leading Bijapuri noble opposed to the Mughals. An open rupture was now inevitable. The Adil Shah appealed for help from both Golconda and Sambhaji, which was promptly given. However, even the combined forces of the Deccani states could not withstand the full strength of the Mughal army, particularly when it was commanded by the emperor himself. Nevertheless, it took 18 months of siege, with Aurangzeb personally present during the final stages, before Bijapur fell in 1686. This provides ample justification for the earlier failures of Jai Singh (1665) and Diler Khan (1679-80) against Bijapur.

            A campaign against Golconda was inevitable following the downfall of Bijapur. The “sins” of the Qutb Shah were too many to be pardoned. He had given supreme power to the infidels, Madanna and Akhanna, and had aided Shivaji on various occasions. His latest defiance of Aurangzeb’s warnings further sealed his fate. Earlier in 1685, despite stiff resistance, the Mughals had occupied Golconda. The emperor had agreed to pardon the Qutb Shah in return for a huge subsidy, the ceding of certain territories, and the dismissal of Madanna and Akhanna. The Qutb Shah complied, and Madanna and Akhanna were dragged into the streets and murdered in 1686. However, even this crime failed to save the Qutb Shah. The Mughals opened the siege of Golconda early in 1687, and after more than six months of campaigning, the fort fell due to treachery and bribery.

            Aurangzeb had triumphed, but he soon realized that the extinction of Bijapur and Golconda was only the beginning of his difficulties. The last and most challenging phase of his life had now begun.

    AURANGZEB, THE MARATHAS, AND THE DECCAN: THE LAST PHASE (1687-1707)
        After the downfall of Bijapur and Golconda, Aurangzeb was able to concentrate all his forces against the Marathas.

        In 1689, Sambhaji was captured at his secret hideout in Sangameshwar by a Mughal force. He was paraded before Aurangzeb and executed as a rebel and an infidel. This was undoubtedly another major political mistake on Aurangzeb’s part. He could have secured his conquests of Bijapur and Golconda by coming to terms with the Marathas. However, by executing Sambhaji, he not only threw away this opportunity but also provided the Marathas with a renewed cause. At the same time, in the absence of a single rallying point, the Maratha sardars were left free to plunder Mughal territories, disappearing at the approach of Mughal forces and regrouping later. Instead of destroying the Maratha state, Aurangzeb made Maratha resistance all-pervasive in the Deccan.

        Rajaram, Sambhaji’s younger brother, was crowned as king but had to flee when the Mughals attacked his capital. He sought refuge at Jinji on the east coast and continued the fight against the Mughals from there. Thus, Maratha resistance expanded from the western to the eastern coast.

        However, at this moment, Aurangzeb was at the height of his power, having triumphed over all his enemies. Some nobles believed that Aurangzeb should return to North India, leaving the task of mopping up the remaining Maratha forces to others. Earlier, there was an opinion—apparently supported by the heir-apparent, Shah Alam—that the administration of Karnataka should be entrusted to the vassal rulers of Bijapur and Golconda. Aurangzeb rejected all such suggestions and imprisoned Shah Alam for daring to negotiate with the Deccani rulers.

        Convinced that Maratha power had been crushed after 1690, Aurangzeb concentrated on annexing the rich and extensive Karnataka region into the empire. However, he overextended his lines of communication, making them vulnerable to Maratha attacks. As a result, he failed to establish a stable administration in Bijapur, which had become the hub of Maratha activities.

        Between 1690 and 1703, Aurangzeb stubbornly refused to negotiate with the Marathas. Rajaram was besieged at Jinji, but the siege proved to be long and drawn-out. Jinji finally fell in 1698, but Rajaram managed to escape. Maratha resistance intensified, and the Mughals suffered several serious defeats. The Marathas recaptured many of their forts, and Rajaram was able to return to Satara.

        Undeterred, Aurangzeb set out to recover all the Maratha forts. For five and a half years, from 1700 to 1705, he dragged his weary and ailing body from one siege to another. Floods, disease, and attacks by Maratha guerrilla bands took a heavy toll on the Mughal army. Weariness and disaffection steadily grew among the nobles and soldiers. Demoralization set in, and many jagirdars made secret pacts with the Marathas, agreeing to pay chauth in exchange for not being disturbed in their jagirs.

        In 1703, Aurangzeb finally opened negotiations with the Marathas. He was willing to release Shahu, the son of Sambhaji, who had been captured at Satara along with his mother. Shahu had been treated well—he had been given the title of Raja and a mansab of 2000/2000. Upon coming of age, he was married to two Maratha girls from respectable families. Aurangzeb was prepared to grant Shahu Shivaji’s swarajya and the right of sardeshmukhi over the Deccan, thus recognizing his special position. Over 70 Maratha sardars assembled to receive Shahu. However, at the last moment, Aurangzeb canceled the agreement, uncertain about the Marathas’ intentions.

        By 1706, Aurangzeb realized the futility of his efforts to capture all the Maratha forts. Slowly, he retreated to Aurangabad, while an exultant Maratha army hovered around and attacked the stragglers.

        Thus, when Aurangzeb breathed his last in Aurangabad in 1707, he left behind an empire in turmoil, where all the internal problems were coming to a head.

    DECLINE OF THE MUGHAL EMPIRE—RESPONSIBILITY OF AURANGZEB
        The Mughal Empire declined rapidly after the death of Aurangzeb. The Mughal court became a battleground for factional conflicts among nobles, and soon, ambitious provincial governors began to assert independence. Maratha incursions extended from the Deccan to the heartland of the empire, including the Gangetic plains. The empire’s weakness was exposed to the world when Nadir Shah imprisoned the Mughal emperor and looted Delhi in 1739.

        It has been suggested that Aurangzeb offered the kingdom to Shahu on the condition that he convert to Islam. However, contemporary records do not support this claim. If Aurangzeb had intended to convert Shahu, he could have done so during the 13 years of his captivity. Generally, a Hindu prince who converted to Islam lost his right to his kingdom.

        To what extent was the downfall of the Mughal Empire due to developments after Aurangzeb’s death, and to what extent was it the result of his own flawed policies? This question has been widely debated among historians. While Aurangzeb cannot be absolved of responsibility, recent trends in historical analysis consider his reign within the broader economic, social, administrative, and intellectual context of the time, as well as emerging international trends before and during his rule.

        The economic and social forces at play in medieval India are yet to be fully understood. As discussed in an earlier chapter, trade and commerce were expanding in India during the seventeenth century, and handicraft production kept pace with rising demand. This expansion was only possible if raw material production—such as cotton and indigo—also increased. Official Mughal records indicate that the area under zabti (land where revenue assessment was based on measurement) expanded, and there is some evidence that total cultivated land also grew. This growth was not only due to population increase and economic forces but also, in part, to Mughal administrative policies. Every noble, including religious grantees, was expected to take a personal interest in the expansion and improvement of cultivation, and meticulous records were maintained. Historians are often surprised at the detailed data available on ploughs, bullocks, wells, the number of cultivators, and land under cultivation.

        Despite this, trade, manufacturing, and agricultural production did not expand as rapidly as required. Several factors contributed to this stagnation. No new agricultural techniques emerged to counter declining soil fertility. The land revenue system was burdensome—under Akbar, the standard state share was one-third of the average produce (excluding the share of zamindars and local elements), but from the mid-seventeenth century, the state’s share generally rose to half, including the zamindars’ portion. The revenue burden varied by region; it was lower in infertile areas like Rajasthan and Sindh but higher in fertile regions such as Kashmir’s saffron-producing lands. However, the revenue demands were generally not so excessive as to drive peasants away from agriculture.

        Data from eastern Rajasthan suggests that new villages were continuously founded during the late seventeenth and early eighteenth centuries (though comparable data is lacking for earlier periods). The limited expansion of cultivation appears to have been due to social and administrative factors. With an estimated population of 125 million, surplus cultivable land was available, yet landless laborers were present in many villages. Most of them belonged to marginalized communities, such as Dalits. The dominant cultivating castes and zamindars—often from upper and middle castes—had little incentive to allow Dalits to settle new villages and acquire land ownership. Instead, they preferred to keep them as a reserve labor force for menial tasks like skinning dead animals and making leather ropes. The rural landless and poor, with small landholdings, lacked the capital and organization needed to settle new villages or reclaim wasteland independently. While the state occasionally initiated land reclamation, it had to rely on local zamindars and village headmen (mugaddams), who had vested interests in maintaining the status quo.

        As agricultural production grew slowly, the demands and expectations of the ruling classes expanded rapidly. The number of mansabdars increased from 2,069 during Jahangir’s accession in 1605 to 8,000 in 1637 under Shah Jahan, and further to 11,456 in Aurangzeb’s later years. Despite this fivefold rise in the nobility, the empire’s revenue increased only marginally. During Jahangir and Shah Jahan’s reigns, territorial expansion was minimal. Shah Jahan also introduced an “age of magnificence,” leading to extravagant court expenditures. Although some nobles engaged in trade through merchants, their primary income source remained land revenue. Rising prices in the first half of the seventeenth century led nobles to squeeze more revenue from peasants and zamindars.

        Little is known about the exact number of zamindars and their standard of living. Mughal policies towards them were contradictory—while they were seen as threats to imperial stability, they were also integrated into administration through mansabs and political offices, particularly Rajputs, Marathas, and others. However, as zamindars gained power, they resisted the excessive demands of nobles. With surplus cultivable land available, peasants—called pahis or uparis—migrated between villages in search of better conditions.

        Efforts to extract more revenue from jagirs, often through unsanctioned methods, exacerbated tensions in rural society. This led to peasant unrest, zamindar uprisings, and attempts to establish independent kingdoms. Administratively, it caused discontent among the nobility and intensified the jagirdari crisis. Many nobles found their jagirs failing to generate expected revenues, leading to difficulties in maintaining the required number of troops. The situation was particularly dire in the Deccan, where disturbances and weak noble contingents led some mansabdars to enter private agreements with Maratha leaders, offering chauth (a quarter of their income) in exchange for non-interference.

        Another problem was the shortage of jagirs. After annexing Bijapur and Golconda, Aurangzeb kept the most productive jagirs under khalisa (direct imperial administration) to finance his wars. Meanwhile, jagirs in newly conquered regions, such as Karnataka, yielded little income due to instability, making them undesirable. Corruption among clerks and officials further worsened the situation, as nobles had to pay bribes to secure favorable jagirs.

        To address the jagir shortage, Shah Jahan had reduced the required number of cavalry a mansabdar had to maintain, but the problem persisted, becoming acute under Aurangzeb. The conquest of the Deccan failed to resolve this, as Aurangzeb had to accommodate officials of former Deccan sultanates and the influential Marathas. In an attempt to conserve resources, Aurangzeb imposed a near-ban on new recruitments, causing frustration among the sons and relatives of existing nobles. Historian Khafi Khan described the situation as “one pomegranate among a hundred sick”—a reference to the intense competition for limited jagirs.

        The Mughal nobility was a key institution, attracting competent individuals from various backgrounds, both native and foreign. It operated within a system that emphasized accessibility and governance, ensuring peace and security. Some historians argue that the nobility decayed because immigration from Central Asia declined after Aurangzeb’s reign. However, by his time, most nobles were already Indian-born. The claim that India’s climate led to character decline is a racialist argument later propagated by British historians to justify colonial rule.

        It has also been suggested that the Mughal nobility lacked a national character due to its diverse ethnic and religious composition. However, nationalism in its modern sense did not exist in medieval times. Instead, a strong loyalty to the Mughal dynasty and Indo-Mughal cultural values bound the nobility together.

        The Mughal administration relied on a system of checks and balances to manage the ambitions of individual nobles. However, as Aurangzeb’s successors allowed the system to decay, factionalism grew. The worsening jagirdari crisis further weakened the administrative framework, accelerating the empire’s decline. The Mughal system was highly centralized, requiring a strong monarch to function effectively. In the absence of such leadership, wazirs attempted to fill the void but ultimately failed.

        It has been argued that by the time Aurangzeb ascended the throne, the Mughal army had become outdated due to the neglect of the infantry armed with flint guns and mobile field artillery. Such forces might have enabled Aurangzeb to deal more effectively with Maratha-held forts in the Deccan. However, such a development required larger resources and would have been resisted by the nobles, as the decline of cavalry would have affected their prestige.

        In the political field, Aurangzeb committed several serious mistakes. We have already referred to his inability to understand the true nature of the Maratha movement and his disregard for Jai Singh’s advice to befriend Shivaji. The execution of Sambhaji was another mistake, as it deprived Aurangzeb of a recognized Maratha leader with whom he could negotiate. Apparently, Aurangzeb had no desire to negotiate with the Marathas. He was convinced that after the extinction of Bijapur and Golconda, the Marathas were at his mercy and had no option but to accept his terms—a truncated swarajya (the term used by Maratha writers for the state carved out by Shivaji) and a promise of loyalty and service to the Mughal emperor. When Aurangzeb realized his mistake and opened negotiations with the Marathas, the demand for chauth and sardeshmukhi proved to be a serious obstacle. Even this was largely overcome. By 1703, an agreement had been nearly reached, but Aurangzeb could not bring himself to trust Shahu and the Maratha sardars.

        Aurangzeb failed to resolve the Maratha problem, leaving an open sore. He did grant mansabs to many Maratha sardars; in fact, they held more mansabs at the highest levels than the Rajputs ever did. Yet, the Maratha sardars were not trusted. Unlike the Rajputs, they were never given offices of trust and responsibility. As a result, the Marathas could not be integrated into the Mughal political system. Here again, a political settlement with Shivaji, Sambhaji, or Shahu might have made a significant difference.

        Aurangzeb has been criticized for failing to unite with the Deccani states against the Marathas or for conquering them, thereby making the empire “so large that it collapsed under its own weight.” A unity of hearts between Aurangzeb and the Deccani states was “a psychological impossibility” once the treaty of 1636 was abandoned, a development that occurred during Shah Jahan’s reign. After his accession, Aurangzeb refrained from pursuing a vigorous forward policy in the Deccan. In fact, he postponed the decision to conquer and annex the Deccani states for as long as possible.

        Aurangzeb’s hand was virtually forced by the growing Maratha power, the support extended to Shivaji by Madanna and Akhanna from Golconda, and his fear that Bijapur might fall under the domination of Shivaji and the Maratha-dominated Golconda. Later, by giving shelter to the rebel prince Akbar, Sambhaji directly challenged Aurangzeb, who quickly realized that the Marathas could not be dealt with without first subduing Bijapur and possibly Golconda. The attempt to extend Mughal administration over Golconda, Bijapur, and Karnataka stretched the Mughal administration to a breaking point. It also exposed Mughal lines of communication to Maratha attacks to such an extent that Mughal nobles in the region found it impossible to collect their dues from assigned jagirs and sometimes made private pacts with the Marathas, as noted earlier. This, in turn, strengthened the Marathas, led to demoralization among the nobility, and caused a setback to imperial prestige. Perhaps Aurangzeb would have been better advised to accept the suggestion put forward by his eldest son, Shah Alam, for a settlement with Bijapur and Golconda—annexing only a part of their territories and allowing them to rule over Karnataka, which was far away and difficult to manage.

        The impact of the Deccani and other wars on the Mughal Empire and Aurangzeb’s prolonged absence from northern India should not be overestimated. Despite his policy mistakes and certain personal shortcomings—such as excessive suspicion and a narrow, cold temperament—the Mughal Empire remained a powerful and vigorous military and administrative machinery. The Mughal army might have struggled against the elusive and highly mobile Maratha bands in the mountainous Deccan, and Maratha forts might have been difficult to capture and even harder to retain, but in the plains of northern India and the vast plateau extending up to Karnataka, the Mughal artillery remained dominant. Even thirty or forty years after Aurangzeb’s death, when the Mughal artillery had declined considerably in strength and efficiency, the Marathas still could not face it in open battle. Continuous anarchy, wars, and Maratha depredations may have depleted the population of the Deccan and brought trade, industry, and agriculture to a virtual standstill, but in northern India, which was the heart of the empire and of decisive economic and political importance, the Mughal administration retained much of its vigor. Trade and industry not only continued to flourish but even expanded. The administration at the district level proved remarkably resilient, and much of it survived and found its way into British administration.

        Politically, despite Aurangzeb’s military reverses and mistakes, the Mughal dynasty still retained a powerful hold on the minds and imagination of the people.

        As for the Rajputs, we have seen that the breach with Marwar was not due to an attempt by Aurangzeb to undermine the Hindus by depriving them of a recognized leader but rather to a miscalculation on his part. He sought to divide the Marwar state between two principal claimants but, in the process, alienated both—along with the ruler of Mewar, who considered Mughal interference in such matters a dangerous precedent. The breach with Mewar and the prolonged war that followed damaged the moral standing of the Mughal state. However, militarily, the fighting after 1684 was not of great consequence. It is doubtful whether the presence of more Rathor Rajputs in the Deccan between 1681 and 1706 would have significantly altered the outcome of the conflict with the Marathas. The Rajputs primarily demanded high mansabs, as before, and the restoration of their homelands. These demands were met within half a dozen years of Aurangzeb’s death, after which the Rajputs ceased to be a major issue for the Mughals. They played little active role in the subsequent disintegration of the empire and did not help arrest its decline.

        Aurangzeb’s religious policy should be understood in its social, economic, and political context. He was orthodox in his outlook and sought to remain broadly within the framework of Islamic law. However, this law had developed outside India under vastly different circumstances and could not be rigidly applied to India. His failure to respect the sentiments of his non-Muslim subjects on many occasions, his policy of destroying many long-standing temples, and the reimposition of jizya (as prescribed by Islamic law) did not help him rally the Muslims to his side or foster greater loyalty to a state based on Islamic law. On the contrary, it alienated segments of the Hindu population and strengthened those opposed to the Mughal Empire for political or other reasons. However, religion itself was not the main issue. Jizya was abolished within half a dozen years of Aurangzeb’s death, and restrictions on building new temples were eased. But these measures had no effect on the rapid decline and disintegration of the empire in the 18th century.

        Ultimately, the decline and downfall of the Mughal Empire resulted from economic, social, political, and institutional factors. The Mughal ruling class’s neglect of modern science and technology was also a significant factor. While Akbar’s measures temporarily kept the forces of disintegration in check, it was impossible for him to implement fundamental changes in the structure of society. By the time Aurangzeb ascended the throne, the socio-economic forces of disintegration were already strong. Aurangzeb lacked the foresight and statesmanship necessary to implement fundamental reforms or pursue policies that could, at least temporarily, reconcile the various competing elements.

        Thus, Aurangzeb was both a victim of circumstances and a contributor to the very circumstances that led to his downfall.


Assessment and Review
    The thousand years from the beginning of the eighth century to the end of the seventeenth century saw significant changes in the political, economic, and cultural life of the country, as well as, to a lesser extent, in its social life.

    In the field of social life, the caste system continued to dominate despite the challenge posed by Islam and the loss of political power by the Rajput rulers, who were duty-bound to protect dharma, which implied, among other things, the upholding of the four-fold division of society (varna-ashrama dharma). Although the Nath Panthis, Jogis, and the Bhakti saints vehemently criticized the caste system, they could hardly make a dent in it. Over time, a tacit agreement was reached. The criticism of the caste system by saints did not, with some notable exceptions, extend to day-to-day or secular life, while the Brahmins acquiesced in the advocacy of the path of devotion as a means of salvation for all castes, especially for the Shudras. Many women saints, such as Mira, and others such as Surdas, opened the path of Bhakti for women as well, allowing them to rise above their traditional roles of service and duty to a husband. However, the Brahmins continued to claim a privileged position for themselves, including the exclusive right to preach and educate.

    Within the framework of caste, new subgroups arose due in part to the absorption of tribal groups into Hinduism, the growth of new professional groups, and local and regional influences. At the same time, the varna status of castes rose or fell according to the economic and political power of the groups concerned. Rajputs, Marathas, and Khatris may be mentioned in this context.

    The Bhakti and Sufi saints gradually brought about a better understanding of the fundamental tenets of Hinduism and Islam, underlining the fact that they shared many similarities. This resulted in a greater spirit of mutual harmony and toleration, although forces advocating a narrow, intolerant approach remained strongly entrenched and sometimes influenced state policies. However, such occasions were, on the whole, limited.

    The Bhakti and Sufi saints also brought about important changes in the approach to religion, emphasizing true faith over formal observances. They also contributed to the growth of regional languages and literature.
