However, the excessive concern with religious and spiritual affairs resulted in a setback to the growth of rational sciences, particularly the cultivation of science and technology.

    On balance, the position of women worsened. The seclusion of women, or purdah, became more widespread, while Hindu women were unable to claim the right to remarriage or a share in their father’s property, which Muslim women had. In fact, these rights tended to be increasingly denied even to Muslim women.

    In the political and economic fields, the most important development was the political and administrative integration of the country, brought about by the Turks and later consolidated by the Mughals. Although the Turkish and Mughal systems of administration remained largely confined to northern India, they indirectly affected other parts of India as well. The institution of a well-minted silver-based currency, the development of roads and sarais, and the preference for city life had a direct effect on the growth of trade and handicrafts, which reached its peak during the seventeenth century. Under the Mughals, political integration was accompanied by a deliberate effort to create a unified ruling class consisting of both Muslims and Hindus. However, the ruling class remained strongly aristocratic in character, with only limited opportunities for talented individuals from lower classes. It also remained largely northern in character. Aurangzeb did attempt to induct large numbers of Marathas and Deccani nobles into the administration. While the Deccanis were integrated, the Marathas were not. Perhaps regional, religious, and social prejudices played a role in this, as unlike the Rajputs in north India, the Maratha sardars came from social sections that had never exercised political power or been a ruling class. Nor were they considered Kshatriyas.

    The Mughal nobility was organized as a bureaucracy dependent on the monarch. However, it derived its income mainly from lands cultivated by peasant proprietors. For the collection of land revenue from peasants, the nobility depended partly on its military following and partly on the strength of the zamindars, whose rights and privileges were defended and maintained by the state in return for their support. This is why many historians argue that the state in medieval India remained essentially feudal.

    A significant contribution of the Turks was the defense of the country from Mongol onslaughts during the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries. Later, for 200 years, the Mughals secured the northwest frontiers of India from foreign invasions. For this purpose, the defense of India was fixed on the Kabul-Ghazni line with the Hindukush mountains to the north. The politics of Central and West Asia were closely followed, and sometimes active participation was taken.

    India’s reputation as a land of spices and its position as the textile manufactory of the eastern world, including East Africa, led European nations to establish direct trade relations with India. The richness of the oriental trade further whetted the appetite of European nations and accelerated their economic and technological growth. Since they had little to offer in demand in the oriental world except for silver and gold procured from Central and South America, European traders, backed by their governments, sought entry into the internal trade of India and Asia. On several occasions, they desired to control Indian territories whose income could be used to purchase Indian goods, much like the Dutch, who had brought the East Indies (modern Indonesia) under their control. As long as the Mughal empire was strong, the European nations were not successful in this objective. However, the decline of the Mughal empire and significant political events in India during the eighteenth century, such as the invasions of Nadir Shah and later the Afghans, along with the rapid economic development of European nations, enabled them to establish their domination in India as well as in many other Asian countries.

    Scholars have attempted to explain the causes of the decline and downfall of the Mughal empire. However, the reasons why India, like many other Asian nations, could not develop as rapidly as European nations in the economic and scientific fields require further detailed study and research. The Mughal ruling classes had no traditions of connection with the sea. While the Mughal rulers recognized the importance of foreign trade and supported European trading companies for this reason, they had little understanding of the importance of naval power in a nation’s economic development.

    India’s lagging behind in naval power was part of its growing backwardness in science and technology. Even the mechanical clock, which integrated many European inventions in the field of dynamics, was not produced in India during the seventeenth century. The superiority of Europeans in artillery was freely acknowledged. Even where Indian craftsmen were able to replicate European developments—for example, in shipbuilding—little innovation was displayed. Apart from the attitude of the ruling class, the social structure, historical traditions, and outlook of various sections were significant factors. There was excessive emphasis on past learning and deference to those considered repositories of knowledge—the Brahmins and the mullahs. Akbar’s efforts to modernize the syllabus by introducing more science subjects of secular interest were defeated due to the pressure of these elements. The very skill of Indian artisans and their availability in large numbers inhibited the development and application of machine power to productive enterprises. The caste system's effect in fostering insularity and conservatism remains a subject of discussion.

    Thus, India lagged behind in science and technology, and the Mughal ruling class remained largely oblivious to these developments. Like all ruling classes on the decline, they were more concerned with immediate matters, including their own comfort, rather than the future.

    Despite this, the period saw remarkable developments. Political integration was paralleled by cultural integration. Indian society developed a largely unified culture despite differences in race, religion, and language. This was reflected in an outburst of creative activity, making the seventeenth century a second classical age. However, this integrated culture came under pressure from religious dogmatists and competing interests within the ruling classes. Yet, it survived, on the whole, until the mid-nineteenth century—a tribute to the saints, scholars, and enlightened rulers who helped build it.

    Despite this, the developments in various fields in India during the period should not be overlooked. The growth of political integration was paralleled by cultural integration. Indian society was one of the few in the world that was able to develop a more or less unified culture despite differences in race, religion, and language. This unified culture was reflected in an outburst of creative activity, making the seventeenth century a second classical age.

    In the south, the traditions of the Cholas were continued by the Vijayanagara kingdom. The Bahmani kingdom and its successor states also contributed to cultural developments in various fields. The rich cultural advancements in different regional kingdoms during the fifteenth century were, to some extent, integrated into the new cultural forms developed by the Mughals. However, this integrated culture came under pressure from religious dogmatists of both faiths, as well as from the competing and conflicting interests of various sections within the ruling classes. Nevertheless, the fact that it survived, on the whole, until the middle of the nineteenth century is a significant tribute to the saints, scholars, and enlightened rulers who helped to build it.

    The period was also marked by economic development and growth. Trade and manufacturing expanded, and there was an increase and improvement in cultivation as well. However, this growth was uneven across different regions and varied during different phases. Apart from the Ganga valley, where the Mughals spent a substantial portion of the empire’s revenue resources, the regions that developed rapidly during the seventeenth century were Gujarat, the Coromandel coast, and Bengal. Perhaps it is no coincidence that these regions have remained at the forefront of India’s economic development in the modern period, particularly in the post-independence era.

    Would India have continued to progress economically and even attained an industrial revolution of its own had the Mughal empire survived? While trade and manufacturing continued to expand during the eighteenth century despite the empire’s downfall, India remained backward compared to Europe—not only in science and technology but in other fields as well. As a result, most manufacturing remained small in scale, with hardly any machinery, and workers relied on the simplest tools. Consequently, no matter how skilled a craftsman was, his productivity and efficiency remained low. Moreover, artisans could not develop into traders and entrepreneurs as they did in the West, both due to caste restrictions and because most artisans possessed little capital. This was a reflection of the highly uneven distribution of wealth, which limited the domestic market.

    During the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, the putting-out (dadni) system grew, increasing production but making artisans more and more dependent on merchants, whether Indian or foreign.

    It was under these circumstances that the British were able to conquer India and turn it into a colony—no longer a manufactory of the East, but a supplier of raw materials instead. It is this ebb and flow that makes the study of history both interesting and rewarding.
